#4159. What Happens When Sixth Graders and Elders Learn Together

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

In Saskatoon, Canada, a group of sixth graders spend their school year outside their normal classroom and at the Sherbrooke Community Centre, engaged in conversation and activities with the long-term care residents who live there. One day, they might be found journaling, another learning new technologies, or writing limericks with the “elders,” many of whom have dementia or other conditions. Or they might be gardening, helping out at the center’s gift shop, or working on an art project.  “From my work with elders, I learned stories, games, and how to become a better person,” said a student named Mya. It even relieved despair for one resident, Jodi, who said, “I went from a daily dread that I was still alive and breathing . . . to anticipat[ing] an awesome day that was full of life and joy.” Many extracurricular activities, sports, meetups, and other community programs bring people of similar ages together: children with children, teens with teens, adults with adults, seniors with seniors. But new research on an intergenerational program known as iGen suggests that these initiatives may be especially beneficial to our health and well-being. Finding meaning in connection For the study, the 25 participating students—as well as three elders and eight long-term care staff members—completed a variety of surveys. At the end of the day, they rated how meaningful their interactions with different groups of people were, how much fun and positive and negative emotions they had, and the quality of their physical health that day. They also reported how many kind and helpful actions they performed, and how strong their social connections and sense of belonging were. Everyone completed surveys for 10 straight school days in late September and early October, and then again in June. The results suggested that people were experiencing meaningful interactions between generations, and highly meaningful interactions with their peers of similar ages. While people didn’t become happier, healthier, or more kind overall over the course of the program, the researchers did observe changes on a daily basis. On days when people had more meaningful interactions, they tended to feel better, more connected, healthier, and kinder. This was true for both interactions with people the same age (like students with students, or elders with elders) and intergenerational interactions—although the benefits seemed more pronounced between generations than with peers. This link appeared even stronger toward the end of the program than near the beginning. In June, having meaningful interactions through iGen seemed to have an even bigger impact on people’s well-being than it did in the fall. The researchers, led by Jason D. E. Proulx, suggest this might be because students had deepened their relationships with elders by that point, or they were savoring their last few weeks together. The length and depth of this program—spending whole days together over 10 months—may have contributed to how powerful it was, the researchers suggest. Although this research only reflects one program, with a majority of white participants, it offers promising evidence that programs like this can help young and old alike, and comprise a valuable part of students’ education. Other research finds that interacting across generations can improve mental health and cognitive function in older people, and a sense of purpose for all of us. “Programs like iGen may be one effective and scalable intervention to foster more inclusive, meaningfully connected, and happier communities,” write Proulx and his colleagues. Mya’s mother Tanya said that she “built long-lasting friendships with the residents . . . but also learned the value and appreciation of life.” “It took those sixth-grade kids to bring life back into focus for me,” said Jodi, who had thought her life was over and sometimes wanted it to be. “iGen saved me.”

#4149. Eight Fun Ways to Keep Your Summer Alive

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Backpack-and-pencil time is just about here, but it’s not too late to treat yourself to a healthy dose of summer joy.   There’s no need to feel bad about stretching that vacation feeling out as long as you can. These eight mini-vacations for the mind and spirit require no suitcases to pack (and unpack), no expensive gas tanks to fill, and no big credit card bills to pay. Not only are many of our favorite summery luxuries simple and inexpensive, research suggests they just might be good for us, too. Before you swap the flip-flops and tanks for boots and sweaters, take a moment to seize some feel-good fun. Catch a concert You don’t need expensive tickets to the hottest concerts of the summer to reap the happy rewards of listening to live music. “Collective effervescence”—the feeling of meaning and closeness with others that we experience in a positive group activity—is key to a concert’s feel-good benefits, researchers say. While listening to songs at home is fun, too, the “sacredness and connection” of collective effervescence is rooted in the togetherness of a crowd at a concert, and the uplift can continue for as long as a week after. Free and cheap local live music options abound in many areas, whether it’s an old-time swing band in the gazebo in the park, a vocalist on the patio at a nearby café, or a full orchestra playing to a grassy outdoor amphitheater. Bring a blanket, let the kids run if you’ve got any, and soak in that sweet collective effervescence. Set your sights on water—or even jump in A beach or lake vacation may not be in the plans this summer, but you can still treat yourself to a walk next to a stream or a paddle in a nearby pond. Research finds that people who regularly spend time in natural environments like parks, woods, and beaches tend to have better physical and psychological health, and water offers specific benefits to well-being. According to new research from 19 countries, water-centric “blue spaces” like rivers, lakes, and oceans bring us feelings of restoration, calm, and greater mental and emotional energy. Being near water is itself beneficial, but swimming or even wading boosts the restorative effects. My own summer favorites are regular evening walks around a small local lake and—if I can make it work—getting out on a stand-up paddle board at least once a summer. Happiness! Look up at the night sky Research on the benefits of nature has generally focused on daytime hours, but that’s beginning to change. Researchers have created a “night sky connected index” (NSCI) to measure the effects of outdoor time at night, especially looking up at the sky, and they found that people with a higher NSCI tend to have “greater mental well-being and happiness.” Looking at the night sky is also linked to feelings of awe, according to the same study, and experiencing awe can help reduce negative thinking and boost our happiness. August is Perseids meteor shower time: Every year, across the Northern Hemisphere, thousands of meteors may be visible as they streak across the night sky. As a child, my siblings and I would often sleep in the backyard in mid-August and happily count meteors while we lay on our backs in our sleeping bags. My backyard sleeping days are behind me, but I admit that lately adulthood has been so busy that I haven’t even looked up at night. Time to find the magic again. Enjoy a meal with others—outdoors No matter what outdoor activity you choose, getting outside in green space specifically (even parks and street greenery count!) is beneficial. But you can boost your outdoor fun—and health—even more by inviting others to share a meal together. Recent research finds that eating with others makes people feel more happiness and less stress, pain, and sadness that day compared to days when they only eat alone. You don’t have to host a dinner party or spring for an expensive restaurant meal. Greater Good reader Catherine Michaud shared her simple, satisfying summer recipe with us: “Find a local park with good shade trees. Bring a book, a blanket, a friend (or three), lawn chairs, picnic, and board/lawn games.” Count us in. Put up your feet while your kid climbs a tree Speaking of kids: As a kid of the 80s, I climbed countless trees with friends and siblings. But I am having trouble remembering a time when my own kids clambered up branches just to see how high they could go. A recent Australian paper reviewed many studies about adventurous play and “overwhelmingly” concluded that children engaging in risky outdoor play showed better overall mental health and social abilities and were less prone to anxiety. “Risky outdoor play” generally includes activities involving high speeds or heights, play with dangerous tools (saws, knives) or near dangerous elements (fire, water, cliffs), rough-and-tumble play, and activities in which the child could “disappear” out of adult sight. This list may make some of us (including me) a little nervous, but maybe we could at least let the kids help build, light, and tend the fire for some late summer s’mores? The authors of the study specifically caution against helicopter parenting; it’s key, they say, that the kids learn to evaluate risks themselves. This isn’t a permission slip for parents to neglect safety, but it is a reminder that this summer it’s OK for parents to turn down the worry-meter, skip the hovering, and instead sit on the patio with a book or a friend while the kids take in a joyful climb or a bike race down the driveway. Watch sports for the win This year’s World Cup excitement is over, but the sporting fun doesn’t need to end: People who watch sports, either live or on TV, tend to show a number of health benefits. A new study published in the journal Nature found that watching live sports reduces the stress hormone cortisol and increases the social-bonding hormone oxytocin. So, whether it’s an evening of Little League or the Big Leagues, and whether you’re in the middle of a cheering crowd or cozied up on the couch during a thunderstorm, take a break from the end-of-summer rush and relax into a game, win or lose.  Apple-pick your way to less stress Greater Good reader and mom Aja Russo loves taking her kids strawberry picking every year in early summer. But if you missed strawberry picking time, and you didn’t plant a garden this year (raising my own hand), it’s not too late. Start reaping the benefits of gardening at a pick-your-own farm. Research finds that even short periods of gardening can lead to feeling calmer and more relaxed. What to pick? Peach picking is still going strong in many areas, and apples aren’t just for autumn—popular varieties are ready for picking in August in much of the United States. If the climate where you live doesn’t cooperate with picking fruit right now, you can still exercise your green thumb and boost well-being by tending a plant indoors. Indulge in ice cream Yes, we said what we said. Most of us intuitively know that eating ice cream makes us feel good, but researchers were surprised when decades of observation found a lower rate of cardiovascular disease among diabetic people who ate a small amount (about half a cup) of ice cream a few times a week compared to diabetic people who never ate ice cream. Now, scientists caution that this doesn’t mean that ice cream is a health food, but it certainly suggests that we don’t need to heap a topping of guilt onto our occasional cool treats. There’s still plenty of summertime left for a sunset trip to the local ice cream parlor with friends or family. Yum! If you live in a place where the autumn chill will soon set in, now is prime time to revel in long evenings, soft temperatures, and as much summer well-being as you can seize. As the famous song goes, “Summertime, and the living is easy.” And healthy!

#4106. What Chimps Can Teach Us About Male Friendships

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Across the Western world, young men and boys face a changing economy, with fewer manual labor jobs and factory floors that used to give a swathe of working-class men purpose and value. This leaves a large portion of recent generations feeling left behind and forgotten—and vulnerable to exploitation. A profitable industry of influencers who misframe evolutionary science to justify their narratives have taken advantage of the addictive nature of social media to hook into this vacuum and light up phone screens every night with the same vitriolic messages: “Reclaim your manhood”; “You are being feminized”; “The world is making you soft.” But this narrative conflicts with the biological reality. Across species—from fungal networks trading nutrients underground to astronauts sharing the role of emptying the latrine on the International Space Station—every organism must strike some balance between competing and cooperating. Lessons from the animal kingdom, from my research and others, show that cooperation doesn’t diminish masculinity. Indeed, dominant primates use their power judiciously, and male friendship forms the foundation of cooperation in a variety of species, with affectionate touch, caring, and vulnerability playing crucial roles in male relationships. The simple picture painted for these boys is built on sand. Once we observe the reality of sex, behavior, and connection in the natural world around us, it washes away. Human males stand to improve their relationships, benefit from a broader base of emotional and social support, and enhance their well-being with these friendship lessons from our closest cousins in the animal kingdom. The role of care and connection One term at the core of the manosphere is the notion of alpha males. Life coaches and politicians push this construct without understanding the true function of hierarchy in the natural world. They believe that to get ahead you must take advantage of others, and that the weak get left behind. In reality, dominant male primates care for relationships and wield their power judiciously. It’s true that male chimpanzees form typically linear dominance relationships, where one submits to another, all the way to the top. They compete, and those competitions can spill into physical altercations. Higher-ranking males do tend to sire more offspring. But these broad truths hide the nuance of layered cooperation, care, nurturing touch, and love among male chimps. Day after day, most of that competitive pressure never turns into a fight at all. What holds chimpanzee communities together is less the male at the top and more the alliances underneath him. Males groom each other for hours at a time. They embrace after conflicts and kiss the arms and shoulders of their allies before potential competition, to resolve any tension. Before risky boundary patrols, when they might encounter rival groups, they even hold each other’s genitals—a sign of deep trust. Data spanning nearly three decades from Gombe show that males with strong ties to the alpha sired more offspring than those without, and so did males with wider networks of close allies, whether or not they ever rose in rank at all. Those who look out for each other and contribute to a strong community end up thriving. You can see the value of those alliances when a hierarchy is disrupted. At Taï National Park in Côte d’Ivoire, Anna Preis and her colleagues measured urinary cortisol in wild males across stable and unstable dominance periods. Stress rose in every male during the unstable stretches, regardless of rank. Aggression went the other way, rising when things were calm and the group faced less risk of destabilization. When the risk of escalation was greatest, males backed off. Stability has its own payoff; a group tearing itself apart can’t defend a territory. Indeed, even in its origins, the term “alpha” contained more nuance than broadly understood. The term rose out of research during the mid-20th century on captive wolves who were thrown together into crowded spaces without room to escape. Such an arrangement would produce a tense, despotic dynamic in any species, including our own. Once biologists began studying wild wolf packs, it became apparent that they are, well, families. They comprise a breeding pair and their grown offspring. L. David Mech, the biologist who popularized the alpha wolf in the first place, has spent more than two decades asking people to retire the term in order to accurately reflect natural communities. Despite its inaccuracy, the idea still perpetuates on podcasts and social media. Alpha-directed consolation from a whole group The role of vulnerability and comfort in the natural world provides another powerful lesson for humanity. At a wildlife sanctuary in Zambia, I’ve come to know a longstanding alpha male chimpanzee named Doug over many years. One day, during my first field season observing his group, he was separated from his community for routine husbandry purposes, alongside a slightly younger rival and the rival’s paternal ally. During this separation, the rival pair ambushed him, leaving him incredibly shaken. I had known Doug as a strong leader who never broke composure, and I felt shocked to see him cowering and screaming, nothing like the composed figurehead his group had respected. A low-ranking male, whom Doug had rarely paid attention to, approached first, offering himself in a gesture of submission. What followed drew in most of the roughly 40 other chimpanzees in the group. They embraced, touched, and kissed him; exchanged genital contact; placed their hands in his mouth; and invited him to mount them. This scale of collective reassurance directed toward a single individual is something I’ve never seen since, and is likely only sparked by moments of genuine crisis. The whole group responded to Doug’s distress with an outpouring of comfort and what to humans would appear to be love. The main aim of this project was to study consolation: how chimpanzees, and their sister species bonobos, comfort each other after fights. But we kept noticing similar intense, friendly contacts occurring in the absence of conflicts, often in the anticipation of competition. To better understand this, we conducted a feeding task with a five-minute window before the food arrived, filming 116 apes across five groups at two sanctuaries. We found that those who exchanged more reassuring contact beforehand went on to spend significantly more time feeding peacefully alongside others. In chimpanzees, males exhibited most of this behavior, but primarily in Doug’s more tolerant group. In a more despotic group where resources were less evenly distributed, the male-male relationships were markedly weaker. Simply put, friendship and reassuring behaviors among males helped these chimps peacefully put aside competing interests, which may lay the groundwork for collective harmony. Some of these behaviors require remarkable trust. Placing a finger, or even a whole hand, inside another male’s mouth or pressing your own mouth to someone’s body after they’ve just been attacked may be misinterpreted as having aggressive intent and could spark a new conflict. Chimpanzees have a reputation for amputating the extremities of others, and that, along with the size of their canines, makes these behaviors exceptionally risky during such turbulent times. Our hypothesis: That could be the point. You only offer that kind of vulnerability to someone you trust not to use it against you, and doing so signals something about the relationship that a show of strength can’t. Again, we noticed that these behaviors were particularly amplified in Doug’s group compared to the one with a less benevolent leader. Doug remains alpha to this day, and from our research, his tenureship seems to be thanks to the bonds he continues to invest in and the support he shows for his peers. He’s flawed and sometimes lashes out as any male chimpanzee might do, but throughout the seven years we’ve studied him, no challenges have managed to displace him. Doug looks out for others, and in turn they look out for him. Over my time studying many groups of chimpanzees, it seems the most tolerant social environments emerge from the leadership of strong, stable, and generally fair alphas who allow others access to resources and who keep tight bonds with their closest allies. The power of touch and care Humans can also learn from the way male chimpanzees can be tender in other contexts. At Taï National Park in Côte d’Ivoire, researchers recorded wild chimpanzees adopting orphaned infants, more than half of them taken in by males with no obvious kinship reason to bother. One adult male, Porthos, adopted an unrelated infant female named Gia and carried her on his back for years, sharing food with her long after any potential social debt to her deceased mother had lapsed. Later analysis found that what predicted whether a male took on that role was not the infant’s sex, and not relatedness, but the strength of the grooming bond he’d shared with the mother before she died. Touch carries power in humans, as well. Research across five European cultures found that the area of the body you’ll let someone touch maps almost directly onto the strength of your emotional bond with them. There is cultural variation. British people are notably more guarded about casual contact than many of their European neighbours, and touch between men in many Western contexts carries particular social weight. Touch conveys intimacy and trust. Yet holding someone’s hand or shoulder can also be used to intimidate or harass. We could learn from primates to stop withholding touch where it would help most and back down from reaching for it where it shouldn’t be used at all. One key area where more touch helps is sports. A study of all 30 NBA teams during the 2008–09 season found that the teams who touched more early in the season, through fist bumps, chest bumps, hugs, and high fives, went on to perform better and play more cooperatively later, even after controlling for player salaries and pre-season expectations. In women’s college basketball, players who received more physical contact from teammates after missing their first free throw were more likely to convert the second. After five straight shootout losses, England’s governing body in football, the FA, spent 18 months rebuilding England’s approach to penalties under Gareth Southgate, not just the technical side. Players were coached on how to treat each other after a miss. They learned to place an arm around the shoulder rather than leaving each other isolated. Celebrating wildly after a scored kick became a deliberate technique for steadying whoever was up next. Locker-room culture has never been shy about physical contact between teammates. Now we know that the contact might be doing real work. To be sure, humans show exceptional diversity in our cultures and behavior, for better and worse. But when humans are violent or cruel, people call it primal, or animalistic. We emphasize our altruism as unique to our species. That’s unfair to animals. Humans have bullied others until they took their own lives. We’ve wiped out entire tribes and cultures. And beyond targeting other people, even now, humans are removing protections for endangered keystone species that ecosystems depend upon to thrive. But we don’t need to accept the drive to dominate as inevitable. If the apes show anything, it’s how much of that drive gets managed and set aside day to day, and how much depends on the community you find yourself in. We are a part of the natural world like all other animals, needing to balance our cooperative and competitive urges. And the evidence suggests we sit at the better end of that range far more often than we might think. Where to start Now that we know how central touch and cooperation are to primates and human nature, I hope we can stop being pessimistic when we predict others’ behavior and motives. A study published in Science this June, covering 125 countries and over 100,000 people, found that 69% of us cooperate with a stranger when given the choice. Asked to guess, people put it at 47%. That pessimism showed up in 124 of the 125 countries surveyed. This matters in the grander scheme of things because those of us who expect others to cooperate are far more likely to cooperate also. When researchers simply told participants that most people worldwide see climate change as a threat, belief in others’ willingness to cooperate ticked up, and actual cooperation followed it, slightly, but in the right direction. Building on the lessons of primate relationships is easier than it seems. Seek simple interactions. One of the main ways our primate cousins build affiliative relationships is spending time sitting in close proximity. They may groom, or play, or even sit in contact. More often than not they just like to hang out near their close partners. Just being close to one another is meaningful. Try a car journey or a five-a-side game. Build trust and relationship through activities where nobody has to look anyone in the eye. If a mate isn’t much of a talker, doing something alongside him might get further than sitting him down and asking how he’s doing. Reach out before a crisis, not after. Great apes exchange most of their reassurance in the tense minutes before competition, not the wreckage afterward. Reconciliation and comforting are commonplace in humans and primates, but it’s much easier to prevent escalation than it is to smooth things over after a fight. Don’t wait to be the one who’s approached. That 22-point gap between how we actually cooperate and what we think everyone else will do shows the power of offering an invitation to relationship. Most of us are waiting for someone else to go first. Influencers selling young men and boys on their primal nature fly in the face of the science and what nature actually looks like. Force and competition are real parts of biology, and something we humans have to manage in our everyday lives. But, if there’s one thing I’ve learned from my many years studying our closest relatives, it’s that touch, care, cooperation, and even love are the most instinctive natural components of being part of the primate family.

#4051. What Bystanders Can Do to Stop Sexual Violence

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

As an undergrad student decades ago, I attended a dorm presentation describing the importance of giving and receiving sexual consent. The workshop leaders explained that if your partner said “no” or “stop” but you proceeded to have sex anyway, it was considered rape.

A high-profile athlete on campus raised his hand and said, in utter disbelief, “That can’t be right. By that definition, everyone I’ve ever had sex with I’ve raped.”

I was horrified by his comment, but most students laughed. He clearly saw nothing wrong with the view he’d expressed, presumably believing it was widely shared.

I immediately thought of this experience when I heard about the alleged sexual assault committed by seven fraternity members at Cornell University. As a social psychologist, and the author of a book examining why so-called good people stay silent in the face of problematic behavior, what I find most tragic about the events at Cornell is that reportedly over 50 men on a group chat learned about the assault as it was happening. Yet there’s been no public report that any of them tried to stop the assault, help the victim, or contact campus or local police departments.

This type of inaction is very common. In about one-third of sexual assaults, at least one other person is present who potentially could intervene but in most instances chooses not to. Why?

Data suggests a minority of men actually commit sexual assault. Researchers estimate about 6.5% of male college students in the United States and Canada have committed rape. Yet many men who find sexual violence offensive mistakenly believe that other men do not.

Undergraduate men in one study reported their beliefs about common rape myths, such as “If a girl doesn’t physically fight back, you can’t really say it was rape,” and “If a girl goes to a room alone with a guy at a party, it is her own fault if she is raped.” Researchers then asked participants how they thought both their close friends and average male college students would answer these same questions.

The results provided clear evidence that college men have skewed views of what those around them are thinking. Most students saw their classmates and friends as more supportive of rape myths than they themselves were.

Unfortunately, believing that other men generally accept sexual violence—even when this belief is wrong—inhibits their willingness to intervene to stop such behavior when they encounter it. In fact, men’s beliefs about their peers’ attitudes are a stronger predictor of their willingness to intervene than their own stated attitudes about sexual aggression.

Men who underestimate their peers’ discomfort with sexually aggressive behavior often fear the consequences of calling out problematic views or intervening in the face of inappropriate behavior. The most common reason male college students give for failing to intervene in situations involving sexual violence is a fear of being laughed at or ridiculed—in particular, a desire not to appear weak to other men.

Men who actually commit assault also tend to overestimate support for such behavior. Not surprisingly, men who report having committed sexual assault believe that other men are more accepting of such behavior and have engaged in such behavior themselves.

This assumed support leads them to feel comfortable describing problematic behavior, which leads other men to believe that sexual violence is more common, and more accepted, than it actually is.

Given the consistent evidence that men wrongly believe that most men are more comfortable with sexual violence than they themselves are, the challenge is not to change the social norm. It’s getting people to see what it actually already is.

Correcting such misperceptions can go a long way toward reducing sexual violence. First-year male college students who participate in a 90-minute sexual assault prevention program—focused on refuting rape myths, increasing awareness of the importance of consent, and correcting misperceptions about other men’s attitudes and behaviors—report reduced levels of sexual aggression themselves four months later.

These programs work in two ways. First, men who receive accurate information about what other men actually believe are less inhibited about speaking out and intervening. Second, men who are at risk of engaging in sexual aggression may change their intentions after learning that their peers do not support such behavior.

At the same time, men in tight-knit groups feel considerable pressure to show loyalty to other group members, which may lead to ignoring bad behavior committed by their peers.

But the underlying dynamics of peer influence can help shift group norms from protecting group members by staying silent to stepping in to prevent bad behavior. One college football player described looking out for his teammates, even if that meant interrupting a potential sexual encounter: “Maybe you don’t get the girl, but you’ll keep your scholarship and still be on the team.”

Another approach for preventing sexual assault is to learn strategies for intervening. Bystander intervention programs emphasize the importance of stopping bad behavior before it escalates, teach specific skills for intervening, and remind people that everyone has a responsibility to prevent sexual violence.

Students who participate in such programs are more confident in their ability to intervene and more willing to do so. Most importantly, rates of sexual violence are markedly lower on campuses that have implemented these programs. For example, one study found that experiences of sexual harassment and stalking were 11% lower and that instances of unwanted sexual activity with women who were too drunk or high to stop them were as much as 17% lower.

It’s tempting to read about the Cornell case and assume the apparent inaction in the face of problematic behavior is uniquely a problem at one college, or only with members of fraternities, or only with college students. But the reality is that pretty much everyone has been in situations where they’ve seen potentially problematic behavior and failed to speak up.

Understanding the very normal psychological factors that lead people to misperceive what others are actually thinking and feeling—and learning specific strategies for intervening—can make a real difference.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

is the Poler Family Professor of Psychology at Amherst College. Her latest trade book, published in North America as Why We Act: Turning Bystanders Into Moral Rebels (Harvard University Press) and internationally as The Bystander Effect: The Psychology of Courage and Inaction (HarperCollins), examines why good people so often stay silent or do nothing in the face of wrongdoing.

#2121. Florida Bar fires employee after investigation into abusive messages to football player

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Damon Wilson II of the Miami Hurricanes rushes the line during the second half of the game against the Wake Forest Demon Deacons on Sept. 18 in Winston-Salem, North Carolina. (Photo by Jared C. Tilton/Getty Images)

The Florida Bar has dismissed a computer technician after a short investigation into abusive Instagram messages sent to a defensive lineman for the University of Miami.

According to a story by the Associated Press, the Florida Bar, which regulates attorneys in the state, said it terminated Kade McLendon on Wednesday. McLendon had been with the Florida Bar for roughly two years.

The AP reported that Damon Wilson II received messages over Instagram that contained multiple racist and homophobic slurs after the Miami Hurricanes’ 43-35 victory over the Florida State Seminoles, its in-state rival team. According to the AP, the messages came from an account from a person using McLendon’s name.

In a statement Wednesday, the Florida Bar told the AP that it condemned the messages as reprehensible and is at odds with its mission, values and policies. McLendon denied sending them, the Florida Bar said, but he did not offer proof strong enough to convince it that his account had been compromised.

The AP said it tried to reach McLendon on Tuesday without success.

#2056. Athletic conference pushes back on university’s recruiting claims over ‘hallucinated’ case citations

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Disputing claims of “hallucinated” case citations in a lawsuit challenging Louisiana State University’s attempts to add former professional players to its sports teams, a collegiate athletic conference asked an Alabama court Thursday to strike the allegations. (Photo from Shutterstock)

Disputing claims of “hallucinated” case citations in a lawsuit challenging Louisiana State University’s attempts to add former professional players to its sports teams, a collegiate athletic conference asked an Alabama court Thursday to strike the allegations.

In September, the Southeastern Conference sued Louisiana State University regarding attempts to include players on its football team who previously had joined NFL training camps, asking the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Alabama to affirm its right to impose penalties under the First Amendment.

The school did not include the former NFL players, but the suit has continued, as the Southeastern Conference asserts that Louisiana State University pushed the NCAA eligibility boundaries by courting former professional basketball players for its men’s team.

Three of the conference’s citations in a recent filing “appear to be negligently supplied or hallucinated,” according to Louisiana State University, saying the cases cut against the conference’s argument regarding the court’s jurisdiction over the case.

On Thursday, the Southeastern Conference pushed back, explaining its citations in greater detail and called on the court to correct the record.

#1204. Spain grows fruit and vegetables for Europe, but it's running out of water

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Spain's southern coast produces up to 25% of Europe's fruit and vegetables. But amid intensive farming, climate change and illegal wells, the water it takes to grow them is drying up.

Donana National Park in southwestern Spain — one of Europe's most important wetlands, home to millions of migratory birds and threatened species like the Iberian lynx — is running dry. But rising temperatures and drought caused by climate change are only part of the problem.

The UNESCO-protected region on the Atlantic coast relies on an underground aquifer that's been tapped by hundreds of illegal wells. The reason: Europe's fruit and vegetable supply. Spain provides one-quarter of Europe's fresh produce, supplying colder parts of the continent with fruit and vegetables year-round.

The scale of water extraction in Spain is staggering: In 2016, WWF Spain estimated that the Donana wetland was home to over 1,000 illegal wells and more than 1,700 ponds illegally irrigating an area as big as 4,200 football fields.

"It's gotten hotter every year. And the water dries up so quickly," one local resident in the town of El Rocio, just north of the wetlands, told DW.

"For me, day to day, the main issue is my animals," said another, hitching his horse up to a tourist carriage. "I have to draw water for them, but the well doesn't have enough water to provide it all at once."

The Donana area used to be royal hunting grounds, consisting of marshes and ponds. In the early 20th century, parts began to be used for dryland farming, agriculture in arid regions that relies heavily on rainwater.

But in 1971, Spain — still under the dictatorship of General Francisco Franco — declared the region's irrigation to be of national interest. The Almonte-Marismas Plan, named after the aquifer it tapped into, was part of a massive, state-sponsored effort to increase food production.

Authorities decided to bet on strawberries, which they called "red gold." According to calculations based on FAO data, strawberries can yield up to €150,000 (about $170,000) in gross revenue per hectare per year, compared to roughly €20,000 for potatoes or €15,000 for grapes.

But growing strawberries at scale also meant using a lot more water. Authorities handed out generous quotas and allotments, seemingly without considering how much extraction the aquifer could handle. Even those legal quotas weren't enough for the ever-expanding fields.

Farmers turned to illegal wells and reservoirs to make up the difference. They built them to stockpile water, draining supplies from local residents and the UNESCO World Heritage Site next door.

"There was extreme drought and lack of attention from the administration," said Pedro Banez, of the Condado Water Users' Association. "And we found ourselves with a number of farms — or parts of farms — that fell outside the land classified as irrigable agricultural land."

The turning point came in 2019 when, following numerous reports of unlawful wells, a 2-year-old fell into deep, reportedly illegal borehole on farmland near Malaga and died. In the aftermath, Spain's Civil Guard police force created dedicated units focused on water theft.

Two years after that, in 2021, the Court of Justice of the European Union ruled that Spain had failed to protect the Donana aquifer, finding the country in violation of EU law.

Today, police and NGOs like WWF Spain track illegal extraction — near Donana, and other overtaxed agricultural regions across the country — using a mix of old and new tools. They start with satellite images of the region, comparing them against land rights maps to flag areas that appear greener than their legal status allows. Drones follow, giving investigators a closer look at where the water is actually coming from.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Some wells are completely illegal. Others are legal but extract more than they're allowed, which requires closer, on-site inspection to prove.

Investigators check the flow meters that measure how much water a well is pulling. Tampering is common, often using a powerful magnet which disrupts the internal mechanism of the flow meter, allowing water to pass through unrecorded.

Farmers caught extracting illegally can be charged with crimes against the environment and fraud. Most face fines, but sentences can run up to five years in prison.

In a bid to undo decades of overuse, local and national authorities have negotiated a €1.4 billion joint plan. The government has continued shutting down illegal wells, sealing almost 300 in the Donana region alone.

Officials have also started buying up farmland, rewilding it and turning it into protected areas, while setting up funds to create jobs in other sectors like tourism and renewable energy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Farmers, meanwhile, have had to change how they water their crops.

Many have switched to drip irrigation systems, where each plant gets its own individual dripper, paired with moisture sensors that report exactly how much water a plant needs. That cuts down on the overuse of both water and fertilizer.

Farmers have also begun planting less water-intensive crops and shifting their growing season to colder months.

Spain isn't alone in confronting this problem. As economies mature, fruit and vegetable production starts being outsourced to places with cheaper labor, fewer restrictions and more sun.

Mexico, Brazil and Colombia grow food for parts of the US, while countries in southeast Asia export a lot of produce to China or Japan. Some countries in northern Africa, including Morocco and Egypt, also supply fresh fruits and vegetables to their European neighbors to the north. But, as in Spain, intensive agriculture has strained local water supplies.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Morocco has tried to turn the tide by using satellite comparisons to monitor irrigation and offering subsidies for farmers who switch to drip irrigation. In India, the government is trying to move farmers toward less water-intensive crops.

And in California, the US state has paid farmers to halt certain types of irrigation and introduced legislation to monitor and allocate water more sustainably.

Securing water, no longer seen as an unlimited resource, will increasingly be a challenge as the effects of climate change intensify. Addressing overextraction will take a coordinated effort from governments, farmers and the industries that depend on their water.

Additional research by Nicole Ris. Edited by: Martin Kuebler, Sarah Steffen

#1112. Senegal's Youth Olympics makes Olympics in Africa 'logical'

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The Youth Olympics are to be hosted in Africa for the first time ever. The edition in Senegal is also a chance to prove the continent can host a full Olympics in the future.

At the end of October 2026, the first ever Olympic event on African soil will take place as the Youth Olympics comes to Senegal.

Around 2,700 young athletes are expected to compete across a total of 153 events in 25 sports, with many qualifying through the African Youth Games last year.

"We were entrusted with hosting this event and that is why from Johannesburg to Nairobi, from Dakar to Djbouti, it is all of young Africa that is watching you and watching you with pride," Senegalese President Bassirou Diomaye Faye said at the arrival of the flame.

The moment marks yet another step forward for global sport in Africa. In 2025, the first major road cycling event took place on the continent, and in September this year it was announced the first-ever World Athletics Championships would be held in Kenya in 2029. There are also on-going negotiations about Formula 1 returning. It appears that 16 years on since the 2010 World Cup in South Africa, global sport is finally returning to different parts of Africa.

"This is a victory for Africa. It opens the door for Africa to host major events," Malick Diouf, head of a marketing agency and founder of the Dakar Sport Summit, told DW.

"Africa is in the process of opening up to the world, hosting major events and everything. And all of Africa must celebrate this kind of victory."

With more major events in Africa, the question around an Olympic Games being on the continent looms.

Sports scientist Kamilla Swart has said awarding Africa a Games would make the world "take Africa more seriously." Diouf believes it's the logical next step.

"The Youth Olympic Games, it's a major test for Africa. And I think this is where we need to succeed in organizing these Games and also in sending a message," Diouf said.

"We, Africa, are ready to host the Summer Olympics, and I think that would be the logical next step after the Dakar Olympics. We have Olympic champions; we have record holders; we have Olympians, so we deserve to host the Olympic Games."

The move to re-use and refurbish existing facilities rather than build whole new ones is likely why Senegal's bid was so tempting for the International Olympic Committee (IOC).

The fear of the white elephant facility appears to have been considered here. The Iba Mar Diop Stadium, the track and field stadium, is expected to be used for athlete training ahead of the 2028 Olympics.

"It’s by using these facilities, by participating in and hosting international events, and by consistently maintaining them to the required standards, that we’ll be able to keep these stadiums up to the standards they currently meet," Diouf said.

There's also the social and economical impacts of the Youth Olympics. Diouf believes the impact is beyond just infrastructure, but into roads, the development of the hospitality, security and broadcasting sectors and the young athletes themselves.

"There's life after the Games. Now we need to figure out how to use the resources we have, we need to develop and capitalize on what we have showcased during the Games," said Diouf.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The significance of these Games stretch beyond even the history they are making in Africa. In May of this year, IOC President Kirsty Coventry put the brakes on the future of the Youth Olympics, leaving real doubts as to whether there will be any more after the 2028 winter edition.

"We need to pause and have a real reflection on why we're doing the Youth Olympic Games and we realized that across the Movement it's very disjointed. There's not a true North Star of why we're doing the Youth Olympic Games," Coventry told reporters.

"So the EB (IOC Executive Board) decided to pause the 2030 process right now so there will not be an election in June, and rather we need to take a step back and develop a youth strategy."

A lot has changed since the Youth Olympic Games were launched in 2010. Initially it was seen as a way to engage a younger audience (athletes were limited by age to between 15 and 17) by trialing new sports as well as testing host country's on a smaller stage. However, with the age of athletes decreasing and the challenges of engaging a younger audience in the modern sports entertainment landscape even more challenging, it appears clear the IOC are reconsidering their options when it comes to investment.

"My only wish is that Dakar be memorable and that people reconsider their decision not to host the Games in 2030," Diouf said.

"I don’t want these to be the last Youth Olympic Games, but if these are to be the last Youth Olympic Games, we need to go out with a bang. Dakar needs to show the world what Senegal is all about, what Africa is all about."

From October 31 to November 13 this year, Senegal will have the chance to do just that.

#1092. Klopp wins first game as Germany coach, without 'top dogs'

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

It may have taken a little longer than he would have liked, but Jürgen Klopp has finally won a game as Germany coach. Despite a number of debutants, it was a familiar face who stood out in the victory over Serbia.

After 47 days, three matches and 44 squad players, Jürgen Klopp has a first win as coach of Germany's men's fotball team. A straightforward 2-0 victory over Serbia in the Nations League on Thursday meant the former Liverpool and Borussia Dortmund coach avoided becoming the first Germany boss to win any of his first three games.

"I'm really happy today," Klopp told German public broadcaster ZDF. "We were outstanding when defending and when we had the ball. We simply played wonderful football."

Klopp had raised a few eyebrows by naming two squads for the four fixtures in this extended international window. But after a somewhat fortunate draw against the Netherlands and a shock loss to Greece with the more senior of the squads, it was left to those lower down the pecking order to get things going Thursday in Munich.

That meant a completely new starting XI and debuts to ‌goalkeeper Jonas Urbig ⁠and ⁠winger Derry Scherhant. Such players should "develop without the top dogs" Klopp said before the game. And while Urbig was untroubled by Serbia, his Bayern Munich teammate Tom Bischof made his mark by turning in the opener after Florian Wirtz, one of the sprinkling of senior players, hit the post. It was Bischof's first goal for Germany in only his second national team game.

"It's indescribable," said Bischof after the game at Bayern's Allianz Arena. "The stadium was on fire. I let it all out and was overjoyed." Unfortunately, he said, "my knees hurt a little from sliding on my knees. But that doesn't matter."

After a string of tournament failures, the latest new dawn for Germany comes with a little more patience. As well as Klopp's track record, there is a general acceptance from inside the country and abroad that Germany are no longer a top tier side.

"We're not the Bayern Munich of European football," Klopp said prematch. "We're a team that needs to find its footing."

While one win against a limited Serbia side will not change that, the emphatic nature of the performance impressed the new boss.

"I liked everything — the opening phase, the middle section, and the end. Our counter-pressing was off the charts," said the man whose Gegenpressing (Counter-pressing) tactic became his trademark at Dortmund. "We can afford to take all the liberties and risks we want when we win the ball back together. Everyone was there during our pressing moments."

The second half brought more debuts for players who may not have made a traditional 26-man squad. Cologne winger Said El Mala and Brentford's Vitaly Janelt made their Germany bows while Celtic's Mika Baur became the country's 1,000th men's international.

It was "surreal," Baur said. "Today was a highlight for everyone, not just for me with my debut."

While Klopp may have seen enough from a few of the second string to afford them opportunities in the future, the performance of Liverpool midfielder Wirtz, who scored Germany's second goal, may be the most critical of the night.

Wirtz, 23, has struggled to find his best form in the Premier League and has blown hot and cold in his 47 caps for Germany. A cool and composed finish suggested that the player who was so highly regarded at Bayer Leverkusen can be coaxed to life by Klopp.

"It was loads of fun. Every single one of us gave it our all on the field. We stuck to the game plan and did almost everything right," said Wirtz, who took the captain's armband later in the game. "The players around me made it easy for me. We had a lot of movement in the middle. We were all fired up."

The challenge now for Klopp is to work out which of his wide pool of players are the ones he can rely on to take Germany to Euro 2028 and beyond. The next step is a chance for revenge against Greece in Thessaloniki on Sunday.

#1075. Manchester City Premier League charges: What do they mean?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Since being bought by the UAE in 2008, Manchester City has become one of the world's most successful football clubs. A raft of serious financial charges threatens to undermine this and bring chaos to the Premier League.

Manchester City has won the English Premier League eight times over the last 14 years. Whether those titles will remain is one of a plethora of issues at stake after England's top division charged one of its most high-profile clubs with more than 100 breaches of its financial regulations.

On Tuesday, the Premier League announced that an independent commission had found City had drawn up "sham contracts" as well as "relying on sham agreements to artificially inflate the club's revenues and reduce its costs." These contracts, dubious sponsorships and a number of other methods were, according to the commission's report, used to inflate the club's revenue to the tune of about £900 million (€1.05 billion) over the period between the 2009-10 and 2017-18 seasons.

The case has been ongoing for eight years after initial revelations from Der Spiegel, a German magazine, and Rui Pinto of the 'Football Leaks' website. City continues to deny any wrongdoing.

"The Club is innocent of the accusations made by the Premier League and a comprehensive body of irrefutable evidence exists in support of all of its positions, relating to this case," read a club statement. "Manchester City FC will now pursue the appeal avenues open to it, on the basis that the opinion contains clear material errors, of law, principle and fact, and is unsafe."

On Friday, the club issued a further statement saying it had lodged a "comprehensive Appeal."

The 750-page report was damning, accusing City of inflating contracts and sponsorship deals, running secondary contracts off the books, making "concerted efforts to stop and frustrate the Premier League investigation" and signing off inflated image rights deals to artificially boost their earnings. The report says all this enabled them to pass both Premier League and UEFA financial spending limits.

The Premier League's chief ­executive, Richard Masters, described the case "as the most significant in Premier League history," adding that the club "systematically broke Premier League Rules for nearly a decade."

Although the Premier League, the world's most commercially successful football league, has drawn some praise for its actions, others point out that it took Pinto leaking emails and media reports to take action. The report shows that Manchester City, which had won the league once in 71 years before being taken over by the United Arab Emirates (UAE) in 2008, were consistently posting revenues way higher than any of their more established and successful rivals.

In this context, its the owners. The Abu Dhabi United Group, essentially an investment vehicle for the UAE royal family, bought the club in 2008. Mansour bin Zayed Al Nahyan, know simply as Sheikh Mansour, fronted the takeover. He is a member of the ruling Al Nahyan family and the UAE's vice president and deputy prime minister. Khaldoon Al Mubarak, who also holds several government positions in the UAE, was brought in as chairman.

Nation-state ownership of this type is controversial in European football. Manchester City's owners have long been criticized for the country's human rights record and using the club for sportswashing. Similar criticisms have been made of European champions Paris Saint-Germain, which Qatar owned and City's Premier League rivals Newcastle United, which is owned 85% by Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund.

City's CEO, Ferran Soriano, joined in 2012 as City expanded their ownership into foreign leagues, buying up clubs in the US, Japan and Australia. He replaced former head coach Roberto Mancini with fellow Spaniard Manuel Pellegrini before former Bayern Munich and Barcelona coach Pep Guardiola took over in 2016, ushering in the club's most successful period.

The club immediately started splashing out on big-money transfers after the UAE investment arrived. At the time, neither UEFA nor the Premier League had the tight financial rules they now have. UEFA introduced its Financial Fair Play (FFP) rules for the 2011-12 season, while the Premier League followed with its Profit and Sustainability Rules (PSR) in 2013-14.

City's free spending and relatively low commercial income meant they were likely to fall foul of such rules. The club was handed a conditional €60 million UEFA fine for losses in the 2011-12 and 2012-13 seasons. They would later be banned from the Champions League on similar grounds in 2020 before being reinstated on appeal.

In an attempt to try and comply with these financial rules, City set up a system they called "Project Longbow" in 2012. The commission said much of this was genuine, but one arm of it was an agreement with a third party called Fordham that was, according to the ruling, "little more than a front." Essentially, it reads, the club's UAE owners "would be used (and were used) to enable Fordham to acquire from the club (at a sizeable, artificially inflated price) the club's entitlement to benefit financially from its players' image rights."

In other words, City's owners were pumping money into their own club through vastly inflated sponsorship and image rights deals so their income looked higher and they could spend more, particularly on player transfers and wages. The report also claims that City paid three individuals, whose names are redacted, through a third party to keep them off the books.

That's the question on everyone's lips. So far, no sanctions have been imposed on City, and the appeal will likely drag proceedings out even longer. Meanwhile, the club, which is top of the Premier League, faces Liverpool on October 11, after the international break.

While there is no precedent for such a tranche of charges, clubs have previously fallen foul of the Premier League's PSR regulations. Everton were handed a 10-point penalty for a £19.5 million breach over a period ending with the 2021-22 season. This was based on a Premier League tariff of six points for the initial breach, plus an additional point for every £5 million the club overspent. Given that City's overspending is close to a billion pounds, applying that would certainly relegate them, though the Premier League does not operate England's second-tier league, the Championship.

For now, it looks like City and the league will play on under a cloud. But if the verdict remains after appeal, clubs and individuals whose careers and honors stand to be impacted will likely come forward and litigate. City themselves could be relegated, docked points over successive seasons, face transfer bans or any combination of those things. The titles won in the relevant period could also be stripped.

This article was originally published on September 30, 2026. It was updated on October 2, 20206 with Manchester City's statement confirming that it had lodged an appeal.

#1057. Germany's Franz Reindl elected new IIHF president

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Franz Reindl has been elected president of ice hockey's world governing body. The former forward was a member of West Germany's Olympic bronze medal-winning team.

Franz Reindl came from behind to narrowly beat Petr Briza of the Czech Republic in a run-off vote to become the International Ice Hockey Federation's new president on Friday.

Reindl had trailed Briza 48-36 in the first round of voting but picked up crucial votes after Aivaz Omorkanov of Kyrgyzstan was eliminated. He then beat Briza 60-57 in the runoff held on the Spanish island of Tenerife. Six of a total of nine candidates had withdrawn prior to the election.

The 71-year-old German, who had been a member of the IIHF Council since 2016, succeeds Canadian-born Frenchman Luc Tardif, who had announced in March that he would not seek a further term in office. Reindl's election comes 5 years after he had finished as runner-up to Tardif. His term runs until 2030.

Reindl has done it all in German ice hockey, having been a player and coach, as well as serving as the first CEO of the top-flight professional league, the Deutsche Eishockey Liga (DEL), from 1994 to 19<96. He also headed the organizing committees for three World Championships — in 2001, 2010 and 2017. From 2014 to 2022 he served as president of the German Ice Hockey Association (DEB), overseeing an upturn in the fortunes of the men's national team, which won the silver medal at the Pyeongchang Winter Olympics in 2018.

"Franz Reindl has shaped German ice hockey for decades—as a player, but above all through the various key roles he has held within our federation," the current DEB chairman, Frank Lutz, said.

"The fact that the international ice hockey family is now entrusting him with the leadership of the IIHF is a great tribute to his work."

Like Briza, who was a goalie for the Czechoslovakia team that won bronze in Albertville in 1992, Reindl was also an Olympic bronze medalist, having been a member of the 1976 West Germany team who beat the United States and Finland to finish third in Lake Placid.

Domestically, Reindl played 669 games and scored 423 goals for SC Riessersee and SB Rosenheim over 16 seasons in the Eishockey-Bundesliga, the predecessor to the DEL.

Tardif's years as IIHF president were marked by two major events: the return of National Hockey League players to the Olympic tournament in Milan Cortina this year, and the exclusion from international play of Russia in 2022 over the country's full-scale war on neighboring Ukraine.

Reindl is the second German to be elected IIHF president after Günter Sabetzki, who held the post from 1975 to 1994.

#973. Munich up against at least six competitors for 2036 Olympics

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The International Olympic Committee has revealed the six other countries intending to bid for the 2036 Olympic Games. This comes after Germany selected Munich as its Olympic candidate.

Munich's bid for the 2036 Summer Olympics will be up against at least six candidates from Europe, Asia and Africa. On Tuesday, the International Olympic Committee (IOC) announced that, in addition to Germany, Hungary, Turkey, India, Qatar, South Korea and South Africa all intend to bid for the Games. All are currently engaged in the Continuous Dialogue stage of the bidding process with the IOC.

However, there could be further applicants, as the deadline for interested parties to submit a bid is October 31. The candidates are to present their plans virtually to the Future Host Commission in November. In March 2027, the IOC is to deliberate, analyze, and narrow down the field of applicants to a group selected to enter into the Strategic Dialogue stage.

A subsequent Targeted Dialogue stage with one or more bidders is planned for 2028, with the final decision on the 2036 Olympic host expected at the IOC Session in the summer of 2029.

However, the IOC did not disclose exactly which cities, alongside Munich, are in the running – although Ahmedabad, Doha, Budapest and Istanbul are seen as possible hopefuls.

Munich secured its bid late last month after beating the Cologne/Rhine-Ruhr region in a vote by delegates of the German Olympic Sports Confederation. The city last hosted the Games in 1972. Berlin had withdrawn its bid in the days leading up to the vote.

"Wherever the Games take place, one thing must never change: Athletes have to be at the heart of everything we do," IOC President Kirsty Coventry said.

"As an athlete, you spend years working to make your Olympic dream come true. The experience you have when you finally reach the Olympic Games stays with you for the rest of your life," added the former swimmer who won gold in the 200-meter backstroke for Zimbabwe at the 2004 Athens and 2008 Beijing Summer Games.

"That is why, for every future host, delivering the best possible experience for the athletes must always be the starting point."

The IOC is to evaluate the bidders' proposals based on "technical factors." These include a master plan for competition venues and the athlete experience, the financing strategy, political and public support, the capacity to deliver the event, and a sustainability strategy. The German Olympic Sports Confederation believes Munich is well-positioned in all these areas.

The Munich bid is not focused solely on 2036 but was also designed with 2040 and 2044 in mind. If it misses out on 2036, Munich can expect to face a similarly large pool of competitors for the following two Summer Games. According to an IOC statement, there are National Olympic Committees "taking part in noncommittal talks with the IOC about hosting future Olympic Games beyond 2036."

#947. Ronaldo stays on under cloud as Messi exits internationals

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Just hours before Lionel Messi played his last match for Argentina, Cristiano Ronaldo posted his latest salvo in a row with Portugal. Ronaldo wants to play on despite the troubles, while Messi bathed in adulation.

They are the two players who have defined the last two decades. As football became ever more popular, commercial and dominant in the cultural and sporting landscape, Cristiano Ronaldo and Lionel Messi remained its twin peaks.

Along with that has come endless social media posts, stories and discussions about who is better. Does Messi's World Cup and two Copa America titles with Argentina trump Ronaldo's Euro 2016 win and two Nations League titles? Or does the Portuguese's greater number of goals mean more? How about their club honors?

It is, essentially, a subjective argument and not one that can ever be resolved. It remains to be seen whether that's also true of the public spat between Ronaldo and his Portugal coach Jorge Jesus.

On Tuesday, Messi scored a 126th goal in his 208th game for Argentina. Shortly before he did, Ronaldo posted a 2,000-word statement in which he stopped short of quitting the national team but said there was an "absolute breakdown of trust" between him and the coach.

Ronaldo's comments refer to Portugal's recent match against Norway in which he said he was initially to be rested before being asked to warm up as a substitute without ever being brought on. The striker, 41, walked away from the team's camp for the remainder of the international break, but said the story is not over.

"I can promise all my fans, and naturally especially all Portuguese people, that we will have one or more farewell matches. There will be time for that," said Ronaldo, who plays for Al-Nassr in Saudi Arabia.

"Despite the absolute breach of trust in the coach's word, as anyone who witnessed these conversations can attest, I should not have done it," Ronaldo said. "The responsibility is entirely mine. I apologize to the Portuguese people once again, but the breaking of one's word has, for me, a defining impact.

"I felt that, from that point on, my staying would only harm the group, and I did what I have always said I would do if I didn't feel useful: I picked up my bags and left," he continued before suggesting he accepted there may be a need for the coach or federation to punish him.

It was a long way from the emotional scenes in Rosario, Argentina. Instead of anger and recrimination, the overriding emotions there were gratitude, nostalgia and sadness.

"Not coming anymore is going to be deeply painful. I swear I'd like to play for the rest of my life, because playing for the Argentina national team is the most beautiful thing that can happen to you," Messi, who now plays his club football for Inter Miami in the US, told the 80,000 fans who had packed in to see him score a penalty in a 3-0 win over Benin.

For some, the contrasting evenings offered a neat synopsis of the pair. Ronaldo, less naturally gifted than his counterpart, has made the most of his talents with a singular focus which many believe often makes him prioritize himself over the team. He has had similar fallouts with managers and head coaches before, including Jose Mourinho at Real Madrid, Erik ten Hag at Manchester United and Portugal's 2022 World Cup coach, Fernando Santos.

Ronaldo's much-discussed aim to reach 1,000 career goals is held up by some as further evidence of where his motivations lie.

"You know what my goal is. I want to win trophies, and I want to reach that number (1,000 goals) that you all know. I will reach the number for sure, if no injuries," he said last December. He is currently on 979.

For others, that is simply what is required to be the best. While Messi's quiet character and general refusal to play out his life in public suggest a more humble character focused on helping the team, his genius has undoubtedly changed the teams around him. In the 2026 World Cup, he barely broke into a run but still led an otherwise fairly limited Argentina to the final with eight goals.

There will be no more Messi goals for Argentina and, despite his promises, no absolute assurances that the world will see Ronaldo in a Portugal shirt again. For now, both will head back to their club sides to play out their twilight years away from the intensity of Europe's top leagues. But wherever they go from here, the spotlight will keep following.

#790. Germany's women: Ena Mahmutovic makes her case to become Germany's next No.1 goalkeeper

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Germany's win against Australia saw Ena Mahmutovic excel in goal. The 22-year-old Bayern Munich goalkeeper is considered the successor to Ann-Katrin Berger and is making a name for herself.

With determination, pace, and four beautifully crafted goals, Germany's women won its friendly against Australia, thrilling not only the fans but also head coach Christian Wück.

"You can see how fired up the girls are, how well they're already playing together, and that we have an incredible amount of talent on the field and on the bench," said a delighted Wück after his team's well-deserved 4-2 victory.

Since there were several notable absences, including captain Giulia Gwinn and top scorer Lea Schüller, others had to step up and fill the gaps. And they did so with flying colors. Melissa Kössler made a strong case for herself as a striker by scoring her first two international goals. Center back Camilla Küver and Sjoeke Nüsken were also on the scoresheet.

Much like it was with the men's team during Jürgen Klopp's first international break as Germany head coach, the question as to who will emerge as the next number one goalkeeper for the women was also a hot topic.

The difference is that unlike Manuel Neuer, who has retired from international football, Ann-Katrin Berger is sticking around for at least one more tournament cycle.

However, with just three matches to go before the start of the playoffs in the National Women's Soccer League (NWSL), where she plays for Gotham FC, Berger is giving these two Germany friendlies a miss.

This means 22-year-old Bayern Munich goalkeeper Ena Mahmutovic, who is seen as a potential successor to Berger, took her her chance.

"Ena deserves to play the next match," Wück said ahead of the game. "We must not forget that she already kept a clean sheet in Slovenia (Germany's last World Cup qualifier). And I hope she can repeat that tomorrow."

Some will draw parallels to Jonas Urbig, who many see as a future number one for Germany's men. Both he and Mahmutovic were born in 2003 in the western state of North Rhine-Westphalia, and both are seen as up-and-coming stars for their club and country.

The difference is that while Urbig remains an understudy to Neuer at Bayern, Mahmutovic is already the first-choice keeper at her club, having won the starting job over Maria Luisa Grohs prior to the start of the season.

For Germany, though, Mahmutovic still has to bide her time, while there are already strong indications that Klopp intends to make Urbig his number one.

"I'm obviously delighted," Mahmutovic said ahead of the games.

"It's always something special, of course – especially playing against a team from another continent. I think testing ourselves against them is very helpful as we look ahead to the World Cup."

Bayern director Bianca Rech recently described Mahmutovic as "the future of the national team," calling on Wück not only to give her a callup, but also regular playing time.

Wück still sees Berger as slightly better than Mahmutovic, but he is also full of praise for the young keeper.

"Ena's performances have been very good," he said. "She will get her playing time."

The match against Slovenia was just Mahmutovic's second appearances for Germany. Her first, which came almost two years ago, was memorable for the wrong reason. In the dying minutes of a friendly in December 2024, she lost control of the ball after a back pass, allowing an Italian forward to score the winner in a 2-1 defeat for Germany. Still, Mahmutovic was included in Germany's squad for the 2025 Euros.

Had it been up to her father – who is from Montenegro – Mahmutovic would never have become a goalkeeper.

"He still says I would have been better as an outfield player because I'm good with both feet," she said.

That's a quality that evidently wasn't lost on Thomas Gerstner, when her coach at her then-club Duisburg, gave her her Women's Bundesliga debut in May 2020. With the team coping with several absences, the then-16-year-old, who had been on the bench as backup goalkeeper, came on as an outfield player.

"The coach brought me on during stoppage time," Mahmutovic recalled. "I got to make two sprints – that was my debut."

That, however, was a one-off, and nobody questions Mahmutovic's strengths as a goalkeeper.

"She is one of the most talented goalkeepers in the world," said Bayern coach Jose Barcala, noting that she is the kind of player who can decide a match.

Mahmutovic will be hoping she gets many more opportunities to do just that for Germany.

This article was updated on October 12 after Germany's friendly against Australia.

#745. Formula 1: Rwanda to bring sport back to Africa

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The last time Formula 1 hosted a race in Africa was way back in 1993. But a new track in Rwanda is set to host a Grand Prix in "the early 2030s," continuing the country’s investment in international sport.

After nearly 40 years of false starts, motor racing's premier sport is returning to Africa, after Formula 1 announced on Tuesday that Rwanda would soon host a Grand Prix.

The race will be know as the African Grand Prix after a 10-year contract was signed between the parties. With no suitable track ready, Rwanda will build one in Bugesera, southeast of the capital Kigali. F1 said it will be ready "early in the next decade."

The last time F1 raced in Africa was the South African Grand Prix in 1993. While there has since been plenty of talk of the sport returning to the continent, most often mentioning South Africa itself, nothing concrete had happened, until now.

"Today is a landmark moment for Formula 1," F1 President and chief executive Stefano Domenicali said. "With Rwanda joining the calendar from the next decade our sport will race in every inhabited continent, bringing the world championship closer than ever to fans around the globe.

"Africa has always been part of Formula 1's future, and from the beginning we were determined to return in the right way, with the right partner, a long-term commitment and a clear vision for creating meaningful impact."

One of the most vocal proponents of a race in Africa has been seven-time world champion Lewis Hamilton. The 41-year-old Black British driver, currently with Ferrari, has said he has been "fighting in the background" for years to have a race in Africa. With the date not yet set in stone for the African Grand Prix, it remains to be seen whether he can prolong his career enough to be on the grid when the lights go out in Rwanda.

For Rwandan President Paul Kagame, the event is "a success and a milestone for the entire continent, especially for the African fans." He added that he hoped it could lead to more F1 events in Africa.

Kagame has been president of Rwanda for 25 years, and essentially ran the country for the six years before that, following the genocide in 1994. He routinely wins over 99% of the vote in elections, and the country is often criticized for its human rights record.

The 68-year-old has bet big on sports. Rwanda has had sponsorship deals with a number of top European football clubs, including Arsenal, Atletico Madrid, Paris Saint-Germain and Bayern Munich. However, fan and media backlash over the deal caused Bayern to drop the commercial element with the country's tourist arm, Visit Rwanda, last year. Arsenal did not renew its deal. Many critics have labeled the policy as sportswashing.

The country also has deals with top US basketball and American football teams, and hosted cycling's Road World Championships last year.

But Formula 1, one of the world's richest sports, will likely be seen as Kagame and Rwanda's biggest sporting coup to date.
