#726. FIFPRO report highlights growing pains in women's football

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Expected global revenue of over $1 billion and new faces at the U17 World Cup show women's football is booming, but a recent report also highlights need for better structures globally.

At first glance, women's football is healthier than ever. A recent report by FIFPRO, football's global players' union, said worldwide revenue is expected to reach over $1bn (€894m) in 2027. The World Cup in Brazil, a first in South America, will be the last with 32 teams and is expected to be the most watched and biggest revenue-generating women's tournament ever.

The good news continues with the U17 Women's World Cup in Morocco, where the tournament is being held every year for five straight years. Germany, France, Spain, Brazil, the United States and defending champions North Korea are all at the 2026 edition of a competition whose reputation continues to grow.

"You can just see where the game is going, which is so exciting," US head coach Ciara Crinion told FIFA. "The level has just gone up. We have to continue to stay with that and compete as well. [The U-17 Women's World Cup] almost feels like that first stepping stone in a major tournament for young players, and now you see how serious it is taken."

Puerto Rico, Australia and Argentina are making their debuts. For Puerto Rico, qualification is historic, as no football team in the men's or women's game has ever reached a World Cup tournament before.

"We've embraced this idea of being Puerto Rico's first team to qualify for a World Cup," head coach Andres Mirabelli told FIFA. Alongside national development plans, player recruitment was aided by a diaspora of nearly four million in the United States, perhaps the strongest place for women's football in the world. According to FIFPRO, the regional football average salary in North America is $5,940 (€5,547) per month, which is far higher than any other continent.

But even for the handful of players who make it out of these youth tournaments to a professional career, creating a living out of the beautiful game remains a real challenge. The aforementioned FIFPRO report details that many professional female players across the world barely earn minimum wage, with some even falling below it.

The report, which sourced its data from player surveys across the world, found that only 66% of professional players are paid for 12 months a year. The global average salary is $2,600 (€2,300) a month, but the range varies drastically across the world. In Eastern Europe, for example, that average is $880 per month. In Africa, it is $432, but in North America it is just under $6,000.

"I think what you also find in the women's game is the gaps are not just between the top five leagues and the rest. There is a gap there, but even within leagues themselves [there is one]," FIFPRO head of research and strategy for women's football Frankie Brown told DW.

"In some of the other leagues you're dealing with teams that are operating professionally, so will be paying their players on a reasonable wage, and playing in the same league as them you'll have players that are on amateur contracts and not being paid at all."

With economic and job security an issue for many, the report reveals that psychological stress factors are now the second main reason many consider leaving football.

"This is one of the most concerning parts of the report," Alex Culvin, the director of women's football at FIFPRO, said in a statement.

"Of the 49% of women’s football players considering leaving the sport early, 46% cited their mental health as the reason. In interviews, players say the sport's growth brings new pressures — including extra workload and social media abuse — but that clubs do not have the necessary safeguards in place to support these changes. We want clubs and national associations to provide mandatory mental health support focused on wellbeing, which is independent of sporting performance objectives."

FIFPRO's report is asking FIFA to set a so-called floor for the women's game so that minimum standards in key areas such as contracts, pensions and health can be enforced. Perhaps inspiration can be taken from the labor deal the WNBA agreed in 2026. It was long-negotiated deal that ultimately sent a message to female athletes everywhere to push for better pay, conditions and a greater share of the revenue they help generate.

"It is positive that average salaries are increasing for some players and that more are earning a satisfactory wage, but it’s important to note that there is a huge disparity in pay across regions," said Culvin.

"There is also an imbalance in conditions, not only across the globe but also within regions; in Europe, for example, the difference in standards between the east and west is enormous. This is a gulf we need to work hard to narrow; the growth in the most advanced markets must not be completely isolated from the rest of the industry.

With high hopes for the 2027 World Cup in Brazil and recent moves to increase professional football domestically, Germany is aiming to become an example for structural improvement in women's football. Hosting the Euros in 2029 certainly gives them an incentive to improve standards.

While salaries are in the upper tier — in the 2023-24 season, a women's Bundesliga player earned an average base salary of around €48,000 per year — the process of structural development in the country is still in its infancy. A new German football association rule shows that even basic professional structures are still being established. Only since the 2025–26 season have teams in the Women's Bundesliga been required to employ assistant coaches, goalkeeper coaches and athletic coaches on a full-time, professional basis.

"The facilities and the staff members," Nicole Anyomi told DW, were a factor in her making a recent move to London City Lionesses. "We have 30-plus staff members here whereas in Frankfurt we had like seven or eight. You can learn from each person and there is something new to learn every day. I think that's how you develop as a player faster."

Ultimately, the faster the game grows, the greater need to close the gaps.

"Despite playing on different continents, players are connected by their precarity," Culvin said. "The redistribution systems in women's football need to be more effective, touching more players and supporting a more sustainable ecosystem."

#631. Germany news: Train station alcohol ban comes into force

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Consuming alcohol is no longer permitted at train stations in Germany, to improve the lot of travelers and staff alike. Coalition talks are restarting in Berlin, and an inmate is loose in Essen.

Below, you can read our roundup of headlines across Germany from Thursday, October 15.

Tired of missing our real-time updates? Click hereto add DW as a Preferred Source on Google

Lawmakers from Germany's Left Party turned their backs on a far-right AfD politician after he questioned the historical consensus that Germany had committed genocide in what is now Namibia. The protest came during a heated debate over reparations for descendants of the victims.

Germany's government acknowledged the genocide in 2021, but parliament has yet to pass its own formal recognition resolution. A formal apology by Germany's president is also still pending. A proposed deal with Namibia includes €1.1 billion in development aid over 30 years, but no legally binding reparations. The agreement remains unsigned and controversial among descendants of the victims.

Germany and France reached a provisional agreement on Thursday that backs a scaling down of European emissions rules for new cars largely in line with the wishes of German carmakers, according to a draft document seen by news agencies.

The provisional agreement centers on a European Commission proposal last year backing off on banning new internal combustion engines by 2035, the new deal instead calls for a 90% drop in CO2 emissions from 2021 levels.

The remaining 10% was to be covered by compensatory measures like low-carbon ​steel and alternative fuels.

Now, the new agreement would scrap those measures, calling for the 10% to be compensated by manufacturers.

Europe's car industry, led by Germany, has been struggling in recent years amid the switch to electric vehicles and competition from China. This has led the EU to come up with ways to increase competitiveness while still maintaining pledges to reduce emissions. Germany and France have disagreed on how to do this.

The draft agreement still needs official confirmation. Then it faces approval by Germany's governing coalition led by the Christian Democrats (CDU), who have pushed for more watered-down emissions regulations than their Social Democratic Party (SPD) partners.

Then Germany and France will still need to convince other EU members and the European Parliament for the agreement to take effect.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Bundestag has lifted immunity of a center-left Social Democrat (SPD) lawmaker, seemingly as part of a corruption probe pertaining to a tender for a hospital site in the northern city of Hanover.

Adis Ahmetovic, 33, had his immunity lifted so as to authorize searches requested by investigators.

He is one of three SPD politicians from the Hanover area under investigation in connection with the site in Lehrte, near Hanover.

The Hanover Public Prosecutor's Office said Ahmetovic's Bundestag office and three apartments were searched on Wednesday after his immunity was lifted.

The case came to fairly considerable prominence earlier in the year, because one of these politicians is Steffen Krach.

Krach was the lead SPD candidate for last month's state election in Berlin, and news of him being under investigation surfaced at the height of the campaign.

This may well have helped the Left Party and the Greens to fare better than predicted in the poll, while the SPD slumped to 12% — by far the party's worst ever result in the capital.

Alcohol bans came into force at all German train stations when the clock struck midnight and Thursday began.

All the roughly 5,700 stations across Germany, including the train platforms themselves, are now booze-free zones.

Pubs, restaurants and other such hospitality businesses at train stations are exceptions to the rule, though, and can still serve alcohol. Consumption of alcohol will also remain legal on many trains, with long distance ones still serving beers and other tipples at their on-board bistros.

Rail operator Deutsche Bahn says the change aims to make train stations safer and cleaner, and more pleasant places to spend time waiting for a (who knows, perhaps delayed) train.

Various misdemeanors and incidents had prompted discussions on alcohol at stations over the years, but two this year in particular brought the issues into sharp focus.

A 26-year-old security staff member for Deutsche Bahn was beaten and kicked by a drunken passenger when trying to check their ticket. During the struggle the train door buckled and opened, and the Bahn employee fell from the train, suffering severe injuries. Earlier in the year, a train conductor was killed when attacked by a man without a valid ticket.

The new rules are part of DB's house rules, rather than being criminal law — meaning that at least in the first instance it is a matter for Bahn security staff to enforce rather than police.

"The alcohol consumption ban applies at stations every day, also on days with major events," Bahn spokeswoman Anja Bröder said when asked about often beer-fueled train usage days, like when football fans flock to Bundesliga games.

Around 40 stations piloted the project for some time prior to its implementation, including some of the largest like Cologne, Munich, Hamburg and Berlin. DB says it has had positive experiences with the early experiments, reporting more peaceful stations with less litter and travelers and employees saying they preferred the atmosphere.

A federal group dedicated to helping the homeless has criticized the measure, however, saying that for homeless people with addiction illnesses, train stations often served as "important, weatherproof and comparatively safe places" to shelter. A spokeswoman also told the Rheinische Post newspaper that if they were driven from sites like train stations, it would be harder for social workers to locate them.

German television's most famous orange mouse, WDR's eponymous star of the long running "Sendung mit der Maus" (Show with the mouse), stands in statue waving to whoever walks by the station's headquarters in Cologne.

And after a torrid few years for the celebrity rodent — in which he was kidnapped one year and set on fire by unknown assailants the next — now the mouse has been joined by its partners in crime the elephant and the duck, as was the case as the long-running show evolved.

"Life is always much better with friends," WDR Director General Katrin Vernau said at Thursday's unveiling. "Together the mouse, duck and elephant stand for a children's program that has conveyed knowledge for decades explaining the world and encouraging children to ask questions."

The children's show "Die Sendung mit der Maus" has been on WDR on Sunday mornings since 1971.

The show has various educational and entertainment elements and has had an array of presenters over the years, but the animated shorts featuring the mouse that separate the segments are probably the best known parts.

These short segments of perhaps 30 seconds of 2D animation never involve the mouse, or its accomplices the duck and the elephant who joined later, speaking.

Typically they interact and try to solve some kind of problem, often seeking a reward, by experimentation and trial and error.

Police in Essen continued their search for a 34-year-old prisoner and the two men who violently freed him during a doctor's visit the previous day.

Officers searched parts of the city center, with the help of tracking dogs trying to catch the fugitives' scent.

Two men are thought to have attacked and tied up the prison officers accompanying the inmate on his medical visit, before the three of them split up and fled the scene. Police warned at the time that it "could not be ruled out" that they might be armed.

The 34-year-old was in the JVA Essen prison on drugs and assault convictions.

Potato prospects this year are poor after the prolonged hot and dry weather in the summer, according to the government's statistics office Destatis.

"The 2026 potato harvest will be considerably more meager than the previous year, according to forecasts, which will have consequences for the prices of potatoes at various economic levels," Destatis wrote.

In September, Destatis said, potatoes were 11.3% more expensive than in the same month in the previous years. For frozen produce like chips/fries, the difference was less pronounced at 4.7%.

But these figures vastly outstripped both overall year-on-year inflation for September, 3.35%, and particularly the rate of inflation for food and drink products, 0.4%.

Destatis estimated that the 2026 harvest would be almost exactly one-quarter smaller than the previous year — partly because of fewer hectares being used to grow spuds but in larger part because of reduced average yields on the farmed soil in use.

Forecasts predict a yield of around 38.9 metric tons per hectare this year, compared to 46 tons per hectare in 2025.

A court in Hamburg has sentenced two men convicted of planning attacks in Germany on behalf of Iran's secret service.

One was given an eight-and-a-half-year prison sentence, while the other was ordered to spend four-and-a-half years behind bars on charges of espionage and attempted murder.

Among others, they were accused of plotting to attack Josef Schuster, the president of the Central Council of Jews in Germany and Volker Beck, a Green party politician and president of the German-Israeli society. Beck hailed the verdict online.

Choo-choo! All aboard the October 15 German rail news express, stopping wherever and whenever the conductor asks politely.

Ansbach train station in northwestern Bavaria, west of Nuremberg, has been named the finest in all the land.

The "Pro Rail Alliance" travel organization's special jury named Ansbach the Bahnhof des Jahres 2026, saying that after a renovation process lasting several years, it now "receives travelers in a light and friendly atmosphere."

They praised its well-lit waiting area, easy accessibilty by car, bike or bus, and ample storage for bicycles. They also noted its practical connection to the wider public transport network.

"Ansbach's bus station can be found right outside the train station building, and so can be reached with the greatest possible ease," the jury wrote.

If you're looking for a reason — other than the now alcohol-free train station, that is — to visit the town of roughly 40,000 people, you'll find it on the historic Castle Road from Heidelberg to Bayreuth. It's near the Frankenhöhe National Park, is home to the Ansbach Residence medieval castle complex, the picturesque Ansbach Court Gardens, an 18th century synagogue, and is also the site of the crypts of the Margrave noble family of Franconia.

The government's planned changes to emergency service response have cleared the lower house of parliament, the Bundestag.

One opposition party, the Greens, also supported the grand coalition's proposals, which are similar to plans that were under discussion in the previous government before it was cut short by early elections in 2025.

Health Minister Carsten Linnemann says the changes aim to ensure that patients receive care quicker, and in the right place.

Its main goal is to help ease the burden for paramedics and emergency first responders, by trying to prevent unncecessary calls to the 112 emergency hotline when a different service might be more appropriate.

Perhaps the main pillar of the plan is to directly network the emergency ambulance phone line 112, with the out-of-hours emergency doctor helpline 116 117 that is designed for less life-or-death medical cases.

Linnemann said the reforms were long overdue and that the reality was still that too many people did not really know who they were supposed to call when, and if in doubt would just call 112.

The reform package also envisages setting up integrated emergency centers, known by the German acronym INZ, that will be open around the clock and will bring doctors and hospital staff and other emergency responders together at one site.

Special emergency centers for children and adolescents will also be set up.

Alla hopp! Another day, another digest of the main news morsels on the menu in fair Deutschland, delectable and perhaps disgusting alike. Thanks for stopping by, Planet Earth.

If you're waiting for a train and frustrated by the delay — hardly an unthinkable scenario in modern Germany — on no account crack open a beer to wile away the time. Starting this Thursday, booze is banned at all of the country's train stations. It's part of a bid to improve behavior, fight littering and in extreme cases protect travelers and staff alike.

Various reform plans should start the parliamentary reading process in the Bundestag, after the Cabinet signed off on them in a Wednesday meeting. These include hikes to tobacco taxes, emergency service reforms, and changes to the federal law on support in education.

The Bundestag will also vote, in what's probably a formality, on extending the military peacekeeping deployments in the Red Sea and in South Sudan into 2027.

Left-leaning coalition negotiations are set to restart in Berlin after hitting a big bump in the road earlier this week.

Essen police are still searching for a prisoner who was violently freed from custody yesterday by two accomplices during a doctor's visit.

And the government stats office is warning of a meager potato harvest and increased prices after the scorching, dry summer.

We'll endeavor to keep you abreast of these stories and whatever else breaks as the day develops.

#426. Wagner brothers begin new NBA chapter on different paths

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

After five years, brothers Franz and Moritz Wagner are no longer playing together for the Orlando Magic. In the new NBA season, their separation will likely mean the new "German" team is the Charlotte Hornets.

In 2021, when Franz Wagner joined the Orlando Magic to start his NBA career, his older brother Moritz was already on the team. Franz, who is four years younger, was selected eighth overall in the draft by the team who had signed Moritz a few months earlier.

It's not just their joint appearances in the NBA that have since turned the "Wagners" into something of a brand in basketball. Just last year, the NBA had a regular-season game in Berlin, partly because of the Wagner brothers.

Moritz is a successful podcast host and often has his brother on the show. Both have their own Instagram accounts, each with around 300,000 followers. In 2024, the German public broadcaster ZDF aired a four-part TV documentary about the brothers, chronicling their basketball history and close relationship.

In recent years, they've also celebrated success with the German national team. In 2023, they became world champions together and were then at the Olympic Games in Paris a year later. In 2025, Franz also won the European Championship title, while Moritz, sidelined by injury, watched on as a TV analyst.

This summer, their time together came to an end. After Moritz wasn't offered a new contract in Orlando, he accepted an offer from the Brooklyn Nets. He now lives in New York with his girlfriend, Maggie McQuade, who works in marketing for the NBA.

"I've already spent a lot of time in New York. It's kind of like my home in America," Wagner said on his podcast, adding that it reminds him of where he's from. "As far as the US goes, Brooklyn is actually very close to Berlin."

The call from the Nets proved a stroke of luck. "When we hung up the phone and both agreed, we both cried. Because you kind of realize that this move feels right somehow," Wagner said. "We're just staying home."

The Nets are considered one of the weaker teams heading into the upcoming season, leaving Wagner a key force in providing stability and helping to develop younger players.

Franz is staying in Orlando. Alongside Paolo Banchero, Franz is one of the team's two stars. The 25-year-old is on a massive contract that runs through 2030 and will earn him a reported total of $224 million (€199 million).

With Sean Sweeney hired as their new head coach for the upcoming season, the Magic are hoping to finally advance past the first round of the playoffs, having lost at that stage in each of the last three seasons.

Success is expected to come from a faster style of basketball than has been played in Orlando in recent years. In the new system, Franz Wagner will take on the role of point guard more prominently than before — moving the ball up the court, creating his own shots, and driving to the basket with speed — much like he does for Germany.

"We want to play tough, play together, play it fast and enjoy playing together," Wagner said during a preseason interview. "We got a little bit more space for our guys, to get in the paint and make plays, for themselves and for others."

However, he now has to manage without his older brother.

"I think we're both a little sad his time here has come to an end," Franz Wagner said in an interview with sports news site ClutchPoints. "I think, though, we're both really grateful for the time we had [together]. To play with a sibling in the NBA for so many years is super special. So we're just grateful for that."

Now that the Wagners’ time together in Orlando is history, German basketball fans may have to adjust their allegiances a bit for the upcoming season.

While many Germans became fans of the Dallas Mavericks during the era of German basketball icon Dirk Nowitzki, and interest in the Orlando Magic has been particularly high in recent years, that interest may spread in the coming season. A total of eight Germans are under contract on an NBA team for the 2026/27 season:

While Hartenstein, who won the NBA championship with the Thunder in 2025, has another shot at the title, Hukporti, now in Philadelphia, tore his Achilles tendon in the first preseason game and will be out for the entire season. The Sixers have signed NBA legend LeBron James and are considered one of the title contenders.

Maxi Kleber, a reliable bench player for the past nine years, did not receive a new contract from the Los Angeles Lakers and is currently a free agent.

The Charlotte Hornets could then become Germany's new favorite NBA team, with Germany captain and NBA nomad Dennis Schröder now on the roster. In his 14th year in the NBA, the Hornets are already his 12th different team.

Playing alongside him will be two German rookies, highly-touted Hannes Steinbach and Christian Anderson Jr. The two newcomers know each other from the German U19 national team, with which they won the silver medal at the 2025 World Championship.

"I'm a veteran — I've been in the league for 14 years now, I've actually been with the team the longest — and of course I'm trying to guide these two young guys through their rookie season and make it as easy as possible for them,” Schröder recently said in an interview with RTL/Sky Sport.

"It's really cool to have someone like that here. Dennis, of course, has already played a lot of games in the NBA," Steinbach said. "You have someone there you can speak German with, who gives you a few tips based on what he sees during the game."

The Hornets are aiming to make the playoffs at the end of the season. Since they play in the Eastern Conference, just like Brooklyn and Orlando, there will be plenty of matchups against the other German players along the way.

#388. Football: German coach Hürzeler flying high in Premier League

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Fabian Hürzeler is younger than his star player, a fellow Premier League German. But, contrary to his reputation in his homeland, Hürzeler has become a popular figure both on and off the pitch in England.

"We are absolutely in love with him. He's been an incredible manager. The results are extraordinary and we're flying."

Those words from Ben Parker will be familiar to fans of any club who have been on the kind of winning run Brighton and Hove Albion are currently on.

But all the signs on and off the pitch suggest that the man in question — Brighton's German coach Fabian Hürzeler — is more than just a flash in the Premier League pan.

Hürzeler has never coached in Germany's Bundesliga. After winning promotion to Germany's top flight with St. Pauli in 2024, he left to become the youngest man ever to take charge in England's Premier League at 31. His team currently sit third, having won six games in a row including a 5-0 thrashing of bitter rivals Crystal Palace last weekend.

It was the repeat of a fixture last season that marked Hürzeler's lowest Brighton ebb, with a 1-0 home loss making it one win in 12. "There are only two options," the German coach said at the time. "Give up or keep pushing harder, and that's always the option I choose in my life."

Parker, the co-editor of Brighton and Hove Albion fanzine, Dogma, admitted to DW that there were plenty who weren't sure if persisting was the answer. "There was some disquiet at the end of that game, there were people shouting: 'You're getting sacked in the morning.'"

While such sentiment may be difficult to swallow for many coaches, Parker said that Hürzeler told him in an interview soon after joining the club that he welcomed it when needed.

"He talked about wanting a fan relationship that had a two-way aspect. The club and him are really ambitious, so if the performances aren't up to scratch, we should be telling him that. And that's what happened on that day. I see the whole thing as being quite healthy."

Hürzeler never played at the top level. He represented Germany's youth teams and turned out for Bayern Munich's reserves but soon grew frustrated with second-team football. He then accepted a player-coach role at German fifth-tier side Pipinsried at the age of 23. From 2018, he was also an assistant coach for Germany's U20s.

He performed this alongside a job at an art dealership in the German state of Bavaria, where he moved with his family at the age of two having been born in the United States. After starting as an assistant coach at St. Pauli in 2020, he took over after head coach Timo Schultz was fired in late 2022. Since then, he hasn't looked back.

That unusual path to top-flight management and some innovative tactics at St. Pauli led to a perception in Germany that Hürzeler was a little aloof and something of a 'laptop manager' in the vein of former Bayern Munich and Germany boss, Julian Nagelsmann who had a similarly nondescript playing career. This, so the charge goes, meant Hürzeler wasn't able to forge the relationships and togetherness needed to make a team more than the sum of its parts.

That was rarely what was reported by his players and staff at St. Pauli, nor is it Hürzeler's belief. "I want to be myself, I don't want to play a role, and my age definitely helps regarding understanding the players," he said.

"I always say I speak their language and I understand their thoughts, because it wasn't that long ago when I played."

His antics on the sideline also earned him a reputation as a bit of a hothead. Hürzeler was booked seven times during St. Pauli's promotion push and since joining Brighton, the German has been booked nine times and been sent off once. When asked about this, he said: "Emotions are part of the game."

Parker said this authenticity has also been reflected in his relationship with Brighton and Hove, on the south coast of England.

"For most Brighton fans he comes across as quite a quirky, interesting, left-field figure. The mantra he set out when he first arrived was quite counter-culturalist, to challenge the establishment, and that fits well here," Parker said.

"You will see him walking along on the seafront, going up for coffees around town, and he travels back and forth on an ebike, not in a chauffer-driven car as a lot of managers might. He's also hosted fan events in pubs, which I believe he paid for. So I'd say he's very approachable."

But Hürzeler isn't the only 30-something German beloved in Brighton.

Pascal Gross, who rejoined the club from Borussia Dortmund in January, is also a firm favorite. The midfielder, who retired from Germany duty after the World Cup earlier this year, has scored three and made four in the club's opening seven league games. The 35-year-old's connection with Hürzeler is also key, with Groß admitting he stayed in touch with Hürzeler even before he resigned. The return is proving to look a great decision for both sides.

"I was here for seven years, got married here and both my kids were born in England. The most important milestones in my life happened here. I missed my friends and the feeling of being deeply connected to the club."

The feeling is mutual, said Parker. "Pascal Gross is everybody's favorite player. He's Brighton's first best signing, from Ingolstadt, and second-best signing, from Dortmund. He's a sensational player, and it's probably true to say there is a lot of warmth towards German football and Germany because of these hugely important characters at the football club."

The Premier League club's success has been built on a smart coach and player recruitment strategy guided by local owner, Tony Bloom. Part of the model is that those who shine brightest will often be snatched by the bigger fish.

"I think he's said he's a supporter of Bayern Munich, and he uses them as an example of the standards he wants," said Parker of Hürzeler. "I can see that he could be a good fit in a couple of years time. He won't go to a Liverpool or a Chelsea, I think there's a strong pull to Bayern in particular."

Bayern's current boss, Vincent Kompany, is in little danger at the moment, though Bayern coaches are only ever a few losses from crisis. For the meantime, Hürzeler is also happy where he is, having extended his contract to 2029. Again, the feeling is mutual.

Next up in the Premier League is a trip to Liverpool, where Jürgen Klopp became the first German coach to make a real mark on the Premier League. But before that, Hürzeler's team have an assignment in the Europa Conference League, a competition Brighton are among the favorites to win. Parker hopes a first major trophy is in sight.

"If he can be the person that wins us that first trophy, then he will go down as the greatest."

#23. Africa's first Youth Olympics open in Senegal amid hope and scrutiny

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Senegal is hosting Africa's first Youth Olympics, with 2,700 young athletes competing in Dakar, Diamniadio and Saly. The Games bring pride and hope, but also questions over cost, rights and legacy.

On the final day of October, what may well be the final Summer Youth Olympics ever will begin. It will be the first on African soil as around 2,700 athletes aged 15-17 arrive in Senegal to compete across a total of 153 events in 25 sports.

Hosted in eight venues across three locations – Dakar, Diamniadio and Saly, 80 kilometers (50 miles) from the capital – these Games are seen by many as a prelude to Africa hosting a full Olympics one day in the future.

"I'm really excited about the Youth Olympic Games because, for the first time, I'll be representing our country," 15-year-old judoka Aisha Diop who is competing for Senegal told DW.

"I am very proud to be able to represent my country at these Games. This will really motivate young African athletes because they will improve, and they will want to continue playing sports now that the Youth Games have been held here."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The 2026 Youth Games in Senegal will also be a first beyond the history it is making in Africa.

"Of the 2,700 athletes who will be competing in Dakar, there will be a 50-50 split between men and women," Olivia Nloga, Chief Brand Promotion Officer on the organizing committee, told DW.

"But this parity isn't just reflected in the overall figure. This gender parity will also be reflected in each event and in each sport where such parity actually exists."

Several sports are also making their debut at the Youth Olympic Games, such as five-a-side baseball, kung fu and the Senegalese specialty of beach wrestling. There's also a refugee team at the Youth Olympic Games for the first time.

"As I said earlier, the Youth Olympic Games aren't just about sports, and they aren't just about infrastructure," said Nloga.

"For us, it was also important to use this time to build on and reinforce the idea that these Games in Senegal are a celebration for the people. We did this by investing in culture—for example, through a fashion show and a program called Yi-sol, which is a mural project on the city's walls."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Other projects designed to celebrate the people include music, and a local student designed the mascot. The biggest focus of all though, is on creating sustainability for the young people of Senegal.

"We did so through a program that is very important to us – one that is close to our hearts, namely, the Olympic Civic and Sports Certificate," Nloga said.

"We traveled to schools throughout Senegal and worked with more than 1,000 instructors to reach and make an impact on more than one million young Senegalese children who, during this hiatus, were exposed to and educated about the values of the Olympic Movement."

With recent flooding in the country, there were concerns that infrastructure would have been affected, but Nloga insisted that flood risk had long been in the planning.

"The prime minister held an interdepartmental meeting during which he established a task force to ensure that the necessary arrangements and follow-up measures are put in place to safeguard both the competition venues and the accommodation sites."

With Formula 1 confirming its return to Africa earlier in October, global sport is slowly becoming a more regular event in Africa. As a result though, there have been questions asked about the legacy of such events.

Many in Senegal concerned by the high cost of living and unemployment will see these Games as an unnecessary expense. For the Senegalese authorities though, they are an opportunity for long-term investment in the country's young people as well as infrastructure. There is also the hope that this will make Dakar a major tourist destination.

There was further criticism of the Games being in Senegal from Human Rights Watch (HRW), with the NGO calling on the Senegalese authorities to change their stance on the LGBTQ+ community.

"You are welcoming all of the world. It shouldn't be 'you are welcome to our country, except this, except that,'" Larissa Kojoué from HRW told DW.

"Anti-LGBT legislation and that crackdown that is going on, they contradict the Olympic movement, the Olympic Charter, and also various human rights convention that the country has signed."

Earlier this year, Senegal President Bassirou Diomaye Faye signed into law new legislation on same-sex relations that have proved contentious abroad despite broad political support within the majority Muslim country's parliament. Several human rights groups had voiced concerns about the bill, including warning that it would dissuade people at risk of illnesses like HIV/AIDS from seeking help.

The Games also see the return of Russians to Olympic competition under their own colours after the International Olympic Committee (IOC) lifted their suspension in July. Nevertheless, each sport's international federation can decide whether to authorize the Russian athletes' participation. World Athletics, for example, is upholding its ban, while judo and swimming have allowed Russians to return to competition with their flag and national anthem. According to the Russian Olympic Committee, 13 athletes will compete at the Youth Games in Senegal accompanied by 10 coaches.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite the various concerns, there is an overriding hope that the Games will be prove beneficial for all involved.

"What makes the Youth Olympic Games in Dakar special is that they are also rooted in Senegal's extremely strong sporting culture," Nloga said.

"Every evening, when you take a stroll along the Corniche in Dakar, you're struck by the number of people you see exercising, regardless of their age or physical condition. What we'll be offering during these Olympic Games is also an immersion into Senegalese life – into the way sports are just as much a part of Senegalese identity."

Sira Thierij conducted interviews for this article. Edited by: Chuck Penfold

#3511. Six concept vehicles from Stellantis exploring the future of car design

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

A Fiat that converts from a family car into a pickup and a Peugeot with a glass-bubble roof were among six concept cars unveiled by Stellantis at the 2026 Paris Motor Show, as captured in this video carousel by Dezeen.

Automotive corporation Stellantis unveiled the six concepts at a preview event in Meudon, outside Paris, on the eve of the motor show, offering an early look at the designs before the exhibition opened to the public.

The concepts explored different approaches to future car design – from reworking historic models to experimenting with colour-changing paint and illuminated bodywork.

Among the cars presented was Fiat’s Multiplay, which combines the proportions of a family vehicle with the functionality of a pickup.

Measuring 4.45 metres long, the concept has a sliding rear section that converts its six-seat-plus-one configuration into a four-seat vehicle with an open cargo bed.

Inside, a front passenger seat called the Sunseat folds into a lounge-style position beneath the panoramic roof, while a modular Petsenger seat can accommodate a pet, a child seat or storage.

The concept was finished in bright green, with a pink floor and red and pink upholstery inspired by the colours of a watermelon.

Citroën revisited its 1948 2CV with a compact electric concept featuring an orange-red body, grey steel wheels and yellow accents.

Inside, yellow tartan upholstery echoes details of the original model, while a fabric sunroof and removable storage basket replaced some of the conventional fittings found in modern cars.

The concept also features a detachable radio called Radioën, which doubles as a portable speaker, as well as door compartments designed to hold eggs, echoing the practical requirements of the original car.

Peugeot presented its RCZ Vision concept, a low-slung coupe with a sculpted body and a glass roof with a distinctive double-bubble profile that extends from the bonnet towards the rear.

Slim horizontal headlights reinterpret the brand’s three-claw motif, while a rear spoiler deploys only when the car is moving, keeping the bodywork smooth when parked.

Inside, a square steering control called Hypersquare replaces a conventional steering wheel and transparent micro-LED displays provide driving information while reducing the visual prominence of screens.

DS Automobiles’ F3VER concept features a compact body finished in Cosmic Blue paint that changes from vivid blue to violet depending on the light.

Its two-tone finish wraps around the rear of the car, while vertical Y-shaped headlights frame the front emblem.

At the rear, 16 LED panels sit within a sculptural light assembly informed by the vaulted roof of Paris’s Grand Palais.

Opel’s STX Concept is an electric estate car with a low roofline, sharply creased bonnet and sloping rear. Its front grille, known as the Vizor, incorporates vertical animated lights around an illuminated Opel emblem.

The rear bumper features extendable aerodynamic elements, while glass light strips run from the rear side windows into the roof.

Cameras replace conventional wing mirrors and three-section alloy wheels are designed to improve aerodynamic efficiency.

Alfa Romeo’s Cuorerosso concept car explores the brand’s future sports-car design through a streamlined body, active air intakes and slim aerodynamic mirrors.

Its rear window echoes the shape of the brand’s triangular front grille, while the interior is organised around the driver, with controls concealed in the central console until needed.

Stellantis was formed in 2021 through the merger of Fiat Chrysler Automobiles and France’s PSA Group.

Its portfolio includes Alfa Romeo, Citroën, DS Automobiles, Fiat, Opel and Peugeot, among other automotive brands.

This video carousel was produced for Stellantis as part of a partnership. Find out more about our partnership content here.

#2714. The Worst Advice I Ever Heard Was “Shut Up And Die Like a Man”

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Will Gadd (left) and his friend, the late Ken Wallator. (Photo: Will Gadd)

“Shut up and die like a man!” sounds like a line from a bad action movie, but we all heard it on the radio from the paragliding competition director. The director was yelling at a terrified pilot who thought he was falling to his death. The pilot had accidentally keyed open his radio’s shoulder mic, allowing his frantic screams to be broadcast across the competition frequency.

The unfortunate pilot begging to god to not die had collapsed his wing and plummeted in front of my eyes. Twenty-one years later, the memory of this sight and soundtrack is unforgettable, and so was the director’s barking, because there was clearly no sympathy for what the pilot thought was his final, horrific ride. Miraculously, the pilot recovered his wing and flew out to land and reflect on his life choices alone.

Most pilots didn’t think the competition director’s statement was out of line for the time. Many still don’t. I see terrible parallels between flying culture and some facets of climbing’s; in alpinism especially, some see death as an attractant, or an inevitability. Over my 40 years as an alpinist and 35 as a pilot, I’ve watched climbers experience gut-wrenching trauma—then do their best to ignore it, too scared or proud to deal with the ugly, messy truth. This avoidance sends a message to younger climbers, much like the competition director’s statement so long ago: Suck it up, move on, and die with honor if things go bad.

But living with trauma doesn’t have to be that way.

When my late friend Ken Wallator was 21, he climbed Mount Belanger in the Canadian Rockies with his girlfriend Heidi. They reached the top of the difficulties, unroped, and walked along a low-angle snow ridge to the summit of the peak. But the snow ridge was actually a massive, overhanging cornice of windblown snow, and it collapsed as they stopped to take a photo. Ken somehow managed to leap to safety but Heidi fell hundreds of meters down the face.

Ken, a brave bear of a man, downclimbed the face, found Heidi somehow still alive, and started carrying her out to a distant hut. She died before they reached it.

In the aftermath of Heidi’s death, I watched Ken receive a surprising amount of blame from grieving members of the climbing community. Didn’t he know better than to walk on a cornice? Why weren’t they roped up? Ken was already wracked with guilt from the accident. When his community piled on, their condemnation lit an internal firestorm.

Thirty years later, in 2019, Ken helped me re-roof my house. He still had the strength of a bear and I watched him haul 100-pound loads of shingles up my rickety ladder. In a rare moment of emotionality, he told me how he still hadn’t gotten over Heidi’s death, or the climbing community’s blame. We talked more about trauma, and I realized only then how that day on Mount Belanger broke my friend in a way that never healed.

As two young, hungry alpinists, Ken and I didn’t know what mental “help” looked like 30 years ago, and Ken was too much a product of his culture to take it 30 years later. Despite his immense physical and mental strength, Ken died by suicide a few weeks after our talk.

More recently, Will Stanhope’s death has brought to mind this idea of “asking for help” once more. For years Stanhope had battled desperately with alcohol. He was facing various charges at the time of his death—including assault and sexual assault—while still succeeding on difficult routes as a professional climber and mountain guide.

In our last real conversation, Stanhope talked about needing to “harden up and stop letting the carnage get to me” one minute, then sobbed about the “death camp” of hard alpine climbing the next. Like Ken and me, he had lost many friends in the mountains.

“You and I [watched] our mentors and friends die, one after another, as if everyone around us was climbing their way to oblivion,” his friend Kieran Brownie wrote in an obituary. “We couldn’t return to the mountains, we couldn’t return to each other.”

Stanhope carried a heavy weight in the final months of his life, made even heavier by grief, guilt, and irreversible mistakes. After a lifetime of hard solos and even scarier roped climbs, it seemed impossible that Stanhope would ever fall off an easy 5.11. But when he did just that last April and succumbed to a head injury, to me and other friends, Stanhope’s death felt more related to the weight he carried in his head than the climb itself. His family and friends made truly profound and sometimes wrenchingly difficult efforts to help him, but, like falling, the weights of addiction and trauma develop their own momentum.

Hard drinking, hard living, and celebrating dangerous acts—usually lauded as “bold”—are hallmarks of every mountain sport I’ve ever done, from kayaking to flying to skiing to climbing. Our literature is full of stories celebrating those who climbed hard, drank hard, and died silently.

I can’t help but wonder if our literature strives to make these deaths seem somehow valiant and necessary, like dying in the mountains is a noble sacrifice rather than the tragedy it really is. The classic mountaineering texts rarely explore the trauma that comes after seeing a friend die. They barrel ever onward to the summit.

Military veterans and first responders have a trauma-treatment framework that climbers should strive to adopt. Soldiers are not considered “weak” if they are affected by bloody combat. Paramedics are not ridiculed for taking leave after responding to a grisly car wreck. These are normal human responses, and professional groups typically have protocols for helping those involved in traumatic situations. These groups have no other choice; a traumatized team simply cannot function.

Those professional protocols weren’t there for Ken, Will, that terrified pilot, or many others, including me.

When I was 19 I tried with every tool I knew to save a fellow kayaker stuck just inches from the surface in his boat. I couldn’t reach him, and he drowned in front of me as I tried to remember how to rig a better haul system. I had nightmares for years of the paddler chasing me in the dark beside that river.

Only decades later did I seek help, after a divorce and a lot of behavior I understood even less than that terrible accident. I’m lucky I survived and sought belated help. Ken and Will and many others didn’t. Many more in our community—your friends and mine—are barely standing today, carrying crippling loads. They need help putting those loads down.

The pain of trauma does not disappear with six or 600 beers. Doing dangerous climbs or flights or other high-stimulus experiences temporarily distracts from the weight, but doesn’t solve it. There is no pack heavier than one filled with trauma you can’t put down.

One of the smartest things I’ve ever heard about trauma came after we failed to resuscitate a dying man at the base of an ice climb a few years ago. A professional responder on the scene could tell I was shaken, and looked me in the eye and said, “Post traumatic stress is normal; post traumatic stress disorder is optional—if you get help. Get help.”

I see real change today, from my wife Sarah Hueniken’s Mountain Muskox foundation to the American Alpine Club’s Grief Fund, two organizations built to support mountain-trauma survivors.

But I also still see a lot of the “Shut up and die like a man” attitude, and the celebration of “bold” yet physically unimpressive climbs that are often little more than Russian Roulette. Huge, low-angle alpine routes with immense serac and avalanche hazard are not that cool, in my book. And I see many climbers who still romanticize the wrong parts of climbing, whether that’s meaningless objective hazard or the very idea of “suffering silently.”

We need to recognize that a mountain death doesn’t end with the funeral; often that’s just the start of a very long and difficult process for everyone involved. In climbing more people die on the descent after the summit than on the climb itself.

At times I’ve felt deeply angry about our outdoor tribe’s celebration of “bold” hazards, and our denial of the after-effects of engaging in high-risk sports where participants die regularly. We often celebrate the bold free solos or dangerous alpine routes where everything clicked into place. We never celebrate the dead parent who doesn’t show up for their kids’ birthdays.

But the truth is that I laughed when the paragliding competition director said his infamous line. I promised myself that if I was in that situation in the future, I’d shut up. That became my normal. Today, I hope I’d speak up on the comment, and reach out to the guy who almost died that day. Because I’ve been him, and it sucks, and I’m trying to do better.

I’m less angry today when I think about Ken, Will, or others who have left the game, and just hope that our culture continues to evolve in the same way the first responders did, and that we ultimately celebrate survival more than dying silently. We can do better, if we want to.

Finally, if, like me, you’ve been through the trauma wringer, it often takes help to sort it out. We’re not tougher than the veterans or first responders even if our culture says we “should” be—we’re just less educated about intense trauma.

The goal is to return to full function and fly in life, not get stuck in the inevitable mental dust devils of mountain death. Peace to those hurting, it can get better with help. Substances and more dangerous living may distract us short term, but long term the combo is deadly despite the romantic words we historically place on both. I write these words for the future Wills and Kens, they are me, you, our friends.

Shutting up is not a solution, because silence is death.

#3310. Yankees Legend No Longer Feels Welcome by the Organization

#914. Aitor Throup Throws Down the Gauntlet with His New Exhibition “Ultra Veil”

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Thirty years into his career Aitor Throup says he is finally ready to be a successful fashion designer. Given this revelation, “Ultra Veil,” a new exhibition of his work opening this week at the Ambika P3 (a former concrete testing facility and now exhibition space at University of Westminster in London) can be read as a statement of intent.

Who is Aitor Throup and why should you care? He’s an Argentinian-British designer who has a cultish status within streetwear, having been scouted by C.P. Company on the day he graduated from the Royal College of Art in 1996 with an unforgettable collection titled When Football Hooligans Become Hindu Gods. His graduation collection merged his experience with racism as an immigrant in England and his attraction-slash-repulsion for the dandy style of the country’s sports agitators.

1996: Throup’s Royal College graduation collection: When Football Hooligans Become Hindu Gods

At C.P. Company, he would go on to design the kits that would be peacocked at the pitches. Recent projects have included a hookup with Umbro (with whom he created the uniforms of the British national team in 2009) and Maharishi. Up next in 2027 is a collaboration with Fred Perry which, is supporting “Ultra Veil,” along with The Westminster Menswear Archive (a treasure trove of 2000+ garments available for study).

Throup’s take on the (in)famous CP Company Goggle Jacket, beloved of football hooligans.

Throup is often described as an “insider’s designer” and it’s true that he operates on the fringes of fashion, as far as forward facing visibility goes. He didn’t grow up wanting to be a designer, rather he discovered in it a connection to other worlds, even those beyond day-to-day reality. Since childhood, Throup’s means of self expression has been drawing; in clothing he saw the possibilities in his illustrations come to life. As he put it on a recent call: “When I saw the first goggle jacket, it wasn’t about the person in the jacket. It was the fact that I could become that character. It was an extension. I could finally become Batman in many ways.”

There is a uniform (as in utilitarian/militaryish) feeling to many of the designs in “Ultra Veil.” That’s intentional and makes the fact that they’re “all constructed from lingerie, women’s lingerie mesh” more dramatic. Throup said he “vandalized” his designs by painting in 3D (i.e. on the body) with a liquid glitter rubber finish that took weeks for him to concoct. Asked to share examples of his design language he singles out “masks and veils integrated into hoods and hats (objects that have a ‘face’)”; integrated gloves; and trousers with “engineered transforming foot covers... thus my trousers have the immediate appearance of ‘legs.’”

Suspended designs in Throup’s 2025 exhibition “From the Moor.”

Suspended designs in Throup’s 2025 exhibition “From the Moor.”

What Throup shares with many other designers is the desire to achieve harmony between opposites, but with him this extends beyond aesthetics to a desired left brain/right brain that unites intuition with deep study of anatomy. In his world, ethereality is matched by a darker energy, to which 100 skull sculpturescontribute. The idea is that the 70 or so prototype garments in the exhibition will be sold like artworks.

“For the last 20 years, essentially what I’ve been trying to figure out is how does one breathe life into an inanimate object?” he said. Throup does not ascribe to the idea that a garment is completed by a body, but rather that a garment can be perfected as an object, and that all objects have anima (soul). “I would say that everything I create is anthropomorphic in the deepest sense. My work aims to capture the essence of humanity objectified into inanimate objects. Be it garments, sculptures, drawings, the aim is to capture the essence of life and in particular, human life.”

Pieces from Throup’s DSA (Daily Sketchbook Archive) Project was conceived as “merch” related to a larger to be launched main line.

For all these years the designer has supported himself mainly through consultancies in music and fashion, which allowed commerce to take a second seat to his theoretical practice. This designer proposes fashion as service, saying: “I instinctively felt I had something important and I felt a sense of service, like my work had a purpose…. I know this sounds big, but [I thought] my work would be in service to humanity in some way.”

If one were to assign a natural element to Throup it would be air, this despite the deep weightiness of his philosophies. His installations, like his drawings, capture moments of suspended animation. That temporary state of existence, floating, is an in-between state in which Throup seems to thrive. “The etymology of the word inspiration is pivotal in the point I’m trying to make,” he explained on a call. “Inspire” means in spirit; your breath being your spirit comes into your body. So when we are inspired, we are in our spirit. When we’ve expired, we’re out of our spirit. So to create something that’s inspiring and to be inspired is foundational.”’

A look from Throup’s Rite of Spring spring 2017 men’s collection.

With his homework done and the prospect of his own line, Aitor Ultra, on the horizon, Throup seems ready to come down to earth. While waiting for that to happen we’ll be caught in a suspended state of anticipation.

“Ultra Veil” will be open from October 30 to November 8.

#1162. <b><strong>What Is RED-S? Doctors Explain the Condition—and Why Women Are at Higher Risk</strong></b>

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

When it comes to exercise, there’s one thing that’s just as important as the training itself: fueling. Having the right nutrients—and enough calories—to sustain your activity and recover from it are critical for staying healthy, feeling your best, and keeping your body functioning in tip-top shape. After all, fueling your body isn't just about performing well today; it's about being able to keep doing what you love for years to come.

Eating can sometimes feel like an afterthought, especially when you’re busy with training, but consistently underfueling can actually result in a condition called relative energy deficiency in sport, or RED-S for short. And women have a much higher risk of developing RED-S compared to men. In a 2026 study in the Journal of Science and Medicine in Sport, researchers found that 70 percent of high-level female athletes were at risk of developing RED-S.

Top-ranked tennis player Emma Navarro recently revealed that she is one of those athletes dealing with the condition. Earlier this month, she posted on Instagram, detailing her experience, which included extreme mood swings, brain fog, a weakened immune system, constant headaches, and bouts of dizziness. Exercises she could do with ease in the past suddenly felt impossible. She was also hungry, all the time. “I had been under-fueling for almost three years,” she wrote. “My body was fighting back any way it knew how.”

But, you don’t need to be an elite-level tennis player to experience the condition. Here’s what you need to know about RED-S, including how to spot—and stop—it early, according to experts.

Meet the experts: Elizabeth Matzkin, MD, is an orthopedic surgeon and chief of women’s sports medicine at Brigham and Women’s Hospital. Amy West, MD, is a sports medicine physician at Northwell Orthopedic Institute. Amy Stephens, RD, is a sports dietitian who specializes in eating disorders.

There’s a simple explanation behind what causes RED-S: It occurs when someone is not eating enough to meet the demands of their training—or even their body’s basic physiologic needs, says Elizabeth Matzkin, MD, an orthopedic surgeon and chief of women’s sports medicine at Brigham and Women’s Hospital. Your body needs energy not only to sustain you, say, on a run or in a weightlifting session, but it also needs energy to support bone health, hormone production, metabolism, immune function, and cardiovascular health.

When you’re consistently not consuming enough food to support your activity levels, over time, your body won’t have the energy it needs to support those critical functions, leading to a wide range of symptoms that define RED-S—which is more than medical jargon for undereating. It reflects what happens when your body is chronically underfueled and can lead to long-term health issues.

Anyone, from the elite athlete to someone training for their first half-marathon to the person increasing their lifting sessions in the gym while trying to lose weight, can be at risk of developing RED-S. Remember, the “r” in RED-S stands for relative; so it’s all about not meeting your energy needs, whatever they are.

When underfueling, in the short term, you may experience fatigue, impaired concentration, more frequent illnesses, poor recovery, and irregular periods or none at all, Dr. Matzkin says. When it comes to your sport, training and performance may suffer too. Low energy availability can make it harder to sustain a workout, decrease strength and endurance, or lead to muscle weakness or pain, says Amy West, MD, a sports medicine physician at Northwell Orthopedic Institute.

You’ll also be much more susceptible to injuries, particularly when it comes to your bones. This has impacts on the long term: “Adolescence and young adulthood are critical years for building peak bone mass, so prolonged low energy availability during this period may have consequences later in life,” Dr. Matzkin says. Down the line, that could mean osteoporosis. Meanwhile, missing your period for long periods of time can have impacts on future fertility, Dr. West adds.

Early stages of RED-S can happen pretty quickly, says Amy Stephens, RD, a sports dietitian who specializes in eating disorders. After just one day of underfueling, you’ll feel symptoms of “hanger”; after just a week, your body may stop building muscle and endurance. Then, she says, it could take six to nine months to fully recover.

RED-S is much more associated with women and girls compared to boys and men. Until 2014, RED-S was referred to as the “female athlete triad,” which highlighted the relationship between undereating, menstrual dysfunction, and decreased bone mineral density. While RED-S can happen to anyone, “female athletes are more vulnerable, especially in sports that emphasize leanness, endurance, aesthetics, or weight categories,” Dr. Matzkin says. (Think: Running, gymnastics, and diving.)

But, that’s not the only reason women may deal with RED-S more than men. There’s also the pressures that women face disproportionately to be thin, says Dr. West, and the fact that women are more likely to have eating disorders.

RED-S doesn’t happen all at once. Earlier, more subtle signs like fatigue and trouble sleeping pop up when a person has what docs call low energy availability, which occurs when they’re not eating enough to support their activity levels. As someone is continuously in a state of low energy availability, RED-S can occur.

Low energy availability and RED-S can affect almost every system in the body, but these are the areas you may most often see symptoms, according to the doctors.

If RED-S is suddenly on your radar now, don’t worry. You don’t need to completely overhaul your life or your training to avoid it. In fact, there are a few intuitive steps you can take to give yourself a good shot at fueling fully—no calorie tracking necessary. Here’s what you can do.

It’s so simple, but occasionally taking stock of how you’re feeling in and out of training can go a long way. The symptoms above are warning signs of not eating enough, but you should also know what a fully-fueled athlete feels like too. A well-fueled athlete has consistent energy all day, mostly only feels hungry around mealtimes, and doesn’t deal with brain fog, Stephens says. Their mood is stable, they recover well between workouts, and they sleep well too. It’s a good idea to check in and reflect on how you’ve been feeling lately—strong and energized and able to “kick” at the end of a workout, or sluggish and hungry, struggling to get in those last few reps.

Plus, if you wanted to dig even deeper, you can also get some more information from an annual blood workup with your doctor. Since RED-S affects so many systems in the body, Stephens says, you might see issues pop up across a range of tests, including your thyroid, white blood cell count, ferritin and iron, vitamin D, or complete blood count. If any of those are out of range, it could be due to underfueling, but they would need to be discussed with your doctor in the context of the rest of your health history and any other testing.

You don’t need to download a macro-tracking app or suddenly start counting calories to avoid RED-S. (For the record: Stephens doesn’t have her athletes do that.) Instead, focus simply on getting three meals per day, with your plate being about one-third protein, one-third carbs, and one-third vegetables each time. Most people don’t eat breakfast, Stephens says, and that’s often one of the quickest culprits when it comes to not having enough food in a day.

This is another simple reminder, but if you’re someone with a training plan on the horizon, keep in mind that what you’re eating now is not what you’ll be eating in the future, Stephens says. As plans progress and get more and more intense (you’re running longer distances or lifting heavier in the gym or simply adding another day of Solidcore per week), you’ll need to increase the amount of food you eat.

One helpful rule of thumb is that for every hour you spend working out, you should add on an extra 500 to 600 calories to your day, whether that’s with the help of snacks or larger serving sizes at meals, Stephens says. Then, anything in the 2-to-3-hour range will require in-session fueling, like gels on a long run.

If you’re noticing any of the above symptoms or suspect that you’re not fueling right, it’s a good idea to see a professional. Earlier signs like fatigue and poor sleep are easy to write off, but checking in and making sure they’re not a result of underfueling can keep low energy availability from developing into a full-on case of RED-S. In addition to setting an appointment with a doc, you can also try increasing your food intake by incorporating more snacks throughout your day or having larger portion sizes at meals. If you’re not already having a carb-rich snack before a workout, try adding that for some extra energy, paired with something high in protein and carbs afterward to support recovery.

Just keep in mind: It’s never too early to bring this up with a doctor or dietitian, whether you want help determining the appropriate calorie and macro goals for your workout demands or for them to evaluate symptoms you’re not so sure about. “Earlier evaluation is better,” Dr. Matzkin says. “We don't want to wait for a stress fracture—this means bone health has already been affected.” Ideally, you’ll talk with a doctor before reaching this point, but two symptoms that should send you to their office ASAP are frequent injuries, especially if you’re having a tough time recovering from them, and period irregularities, Dr. West says.

From there, treating RED-S is all about getting your body back to the right balance of energy intake to energy output and managing any injuries that may have arisen. You’ll likely need to work with a doctor and/or dietitian to help you get enough calories and nutrients and reduce your training demands while you get caught up. In addition to a sports medicine doc and/or dietitian, you may also work with a bone endocrine specialist (if your bone health has been affected) and/or a sports psychologist, Dr. Matzkin says.

Whether you are at risk for RED-S or not, consider this your reminder that fueling is critical for any active person. Being thoughtful about getting enough calories, carbs, and fat for energy, and protein for recovery is your best line of defense against the condition. While everyone’s needs will be different, a rule of thumb, per Dr. West: “Athletes often need more food than they think.”

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.
