#1128. The 30 Best Halloween TV Episodes of All Time

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

There is no shortage of sitcoms, superheroes, or Snoopy to binge before trick-or-treating.

Fall TV died a slow and painful death many years ago. But that doesn’t mean we’ve all lost the plot on Halloween. Even in the streaming era, television shows still know when to drop the occasional Halloween episode, wherein familiar fictional characters dress up as other familiar fictional characters. So now is a great time to throw on a few favorites while you put together your last-minute costume. (You do have a costume, right?)

If horror movies, even the not so-scary ones, aren’t the vibe you’re looking for, let good ol’ TV help set the tone for All Hallows' Eve. From classic shows like The Brady Bunch to more modern favorites like Abbott Elementary, there is no shortage of great Halloween-themed episodes to binge before November 1. In the spirit of Halloween, Esquire’s entertainment editors spent some time digging through our favorites to settle on the best 30 Halloween episodes ever.

Grab some candy corn, put on your coziest jack-o'-lantern socks, and settle on the couch. These are the best Halloween-themed episodes of TV ever made.

Ted and John’s annual tradition of throwing eggs at bad Halloween costumes is thwarted when Blaire ropes Ted into being her designated driver for a party. Between Blaire’s impressive Catwoman costume, a killer gag referencing the Milgram Experiment, and a surprisingly moving story about seizing the day, Ted somehow has one of the best Halloween episodes in the entire streaming era.

One of the most popular episodes of Bob’s Burgers is this Halloween episode from season 6. When Louise claims she’s never been frightened by anything, the family tries to scare her good with their own haunted house. Just as the kids are ready to write off their parents’ lame efforts, the house seems to come alive. Stay for the credits to hear the hot new single from Boyz 4 Now, “I Love You So Much (It’s Scary).”

“Home” is one of the darkest episodes in all of The X-Files, which is really saying something. Originally aired in October 1996, the episode sees Scully and Mulder investigate a secluded farm home in Pennsylvania. While the episode is basically a tribute to American horror classics like The Texas Chain Saw Massacre, “Home” delivers its own hair-raising scares in the Peacocks, a family of violent inbreds. Though The X-Files was limited in what it could show onscreen due to broadcast restrictions, its suggestive nature lends the episode its pitch-black power.

For quintessential ‘90s sitcom antics, look no further than Home Improvement. The first Halloween episode of the series mostly centers on Brad being in his first-ever fight with his girlfriend, which only Tim can help him sort out. But the Halloween stuff happens in the subplot, where Tim and Al build a haunted house to scare the daylights out of one smug kid in particular.

Zoinks! This immaculate crossover from Supernatural’s thirteenth season feels ready-made for an All Hallows’ Eve marathon. (Never mind that it aired in March 2018.) In “Scoobynatural,” Sam, Dean, and Castiel get sucked into a television set and crash the Scooby-Doo, Where Are You? episode “A Night of Fright Is No Delight.” The Scooby Gang has met a lot of pop culture legends—Dick Van Dyke, Batman, John Cena—but their outing with the Supernatural guys marks the first time they ever tangled with genuine mystical evil.

Decades of Halloween parties are our way into Mr. Peanutbutter’s psyche and patterns of problematic behavior. In this season 5 episode of BoJack Horseman, the annoyingly upbeat Mr. Peanutbutter throws his annual Halloween party at BoJack’s place, which happens to be ground zero for his biggest relationships coming apart. Set against four different Halloween parties—in 1993, 2004, 2009, and 2018—audiences find new angles to a staple character we thought we had figured out.

South Park has been on the air long enough to have several Halloween episodes, but this season 1 hit is still the best. After Kenny is killed (again), he’s brought back to life as a zombie, whose condition is mistaken for a severe case of pinkeye. As “pinkeye” spreads through South Park, the boys try to celebrate Halloween with some very questionable costumes.

In Abbott Elementary, Halloween festivities descend into madness when a stolen bag of candy turns all the kids into hyperactive, sugar-infused monsters straight out of 28 Days Later. As the teachers try to corral the student body, Janine stresses over a Halloween party invitation. The episode caps off with a perfect Avengers: Infinity War reference.

This season 5 episode of Boy Meets World is technically not a Halloween special at all. It premiered in February 1998. But when teen slashers like Scream and I Know What You Did Last Summer were ruling the box office, Boy Meets World got in on the craze with a surprisingly emotional episode that interrogates Shawn’s guilt over the breakup of Cory and Topanga. During detention, the characters get locked inside the school with a masked killer on the loose.

After the Brady boys scare the girls with a fake ghost, the girls strike back with a taste of their own medicine. “Fright Night” is classic Brady Bunch antics, as the whole household gets in on the fun. Never mind that at this point in season 4, Greg and Marcia look old enough to be at parties instead of coming up with pranks.

It's Halloween at Cloud 9, which is an oddly fitting time for them to find a dead body in the store. A creepy employee, likely peeping in on the women's restroom, apparently got stuck and starved to death in the walls. With hours to spend before the coroner can get the body, everyone at Cloud 9, employees and shoppers alike, must go about their day as a rotten corpse watches over them all.

The Paddy's gang experiences a real scream when Dee gets pregnant, and everyone retraces their steps to figure out what the hell happened at their Halloween party. Leave it to It's Always Sunny in Philadelphia to turn their Halloween episode into a Rashomon scenario.

Before Deadpool, Ryan Reynolds was a sitcom star in the forgotten but outrageously funny Two Guys, a Girl, and a Pizza Place. In the show's first Halloween episode from season 2, it breaks all its rules by unleashing an evil doppelgänger of Reynolds' Berg who hacks his way through the group. Unfortunately, you cannot stream Two Guys, a Girl, and a Pizza Place on any platform in the U.S. But the heroes over at Shout! Studios produced a DVD box set that deserves a spot on your shelf.

When Ben starts living with Andy and April, he has to put up with their (unannounced) Halloween party. But while most of the episode follows conventional All Hallows hijinks, the real attraction is the cold open where Andy and April talk about “blood orphans” that sealed it as a classic. Would it surprise you to know that Widow’s Bay creator Katie Dippold wrote this one?

You can make the reasonable argument that It's the Great Pumpkin, Charlie Brown is still the greatest Halloween special ever made. It gave us Snoopy’s World War I battle with the Red Baron, Lucy and the football, “I got a rock,” and so much more. Honestly, without Great Pumpkin, we wouldn't be here making this list at all.

Just days before The Walking Dead premiered and became a cable TV monster, Community spoofed zombie movies with the hilarious and memorable “Epidemiology.” At the shockingly well-decorated Greendale Halloween party, some bad mystery meat turns the partygoers into grotesque husks with a taste for flesh. The episode peaks in the climax, when Troy reclaims his impressive Aliens loader cosplay to save his friends. He fails, but it’s still a cool moment.

All of Buffy the Vampire Slayer is tailor-made for Halloween. But in season 2, the cult series delivered a banger with the simply-titled episode, “Halloween.” When Buffy and the gang buy cursed costumes, they spend Halloween night believing they are their costumed alter egos—including Buffy, whose badass slayer skills are neutered when she believes she’s a dainty noblewoman from the 18th century.

If you think this Power Rangers Zeo episode has the mighty morphin’ heroes clashing with a pumpkin monster or something, think again. In this Halloween special from the 1996 iteration of the show, the Green Ranger, Adam (Johnny Yong Bosch), falls asleep while watching a horror movie marathon. He then wakes up in a black-and-white world of vampires, werewolves, and mummies. Here, the Power Rangers pay tribute to the Universal Monsters, plus a few karate kicks.

One year after Stranger Things became a Netflix juggernaut, it returned for its blockbuster second season. The premiere takes place during the greatest day of all: Halloween. While it is just the first chapter of another long and terrifying ordeal with the Upside Down, “Trick or Treat, Freak” is full of hijinks and house parties, and some of the sickest Ghostbusters costumes I’ve ever seen.

You know what's scarier than a ghost? Firing someone. In the first Halloween-themed episode of The Office, Michael Scott is forced by corporate to fire one of his employees before EOD. While later episodes of The Office would crank up the volume on the outrageousness—including a wildly controversial cold open from season 6 that was pulled from DVD and streaming—“Halloween” represents exactly the sort of banal black comedy that made the series so great in the first place.

From its perfect cold open to Jake proposing to Amy, “HalloVeen” takes the belt (sorry, cumberbund) as not just one of the best episodes of Brooklyn Nine-Nine but among the best Halloween episodes in any TV show. It’s the fifth annual Halloween Heist at the Ninety-Ninth Precinct, but this time, the stakes are higher than ever. Normally, the Halloween episodes of Brooklyn Nine-Nine are skippable filler, but given how this one cements Jake and Amy’s relationship in stone, fans can’t miss this one.

Friends might have some great Christmas and Thanksgiving episodes, but season 8's “The One with the Halloween Party” tops them all. As the pals throw a costume party in their improbably large New York City apartment, Phoebe runs into her twin sister, Ursula, and her fiancé (played by guest star Sean Penn). Peep some corporate synergy, as this Warner Bros.-produced sitcom dressed up Monica and Phoebe as Catwoman and Supergirl.

This show aired for just one season, so it has only one Halloween episode. But it’s a great one. Lindsay thinks she’s too cool to get into Halloween, but her mom wants her to stay home and pass out candy to trick-or-treaters. Meanwhile, we get some pretty solid costumes (“I’m not a girl, I’m a... Bionic Woman!”) from Sam, Bill, and Neal, who take to the streets for one last childhood All Hallows’ Eve.

Only Larry David would accuse trick-or-treaters of rigging the system. In this episode from season 2, Larry gets irritated after some older kids end up at his doorstep. But he gets what's coming to him when he wakes the next morning to find his backyard vandalized. “I didn't know there was going to be felony or treat!”

What starts as a pretty normal Halloween episode becomes a love letter to filmmaker Alfred Hitchcock. In this season 3 episode of That ‘70s Show, the guys feel stuck between childhood and adulthood while watching a Hitchcock movie marathon on Halloween. But when they suspect that Bob has murdered Midge, they spring into action, Rear Window style.

You can spend all of Halloween watching every Treehouse of Horror special from The Simpsons. There are dozens of them. Literally. But “Halloween of Horror” from season 27—the first time that The Simpsons ever did a “normal” Halloween episode—is a strong episode that uses the season to explore real emotions like fear and bravery. After a traumatic night at Krustyland, Lisa has a hard time getting over her new fear of everything.

It’s Halloween in New York City, and what’s Ted Mosby doing? Waiting for the Slutty Pumpkin. This season 1 episode of How I Met Your Mother playfully rips off Charlie Brown as Ted, dressed in a comically dated costume, waits for the mysterious girl who got away all those years ago at a Halloween party. Years later in season 7, Ted finally closed the book on the Slutty Pumpkin when she surfaced at last, and was played by guest star Katie Holmes.

In the first Halloween special for Modern Family, Claire tries to claim Halloween as her holiday (after Gloria had seized on Christmas and Thanksgiving) by creating the greatest haunted house in the neighborhood. While the episode is primarily concerned with Claire opening up about feeling sidelined in holiday traditions, this season 2 episode soars when Mitchell, dressed as Spider-Man, tries to avoid embarrassment at work... by climbing out of the building.

When the Marvel Cinematic Universe returned for Phase Four, it did so with WandaVision, the franchise's first Disney+ series. A tribute to TV sitcoms, “All-New Halloween Spooktacular!” grounded the show in the late ‘90s and early ‘00s with a Malcolm in the Middle-style episode where Uncle Pietro (guest star Evan Peters, whose appearance bridges the X-Men franchise to the MCU) comes to hang out on Halloween. Peep Elizabeth Olsen and Paul Bettany donning their characters' original costumes from the comics.

New Girl knew how to have fun on Halloween, and in season 2, it used the spooky season to interrogate the scariest thing in dating: situationships. When Jess takes a job working as a zombie at a haunted house, her not-that-serious boyfriend Sam comes to see her—but Nick finds out just how detached he is from her. Meanwhile, Schmidt and Cece wind up with an “accidental” couples costume.

MobLand’s Frankie Cooper Is the Best Villain on TV

#3286. This 33-Song Playlist Is What Jess McClain Uses to Train for Crushing Marathons

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

For the American course record holder from the 2026 Boston Marathon—who is about to take on the New York City Marathon—music is an integral part of training.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Good music can often make the difference between a rave run and a blown goal. That’s why we’re putting together 90-minute playlists each month chock-full of songs with enough of a beat to keep you moving. For even more tunes, check out our list of The Best Running Playlists to Power Every Pace and Distance.

Jess McClain learned during her first stint as a professional runner that too much of a good thing and singular focus can turn bad. The Arizona native ran competitively in high school, mostly racing 5K and 10K races, and later at Stanford University, where she became a seven-time NCAA Division I All-American. In 2015, however, she went pro, signed with Brooks, and that’s when things took a turn for the worse.

“Through high school and college, running was just so fun; I had other things going on at all times: extracurriculars, stuff with friends on the weekends, football games, and clubs,” she says. “With professional running, I was so excited to go all in, but I realized very quickly I’m not one-dimensional. It’s really hard for me not to have other buckets to pour myself into.”

Back in the 2010s, pros were mostly all about running, and not as much about side hustles and other hobbies. “This is the way it has to be if I want to go all in: I have to live, train, breathe, eat, and sleep running,” McClain says. That all-or-nothing mindset led to repeated injuries and burnout, which forced McClain to part ways with her sponsor in 2018 and, at 26, move back to Phoenix with her parents.

Over the next few years, McClain took a full-time marketing job and got married. “It was a good reset for me,” McClain says. “It gave me a lot of perspective and helped me detach my identity from running. I’d been running competitively since I was 12, so I really didn’t know who I was or what I wanted outside of that. I needed self-discovery time to find out who Jess is when she’s not racing.”

Just when she started considering a return to running, the pandemic shut down racing. But then, in 2022, right before her 30th birthday, McClain raced the Mesa Marathon, her first 26.2 miles, on a lark, training only six weeks before the big day, and finishing in 2:33:34. She qualified for the 2024 Olympic Marathon Trials, where she placed fourth. At the 2024 New York City Marathon and 2025 Boston Marathon, she scooped up top 10 placements. And at the 2026 Boston Marathon, McClain broke the American course record with a time of 2:20:49.

She returned to her sponsor, Brooks. This go-round feels different, she says. “I’ve just had so much fun,” she says. “It took that reset and step back to rediscover why I love racing and getting the best out of myself, but I’m able to do everything else in life to some extent, too. It took a minute to find my recipe, but I did.”

Part of reclaiming her love of running came through music, something that’s been with her since her early days of running. “I remember having the clunky MP3 player and the old headphones that if you moved them a certain way, the music would scratch,” she says, laughing. “Now I am somebody who likes music and podcasts, definitely on my easy runs or solo runs.” Sometimes she’ll put her phone in the back pocket of her sports bra and play music aloud during track workouts for an extra boost.

“I’m a Swiftie for sure—I grew up with her, and we’re the same age, so it’s a nostalgic thing,” she says, “but I love the teenage angst in ‘90s alternative punk rock.” She’s recently been listening to Taking Back Sunday, My Chemical Romance, Matchbox Twenty, and Papa Roach as well.

Runner’s World asked McClain to guest-DJ a running playlist, and she’s put together quite the treat. Her playlist, of course, includes plenty of Taylor Swift, but also some blink-182, Hoodie Allen, and The Veronicas to give it a bit of edge. It may just be the boost you need as well!

#2727. The Overlooked Muscle You Need to Strengthen For Better Knee Health This Ski Season

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Including eccentric work in your exercise routine is essential to making sure your muscles are balanced. (Photo: Tory Powers)

Strong legs are essential for skiing without injury. But many traditional strength training programs focus on the bigger muscles in your lower half, including the quads, hamstrings, and gluteus maximus (your largest butt muscle).

That means the smaller guys often get neglected. And one small guy in particular–the gluteus medius, which forms part of your side butt–is worth paying attention to if you actually want to reduce your risk of knee pain and injury this winter.

Because ACL tears are among the most common ski injuries, Dr. Gregory Lichtman, an orthopedic surgeon in northern California and a physician for the U.S. women’s ski team, puts heavy emphasis on strengthening the glute medius. In his view, it’s one of the most important muscles for staying safe and skiing well.

Unfortunately, even high-level athletes can have a weak glute medius, according to Lichtman. That’s because unlike the quads, hamstrings, calves, and glute max–which can naturally get strong from activities like road running and hill climbing–the glute medius typically needs to be intentionally strengthened.

Read on to learn why this small muscle is so important, Lichtman’s favorite way to strengthen it, and other essential fitness tips for injury prevention.

The glute medius is a small muscle on your outer hip that fires up when you bring your leg out to the side, a motion known as hip abduction. It also helps control the knee by preventing it from caving inward (a position known as valgus) as you perform various movements. And it assists you in balancing on one leg, which is important when loading your downhill ski, Lichtman says.

In skiing, if you get into the back seat (meaning, you shift your body weight backwards, instead of staying centered over your skis) and the knee goes into valgus, the tibia can internally rotate, “and that’s where the ACL tears occur,” Lichtman says. Even if you don’t get a full-blown ACL tear, going into valgus when you ski can contribute to patellofemoral pain syndrome, a fancy term for pain at the front of the kneecap, Lichtman says.

By strengthening the glute medius, you can make it more resistant to valgus in the first place, which decreases your chances of both ACL tears and general knee pain.

There are also performance benefits: “A strong gluteus medius also really allows you to stand on your downhill ski a little bit more aggressively and really make a more aggressive turn,” Lichtman says. That’s because when you place most of your weight on your downhill ski (the one that’s closest to the bottom of the hill), your G-forces will want to throw you down the mountain. But having a strong glute medius–in addition to strong quads and core muscles–helps form a strong pillar on your outer hip, which ”really allows you to withstand those G forces the best,” Lichtman says.

One of the classic ways to target the glute medius is to strap on ankle weights, lie on your side, and lift and lower your top leg. Lichtman says. This exercise can absolutely strengthen your glute medius, but it’s not Lichtman’s go-to. That’s because it primarily just isolates that muscle and doesn’t mimic how we move in sports.

A better bet, Lichtman says, is to focus on exercises that simultaneously activate the gluteus medius and other muscle groups because that better resembles how we use this muscle during athletics. His favorite exercise that ticks that box? The monster walk. Not only does this move really fire up the gluteus medius, but it also works the core, hamstrings, quads, and glute max, he says.

Incorporate this exercise into your workouts three days a week, with at least one day off in between each session so that you give the muscle the downtime it needs to recover and build back stronger. To stave off boredom–and also work different parts of your hips and quads–incorporate different variations of the monster walk throughout the week. For example, instead of stepping straight out to the side, step forwards and backwards, or diagonally forward and diagonally backwards. As long as you have good form and keep your legs spread apart so that you feel tension from the band the whole time, “all directions can be effective,” Lichtman says.

Lichtman suggests doing monster walks during both pre-season and throughout ski season. “One of the big pitfalls is that we will all get ready for ski season, and then taper off our dry land training,” he explains. “If you’re one of the fortunate few who can ski five or six days a week, you’re probably getting enough exercise, and you probably don’t want to stress your body out any more than that. But for those of us who are skiing two to three days a week, finding some time to do some cross-training and continue to work on our core and our hip abductor strength is really important because those are the less commonly used muscles during skiing.”

Once this exercise becomes easy, level up the challenge by using a stronger resistance band.

A strong glute medius can keep you safe on the slopes. But it’s not the only fitness-related aspect of injury prevention.

Having a strong core and quads, along with good overall endurance, is also key. That’s because one of the essential components of an ACL tear pattern in skiing is getting kicked into the back seat. And while the gluteus medius helps guard against knee valgus in this position, it doesn’t prevent you from getting into the back seat in the first place, Lichtman explains. What does help? Having strong core and quad muscles, plus overall endurance. This trifecta, Lichtman says, is “fundamental” to staying out of the back seat.

On the endurance front, the goal is to be as fresh after lunch as you are after breakfast, Lichtman says. “Fatigue is definitely a risk factor for ACL tears,” he says, explaining that fatigued skiers more frequently get into the back seat and sustain injuries than those who aren’t zapped. So make sure your dryland training doesn’t just focus on lifting weights: “A balance of endurance and strength training is integral for a skier preparing for ski season,” Lichtman says.

#1012. The Most Consequential Ballot Measures for the 2026 Midterm Elections

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

A week before voters hit the polls, we break down everything you need to know

This November 3, voters will head to the ballots for the midterm elections. In addition to choosing the people who will represent them in office, they will also be making important decisions about policies that will shape everything from abortion rights to income taxes.

Nearly 150 statewide measures will be determined on the ballot during this year’s midterm elections. Key issues that have been dominating the news cycle—including voter ID registration, transgender rights, and affirmative-action laws—will be reflected in these initiatives, with the outcome affecting the lives of millions of people.

With just a week before Election Day, we’ve put together a guide to some of the ballot measures up for discussion in this year’s election. Keep scrolling to learn more.

As income inequality in the U.S. continues to worsen, some voters will get to decide whether or not to impose an income tax on their state’s richest residents.

In California, Proposition 40—colloquially known as the “billionaire tax”—would enact a one-time 5 percent emergency tax on any resident whose net worth exceeds $1 billion. Revenue from the tax would be used to fund state health care, education, and food-assistance programs. It is estimated that it would raise about $100 billion in five years, according to the health-care workers union that helped put the measure on the ballot.

Naturally, the measure is facing pushback from the state’s powerful and wealthy. California governor Gavin Newsom has publicly opposed Proposition 40, joining detractors who argue that the state relies on these billionaires and that such a tax would push them out. Opponents have even placed two rival measures on the same ballot: Proposition 41, which requires audits of new tax programs, meaning that Proposition 40 would automatically violate the provision since it wasn’t designed to follow the state’s annual spending limit; and Proposition 42, which blocks taxes based on the ownership of personal property, including financial accounts, investments, and art collections, which are the types of properties that Proposition 40 specifically targets. If either or both of those initiatives pass, then the passage of Proposition 40 will be invalidated. Among Propositions 41 and 42’s proponents is Google cofounder Sergey Brin, who has an estimated net worth of $242 billion and is one of the richest people in the world. He has reportedly spent $138 million on the campaigns for Propositions 41 and 42—or about 0.057 percent of his total net worth.“It’s very disappointing that they’d go to these lengths and spend all of this money when really they could do the moral and ethical thing of being part of our social contract,” Suzanne Jimenez, chief of staff for SEIU-UHW, told LA Public Press. “They could pay a very minimal tax that, quite frankly, folks like Sergey Brin are going to make up in mere months after they would have to pay this tax.”

Four years after the Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade, voters are continuing to determine the fate of abortion rights on the ballot.

In Idaho, a vote “yes” on Proposition 1—also known as the Reproductive Freedom and Privacy Act Initiative—will establish the right to abortion across the state. Currently, Idaho prohibits abortions except in the case of medical emergencies that will save the life of the pregnant person or in cases of rape or incest.

Voters in Virginia and Nevada will decide on constitutional amendments for access to abortion and reproductive freedom. In Missouri, voters will decide on Amendment 3, which would repeal an amendment that Missourians previously passed in 2024 that enshrined the right to an abortion in the state constitution.

Six ballot measures in five states will shape the rights of transgender people.

Both Propositions 134 and 135 in Colorado affect the lives of the state’s transgender residents. Proposition 134 would require students in all public schools, from kindergarten to college, to play on athletic teams that align with their assigned sex at birth. Voters in Arizona, Washington, and Nebraska will also decide on initiatives that could potentially prohibit trans students from participating in school sports teams.

It’s unsurprising that some states are attempting to litigate the freedom of trans student athletes considering that, in June, the Supreme Court upheld a ban on transgender girls participating in school sports. Opponents of these initiatives argue that these laws would isolate the transgender community, as well as undermine the decision-making ability of parents and schools and lead to invasive personal exams for trans and cisgender students.

Additionally, Proposition 135 would ban gender-affirming care surgery for minors in Colorado. Voters in Missouri will vote on a similar amendment, which would ban gender-affirming surgeries in addition to hormone treatment and puberty-blocking drugs.

With the Trump administration continuing its efforts to undermine public confidence in election security by making false claims of voter fraud, multiple states will leave changes to voter ID registration rules up to their voters.

While it is already illegal for noncitizens to vote in federal elections, six states—Alaska, Arizona, Arkansas, Kansas, South Dakota, and West Virginia—will vote to amend their state constitutions to include language that explicitly states that only U.S. citizens can participate in elections. Additionally, voters in Arizona, California, Colorado, Nevada, North Carolina, and Ohio will decide on election security measures that will require identification when people vote in person or by mail. Opponents of these measures argue that it will make voting harder.

For example, Proposition 144 in Arizona—a state where 75 percent of voters voted by mail in the 2024 election—would amend the state constitution to require voters to show valid identification before each election, regardless of whether they choose to vote in person or by a mail-in ballot. The measure does not explain how voters would be able to comply with such a rule.

Proposition 39 in California would similarly change voting rules by requiring citizens to present government-issued IDs before submitting their ballot, but the measure does not clarify which types of ID would be considered acceptable.

In Arizona, Proposition 142 could shape diversity, equity, and inclusion efforts at public institutions. If passed, the Republican-backed measure would amend Arizona’s constitution to prohibit the state from requiring anyone to endorse preferential treatment or discrimination against people on the basis of their race or ethnicity.

This debate fits within the larger national conversation about DEI initiatives, with President Trump working to dismantle DEI at the federal level through a series of executive orders.

“Arizona already bans racial discrimination or preferential treatment. That’s already in our constitution,” state senator Analise Ortiz said in a message shared to social media. “[Proposition 142] is not needed and it’s normalizing discrimination. Diversity makes Arizona great. Equity gives people a fair shot. Inclusion makes our community strong.”

#2744. Tanner Wanish and Michael Vaill Are Ready for Rock Climbing’s Limelight

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Wanish (left) and Vaill during the Goliath Traverse (Photo: Joshua Noll)

My favorite scene from the new adventure film Nobody & Goliath involves cigarettes.

Having completed most of the gargantuan Goliath Traverse in California’s Sierra Nevada—a 32-mile ridgeline that passes over 60 summits—rock climbers Tanner Wanish and Michael Vaill are dizzy with exhaustion. They’ve spent the past eight days climbing in the no-fall zone. They’ve gone long stretches of the trip without water or food.

To celebrate almost being done, Wanish and Vaill spark up a couple of lung darts and puff away at 12,000 feet, giggling and backslapping like a couple of high schoolers.

“We were just daydreaming about all of the food and comforts we wanted once we finished, and Mike randomly said he could go for a smoke,” Wanish told me recently. Both he and Vaill sometimes chew nicotine pouches for an extra burst of energy on the crag, he added, but never puff cigarettes.

“I’m not a smoker, but that one was quite enjoyable,” he added.

Look, I’m no smoker either—tobacco products of all forms make my stomach turn. But I still love the scene. It hammers home the film’s primary thrust, which is to introduce mainstream audiences to Wanish and Vaill, alongside an intoxicating message: Climbing’s newest superstars are a different breed.

And no, it’s not just because they enjoy a cig every now and then.

I called Wanish and Vaill recently to talk about the film and their newfound stardom in rock climbing.

“We do still feel like outsiders,” Wanish told me. “I do have this really significant sense of imposter syndrome when I’m with these professional climbers. But it’s starting to change.”

Don’t feel bad if you’re unfamiliar with their names. The duo has built a following within rock climbing circles for their speedy ascents of the biggest walls in Yosemite National Park. Nobody & Goliath, which is touring theaters across the U.S. in 2026, is their first big film project.

Outside has written about them once before. In October 2024, they became Yosemite’s new power couple with a speedy finish on the so-called Yosemite Triple: El Capitan, Half Dome, and Mount Watkins. They did it faster (17 hours, 55 minutes) than any team in history, surpassing times set by some of the valley’s greatest climbing duos: Alex Honnold and Tommy Caldwell, Dean Potter and Timmy O’Neil, Brad Gobright and Jim Reynolds, among others.

A week after the triple, Vaill and Wanish did the route again, and then tacked on a fourth climb, the south face of the Washington Column. They called this pioneering test of endurance the “Yosemite Quad.”

Lost in many of the news stories about their fast climbs was an eye-popping datapoint. Neither Vaill nor Wanish had been climbing for very long. At the time, Wanish had been in the sport for less than five years. Vaill, similarly, had started climbing in his mid-twenties.

“Christmas Day, 2016, that was the first day I ever touched rock,” Vaill, who is 34, told me. “I’d seen pictures of Yosemite, but had no idea I’d end up doing those types of climbs.”

Prior to becoming full-time climbers, Wanish served in the Navy SEALs, while Vaill was a scientific researcher with a PhD in biomedical sciences. They grew up in the southeastern U.S.—Wanish in South Carolina and Vaill in Georgia. Both guys told me their early twenties revolved around booze and school and attending college football games—not pushing their bodies on vertical faces of rock.

“I was partying way too hard,” Wanish said. “Bars every weekend, drinking with the guys. That lifestyle.”

Plenty of late bloomers go on to achieve lofty goals in climbing, but few become the focus of the feature-length films. It’s no secret that climbing’s marquee documentaries tend to focus on athletes with long and storied histories in the sport: think Girl Climber‘s Emily Harrington, or Dawn Wall and Caldwell, Honnold and his many films, and even more recent movies about wunderkind Connor Herson.

Even more core climbing filmmakers train their lens on the sport’s child prodigies, like Adam Ondra or Chris Sharma.

Wanish and Vaill recognize their outsider status amongst climbing’s biggest athletes. Ondra and Herson tackle routes rated 5.15. Vaill and Wanish, meanwhile, hit their limit on routes in the 5.11 or 5.12 range.

“We haven’t spent the last 20 years training on a campus board,” Vaill said. “One in a million people has the genetic potential to climb a route rated 5.15, and we’re not those guys. Our story is about simple things that every single person out there has. Our story is about how we tapped into it.”

The secret sauce behind Wanish and Vaill is their climbing partnership. Neither guy, on his own, is anywhere near being crowned the world’s best rock climber. But when teamed together, the duo push each other to climb harder and faster and longer than even Honnold and Caldwell.

“I don’t think we would be able to do this stuff without each other,” Wanish said. “Both of us have this ability to really commit to something—like a big climbing objective or to the micro moves that get you up a single pitch.”

Nobody & Goliath, which was made by filmmaker Joshua Noll, shows what this actually looks like. Footage captures the two egging each other on as they zip up big walls in Yosemite at 2 A.M. They coach each other as they grab at tiny holds on the side of El Capitan.

They move fast on the rock wall and rarely stop to rest. When one guy is exhausted and the other is still going strong, they use this imbalance to push on until they reach the top.

“There are times when you’re climbing and you get tired, and you think, ‘This kind of sucks. Should we just go down?’ And if your partner agrees with you, then you’re probably going to bail,” Vaill said. “With Tanner, I know he’s going to want to keep going, which means I am, too. And after talking it over, I know we’re going to be 100 percent ready to rage.”

The first half of Nobody & Goliath showcases how their partnership propelled them from anonymous dreamers to the pinnacle of Yosemite big wall climbing.

The two met on a Mountain Project message board after Wanish, who had just become obsessed with the sport, was looking for a climbing partner with equal levels of enthusiasm. At the time, both guys were at that very special period in one’s relationship with outdoor recreation, when all you want to do is practice your specific sport, all day, day every day.

When they found each other, their mutual desire to push one another led them to progress from local crags near San Diego to El Capitan in under a year.

It then propelled them to take on the valley’s speed climbs. As Wanish said, their ability to push each other “pulls every second of slack out of these walls.”

“If you look at the way a normal person climbs a pitch, they look up, look down, think about what they’re going to do, and then maybe make a move,” he said. “We are able to commit to these moves without thinking about them. We’re not hesitating or pausing because we’re not scared. And when you do that, you’re able to take these walls that take people three or four days and do them in five hours.”

Vaill called his partnership with Wanish a “cheat code.”

“I feel like Tanner and I have been on this rocket-boosted trajectory through rock climbing because we tapped into the power of partnership in climbing,” he said.

Stories come and go about famed duos in the world of adventure, and the tales usually include platitudes like the ones Wanish and Vaill have expressed about teamwork and commitment. To see a world-class climbing partnership actually take shape on film gives Nobody & Goliath mainstream appeal that other climbing movies don’t always capture.

I’ve watched most climbing documentaries that have attracted casual audiences, and many have a similar structure. A prodigious climber becomes obsessed with a mountain or route, and commits all of their energy to completing the goal. The climber can’t move on with his or her life until they reach the summit.

Nobody & Goliath is different. After their Yosemite exploits, the two take on the Goliath Traverse, a brutal trek across the Sierra Nevada’s highest peaks, hoping to become the second group to complete it behind climber Vitaliy Musiyenko, who first did it in 2021.

But at no point during the arduous trip do Wanish and Vaill seem singularly obsessed with the accomplishment. Sure, they want to complete it. But the trip isn’t a life-altering mission, à la Harrington free climbing the Golden Gate route up El Capitan in Girl Climber.

Instead, Wanish and Vaill commit to each other. Cameras capture them as their strength and stamina ebb and flow over eight days of punishing climbs. At times, Vaill is totally depleted—the result of a recent climbing injury. Wanish must navigate the strength imbalance, and eventually finds ways to pick him up.

In other scenes, the guys find humor in the sucky repetition of climbing loose and dangerous pinnacles and then rappelling back down, only to face another tricky ascent. Wanish and Vaill come across as guys you’d want to grab a beer with—a quality that lifelong elite climbers don’t always possess.

The camaraderie and friendship is what ultimately help them finish the route.

In some ways, this story is more relatable to the average outdoor athlete than that of uber-talented pro climber taking on a lifelong dream that mere mortals will never approach. Wanish told me the film’s name originates with this idea.

“That’s why they call it Nobody & Goliath, because we are the nobodies,” Wanish said. “We’re just two guys who are willing to try hard and commit. And in a way, it shows that most people are capable of doing something like this. You just need to be willing to go all-in, and you’ll be shocked what you’re capable of.”

#2777. An Old-School Perspective on Finding Better Duck Hunting: Get Creative and Target the Hardest Places

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published Oct 27, 2026 3:00 PM EDT

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

The way some hunters talk, you’d think good waterfowl hunting was a thing of the past. Sure, it was great 25 years ago. You could pick almost any field or pothole and shoot a limit of ducks. But millions of ducks and geese still sweep the flyways each fall, and a determined hunter can find good shooting. It’s just that you have to hunt the hard places.

That’s my name for spots that teem with ducks and geese but rarely see a hunter. Sometimes these areas demand too much effort, but more often hard places are open expanses that offer a hunter no place to hide. Pastures, the favorite haunts of Canada geese, are one example. How can you blind on a pasture as flat as a billiard table? A flooded field also is a problem. Hunters line the dikes of flooded stubblefields like fence posts, but few of them head for the middle because there’s no place to hide. The potential for open-water hunting on lakes, reservoirs, and bays, where waterfowl raft by the thousands, has hardly been touched. The windswept expanses of open ice where geese frequently rest has great possibilities too. In some parts of the United States a person has little choice about where to hunt. But in many regions, particularly in the prairie and Western states, hard places with excellent hunting abound.

Most hunters credit ducks and geese with too much intelligence. Some waterfowlers would have you believe that the Canada goose, for example, is second cousin to Einstein. That image of brilliance is ridiculous. Geese and other birds are smart just as dogs are smart. To teach a dog to sit, you push his rear down and say, “Sit!” After a few lessons he’ll sit when you give the command. Waterfowl learn similarly. On heavily hunted lands waterfowl avoid cover because they’ve learned that’s where the shots come from.

In much open country they’ve had no such conditioning. No one has shot at them, so ducks fall over themselves to land in decoys placed far from cover. In the hard places, waterfowl aren’t conditioned to be cautious. That is the basis for good bird hunting. It also accounts for the fact that you don’t need much of a blind in the open. Hunting hard places is more a matter of philosophy than of technique. Once you’ve decided to hunt a hard place you’ve cleared a major hurdle. Then you just get out there and start hunting. To show how simple it is, I’ll tell you about some ways my friends and I have hunted the hard places.

I usually don’t use a blind. In my part of Oregon, Canada geese spend a lot of time feeding and resting on pastures, and most ranchers don’t want their sod dug up for blinds. A blind above ground also is about as conspicuous as a high-rise apartment building. The only option is no blind. So you just lie on the ground.

Any hunter knows movement really spooks waterfowl, so being able to disguise yourself as a frozen clump of mud is the big step in making this work. I usually clump a few goose decoys around my body and put one in front of my face. The oversize shell decoys made today are especially good for this kind of hunting. A friend of mine has made silhouette decoys that are so large he can curl up behind one. In late season, when swans are present, I’ve used large swan silhouettes among my duck and goose decoys. This technique has worked especially well on ice, when I’ve worn a white suit and lain behind a swan silhouette.

Put your decoys as far from ditches and weed patches as you can. Set up where birds don’t expect danger. Look for small depressions, places where the grass is lumpy, or pressure ridges on the ice so you can hide. Even a slight variation will make you less obvious. And wear clothing that looks like the ground. The obvious choice after a snowfall would be to wear a white suit or to cover yourself over with a sheet. When the cover is green, pull a square of green camouflage cloth over yourself. Some of the pastures I’ve hunted in are muddy. My hunting coat is so impregnated with dirt it looks like part of the ground, so I often don’t cover myself with anything. An occasional roll in the mud keeps the blind fresh.

On windy days this simple approach is almost foolproof. The birds fly low and come directly in from the downwind side. Obviously you want to lie on the upwind side of a decoy. On calm days when waterfowl fly higher and sometimes circle, it’s not as easy, especially if you gawk at them.

In my region farmers flood their grainfields after harvest. Flooded fields draw ducks the way a candy store draws kids. For years I pondered the featureless expanses of water and mud, trying to figure out how I’d get out there without sinking out of sight forever. Then it struck me—an air mattress. Along with a huge bag of floating decoys, I dragged a well-inflated canvas air mattress into a partly flooded field. With the decoys downwind, I flopped the mattress on a clump of mud and lay on it, face up, and pulled a piece of camouflage cloth over me. Ducks and geese nearly landed on me. An air mattress will do the trick in any soggy spot where you can anchor it to some ground. But don’t try it in deep water.

A rubber raft is best for deep water. I use a two-man, rubberized, olive drab canvas raft. You can find these at big sports stores or surplus stores.

The raft can be dragged or carried to any location, and it often serves where a rigid, heavy duckboat won’t. I’ve used it in the same fields I used to hunt with an air mattress. It also works well in marshes where only a few inches of grass show above the surface of the water. I got this idea from watching a hunter one day in a flooded stubblefield. He had a low-profile boat, which he covered with chicken wire. He tied stubble all over the wire, then crawled under it into the boat. His simple blind looked like a mat of straw lying on the water. Ducks swarmed into his decoys as if he weren’t there.

From that example I made a chicken-wire cover for my raft, with a flap cut out of the middle so I could sit up and have an area to shoot through. I tied short pieces of decoy line on the chicken wire and used these to tie grass, stubble, lily pads, and other appropriate plants to the wire. In addition, I cut a floor for the raft made of 3/8-inch plywood. With some spar varnish for weatherproofing and a strip of outdoor carpet stapled around the floor’s edge to keep it from chafing the raft, I had a rigid bottom that made the raft stable. I can sit, kneel, or stand in it without much wobbling.

Another item that could be used in the same way as the rubber raft is a float tube, or belly boat. While these are made for fishing small waters, they’re well suited to hunting. I recently got one with a camouflage cover, and it looks like part of the marsh. With a chicken-wire cone covered with tules, it would look just like a muskrat house.

If the water is too shallow to use a float tube in the conventional manner—with your legs dangling in the water—you can use it as a shooting platform. One friend, Dave Hummel, did something similar with a tire inner tube. Dave saw lots of pintails on big marsh ponds. Those educated birds weren’t about to land within shooting range of the tule fringes, where a hunter could hide. They stuck to the middle of the huge ponds, where the only cover was a few patches of lily pads that stuck up a few inches above the water. There was no way to blind a boat out there, and a person couldn’t lie down in the foot-deep water. So Dave wired a round piece of plywood to the top of an inner tube. To hunt those smart pintails he threw his decoys onto the open water and put his inner tube table in the lily pads. He laid his gun and shells on the platform and camouflaged it with lily pads. Dressed in chest waders he was able to kneel behind it. To hide from circling ducks, he put his face down on the platform. When ducks were within range, he rose on his knees to shoot. Tough shooting? Yes. But it was the only way to get within range of those ducks, and Dave got his share of them.

I recently fished with a fellow who had a “boat” called a Bass Tracker Bantam. It’s a flat plastic hull about five feet long made for poking around backwaters for bass. It’s very stable and rises no more than six inches from the water. It looks to me like an ideal piece of equipment for hard-place hunting.

Another friend hunted from shallow water by using a piece of heavy brown plastic. The water, matted with weeds, was only three or four inches deep. He laid a 12×12-foot square of plastic on the water and jumped into the middle of it. The weeds held up the edges of the plastic to keep water out and he was able to lie down to hide from ducks without getting wet.

Open water may be the extreme example of a hard place to hunt. These days, with many huge reservoirs next to croplands, lots of ducks and geese rest on wide water. A sink box is the answer, but sink boxes are illegal. The next best thing is a low-profile boat. You can’t hide such a boat so you try to make it look like floating debris. My friend David Hagerbaumer hunts extensively on open bays along the Pacific Coast. He uses a Barnegat sneakbox, a flat boat that looks like a miniature hydroplane. Dave has a net for the boat that camouflages it, and he puts seagull decoys on the decks. On open water the boat just looks like a piece of flotsam drifting on the bay. Dave lies on his back in the boat, facing his decoys, which are downwind. This rig works well on diving ducks, and Dave often takes puddle ducks and brant while hunting from it too.

On an incoming tide, puddle ducks often work the shores of estuaries to feed on the flooding beaches and tidal marshlands. That’s when Dave hunts the edges, and he camouflages his boat by tying grass to the net covering, or by shoveling mud clumps onto the decks.

Dave has another rig, called an alligator, that should really do the job for you on mudflats or beaches. These are no longer available commercially, but you could easily make your own. The alligator is a fiberglass coffin, shaped like a mummy with an oblong hole cut out of one side. You drag it out to where you want to hunt, dig a hole, and bury the thing, except for the opening. Then you crawl in, lie on your back, and face up through the hole. It’s no more than a way to stay dry while hiding in a soggy place, but that’s all you need in the hard places.

Hunting hard places can be a little wet and dirty. A rugged, heavy pair of chest waders and a canvas coat that can withstand abuse are necessities. The right gun also is important. Guns get muddy in hard places, and most autoloaders I’ve used turn into single shots under these conditions. A pump gun is OK because you can grind shells through even if the gun does take on a load of grime, but the best choice is a double barrel, which is nearly impervious to dirt. Invariably in hard places you must shoot lying on your back, sitting, or from your knees. Your feet may be stuck in the mud, and you’ll feel like you’re caught in a spiderweb when you start trying to shoot through a chicken-wire cover. Add to that a good stiff breeze, which ducks can use to get quickly out of range, and you have a shooting challenge. A good idea is to practice shooting claybirds from awkward positions, also lie down and practice throwing your gun to your shoulder and swinging it. You may want to learn to shoot both right and left-handed so you can handle ducks coming in from both sides.

Hard places generally call for big decoy spreads. In open expanses, ducks in particular congregate in huge swarms. I can pile about five dozen inflatable ducks on my rubber raft so that’s about my maximum, but more decoys would be even better.

The last day of the season a couple of years ago typifies hard-place hunting. Ducks were streaming into a flooded stubblefield and hunters lined the dikes around the field. I waded out to the middle, found a small patch of mud to lie on, and threw out some decoys. Mallards broke their necks to get into that spread. But the mud was sticky and the wind was howling. By the time I could sit up to shoot, the ducks usually had flared to long range. The one box of shells in my pocket accounted for only four mallards and a honker. But it was an unforgettable afternoon of duck hunting. While hunters pass-shooting from the dikes only took an occasional high bird, I was at the center of a swarming, teeming mass of ducks.

Read Next: Duck Hunting’s New Reality: It Will Keep Getting Tougher for the Workaday Waterfowler

Great hunting still does exist. It demands some ingenuity and hard work. But if you hunt the hard places, you’ll find the birds. And that’s what duck hunting is all about.

This story was originally published in the November 1981 issue of Outdoor Life.

The film industry has the Oscars. Fashion has the Met Gala. And soccer has the Ballon d’Or. Last night, soccer legends from every corner rocked up to the London Palladium to see who would be crowned the year’s best baller.

The stitching on the lapel. The gold brooch. The pleated trouser. The devil's in the details when it came to Diomandé's Dolce & Gabbana suit.

The Aston Villa player swapped out a tie for a gold chain, which nicely matched the brooch pinned to his Saint Laurent suit.

Your first Ballon d'Or invite is a pretty good excuse to get properly dressed up, and Maza kept things nice and straightforward in a classic black tux.

The Spanish goalkeeper collected his award in classic Dolce & Gabbana, and this photo op looks like a keeper that should be framed for the mantelpiece.

Casparij went big on the sleeves and cropped on the trousers—a very good argument for playing around with your suit's proportions.

Raya's kept his suit lapels just like his arms when he's in goal: way out wide.

#3818. Soccer Club Backtracks After Telling Photographers to Stop Wearing Revealing Outfits at Stadium

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Soccer Club Backtracks After Telling Photographers to Stop Wearing Revealing Outfits at Stadium

A soccer club has been forced to back down after a prominent female sports photographer felt targeted by its request that photographers stop wearing revealing outfits and leggings.

Sportivo Ameliano, a professional soccer team from Paraguay that plays in the nation’s top soccer league (Paraguayan Primera División), is facing backlash over its new dress code for photographers and reporters in the stadium.

The soccer club requested members of the press to avoid wearing revealing outfits at the ground “in order to maintain… the club’s institutional image.” In the new guidelines, Sportivo Ameliano banned both female and male photographers from wearing “low-cut blouses” and outfits that exposed the navel. On top of that, women were banned from wearing leggings that weren’t black or dark blue.

Female photographer Adriana Caballero — a professional sports photographer who regularly covers games in Paraguayan Primera División and has over 200,000 followers on Instagram — felt targeted by Sportivo Ameliano’s new dress code.

Caballero spoke publicly about feeling singled out by the new rules, sparking widespread discussion about discrimination against women in Paraguayan sports journalism.

In a series of public statements, 29-year-old Caballero says she believes Sportivo Ameliano introduced its new dress code for photographers after images of her taking photos at a soccer game circulated widely. She says the club’s clothing ban left her feeling uncomfortable and surprised, adding that she always wears clothes she finds comfortable for work.

“I always want to wear the most comfortable clothes possible for my job. Sooner or later it probably had to come to this,” Caballero says, according to reports.

In a further radio interview, Caballero expressed her concern that the soccer club’s new dress code would hinder other female sports photographers and journalists.

“I always prioritize my own comfort. When I read the statement [from Sportivo Ameliano], it came as a shock, but I said to myself: ‘This could harm my colleagues,’” the photographer says.

Caballero’s comments prompted fierce criticism of Sportivo Ameliano on social media, prompting club president Hector Melgarejo to backtrack and say the dress code had been issued without management’s approval.

“I deeply regret that this has happened. It did not leave the club, it was not institutional, but it was something that came out of the person in charge of the press without any authorization from the club,” Melgarejo says in an interview with Fútbol a lo Grande on Thursday.

He says the club will issue an official statement distancing itself from the dress code, which was introduced without his knowledge, and that he is still waiting for an explanation.

“We are going to issue a statement institutionally where we are going to clarify that it is not what the club reflects as an institution,” Melgarejo explains. “It was something that was done on its own initiative, but I don’t know, I don’t know the reason why it was done either, right? I have not yet finished the meeting here for him to explain that to me.”

#2756. Surefire Ways to Beat Jet Lag, According to Elite Athletes

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Mountaineer Megan Rath reaches the summit of Lhotse (the world's fourth-highest mountain at 27,939 feet). (Photo: Courtesy of Megan Rath)

Imagine not only enduring a long-haul flight, but then having to compete physically soon after you land. Top athletes have no time for jet lag symptoms, such as extreme fatigue, headaches, insomnia and gastrointestinal issues. They have more important things to do, like scaling mountains or qualifying for the Olympics.

Two recent research reviews focusing on how jet lag impacts athletic performance outline how jumping three or more time zones during extended east- or westbound travel disrupts the body’s internal clock. Current Sleep Medicine Reports says transatlantic travel exposes athletes to stressors like prolonged inactivity and dehydration, which not only affect sleep patterns and quality but can also impair an elite athlete’s cognitive and physical performance with symptoms including trouble concentrating, disorientation, light-headedness and irritability.

The journal Sports also found jet lag can disrupt athletes’ physiological, circadian, and performance systems. Athletes might experience hormonal and mood shifts, blood pressure or heart rate changes, and cognitive impairment that can affect strength, coordination, reaction time and velocity—any of which could impede high-performance outcomes.

From sleep scheduling and strategic exposure to light and dark to proper nutrition and hydration, four top competitors, including a high-altitude mountaineer and an ultramarathon runner, share with Outside how they beat jet lag and stay on their game while traveling.

Long before she plunges her paddle into the water, two-time Olympic sprint canoe medalist Katie Vincent prepares for international competitions by following a detailed protocol.

“Having a plan helps the transition,” Vincent tells Outside, having competed across Europe, Asia and South America. “The ideal situation would be to go a week or two early, have a full prep camp and slowly get over jet lag. But even with three to five days, you can at least get enough sleep, get into your routine and start competing.”

During flights, Vincent wears compression socks, a sleep mask, and earplugs, and prefers a window seat where she can curl her 5-foot-9 frame into a comfortable sleeping position. Upon arrival the next morning, she drinks two cups of coffee and aims to stay awake all day, i.e. no naps. The next few days are spent waking up, training, and going to bed at her regular time. She avoids napping and takes melatonin as needed to help her sleep.

“By day four, we’re adjusted, which is crucial as our nervous systems need to be regulated to produce the best performances on the water,” explains Vincent, who also tracks her sleep patterns on a Garmin watch.

“Most athletes on my team use their watch to gauge sleep quality. But everyone’s different; some can fall asleep in an instant, and others need more time to fall into the rhythm. There’s no one way to handle jet lag, but there are ways to navigate it.”

As a professional ultrarunner, former U.S. Biathlon National Team member, and a Carmichael Training Systems (CTS)-certified endurance sports coach, Corrine Malcolm has clocked countless hours in the air to run 100-mile races in Asia, Europe, Australia, and New Zealand.

“Jet lag impacts more than your ability to sleep or feel awake—there are hydration components, thermal regulation, and GI distress,” Malcolm tells Outside. She is based in Leavenworth, Washington, and hosts Trail Society, a podcast about trail running.

In the days leading up to a flight, Malcolm practices sleep banking—going to bed earlier and staying in bed longer to shore up resilience in anticipation of sleep loss.

“When you’re racing 100-plus miles, coming in with a lot of sleep banked can be important for feeling good,” she explains. “You’re also trying to move your circadian rhythm closer to the time zone you’re moving to. It takes a whole day for every time zone over three hours you’re crossing eastward versus a half-day adjustment going westward.”

Smart long-haul flying starts when booking your ticket; Malcolm opts for evening flights that head directly to her destination.

“You’ll sleep more readily on a ten-hour [direct] flight, versus flying from Seattle to Newark, and then to Geneva,” she says.

Malcolm brings her own food so she can eat quickly and get sleep-ready with noise-canceling headphones, compression socks, and an eye mask.

“Getting the biggest chunk of sleep possible goes a long way in managing how you feel on the ground. I also make sure I’m getting good morning light and eating familiar foods,” she says, noting that high-protein meals boost arousal while high-carbohydrate foods increase drowsiness.

“Control what you can control. Fixating on things that are out of your control will keep you up at 2 A.M. when you’re trying desperately to sleep. I always remind athletes: One bad night of sleep isn’t ideal, but focus on how you’ve been taking care of yourself for the last couple of weeks.”

“Some people I know start adjusting to a schedule ahead of time; I do not,” Emily Harrison Torrence, an ultramarathoner in Rapid City, South Dakota, tells Outside. She is also the co-owner of Sundog Running, which coaches athletes training for ultramarathons, endurance sports, and trail running.

“I try to nap on the plane, but I let that happen naturally, because it’s hard to know exactly what time zone you’re in when you’re in the air.”

Torrence packs lots of snacks for the plane: protein, healthy fat, nothing too sugary.

“It’s not uncommon for athletes, myself included, to have food allergies or food sensitivities, and sometimes the meals served on planes may not be too friendly, so make sure you’re prepared to be self-sustainable,” she suggests. “On international flights—before, during, and once you land—always have fluids on you. Plain water is great, but adding electrolytes is also good. Travel is dehydrating, and that can affect your performance and how you adjust to the new time zone.”

Once Torrence lands, she tries to adapt to her new time schedule but doesn’t overthink it.

“It depends on how long I’m going to be there; if I’m just flying in for a race a couple of days ahead of time, I’ll nap if I need to when I land, but just for 20 minutes— avoid passing out for three or four hours,” she says. “Good nutrition and staying well hydrated will help you offset that sleep deficit as you’re adjusting.”

Megan Rath, a triathlete-turned-mountaineer in Philadelphia who has scaled the Seven Summits—the highest mountain peaks on every continent—likes to add new methods to her mix of jet-lag-busting tricks. So, when she read about Timeshifter, a science-based circadian rhythm app, she was eager to learn more.

The app uses circadian neuroscience algorithms to create a custom schedule that slowly resets your body’s clock before, during, and after travel. After entering your sleep habits, age, sex, caffeine or melatonin usage, and flight itinerary, the app generates a personalized plan that sends you prompts to wake up, get bright light or none at all, drink coffee, and sleep.

“It’s all about light control—when you’re seeing light, when you’re not—and that mindset shift was a game changer,” Rath tells Outside. When climbing Lhotse, the world’s fourth-highest mountain, last spring, the mountaineer followed her Timeshifter plan.

“It’s a funky time change of nine hours and 45 minutes, plus flying from Newark to Istanbul and Istanbul to Kathmandu—essentially two red-eyes in a row—so it was tough trying to time when I’m putting my sunglasses on at the airport to start signaling my time to sleep,” recalls Rath.

“By the time I got to Mera, where they drop you off in the middle of nowhere, I felt pretty good. I’ve never felt this good on an 8,000-meter climb, and I was very dialed in with my preparation, including the hypoxic tent so I wouldn’t have to spend as much time on the mountain.”

Powered by a generator that reduces the oxygen concentration, hypoxic tents simulate higher altitude to allow the body to acclimatize before a climb. In addition to the tent, Rath prepares for rigorous treks by being laser-focused on hydration.

“If you’re drinking two liters at sea level, you’re drinking four at altitude, no exceptions, because your body is working that much harder. It’s dried out, and water needs to get to your cells; otherwise you’ve got headaches and cramping,” she explains.

“Everything I do is measured. I know exactly how much I’ve had to drink and when I’m drinking it. You don’t want to just guzzle water, because your body doesn’t have a chance to absorb it. They tell you to drink three or four sips every 15 minutes.”

Rath also limits sugar and avoids alcohol, which can increase dehydration due to its diuretic effects while also decreasing sleep quality.

With smart tools, proper nutrition,  and customized schedules, you can optimize performance instead of crashing in a jet-lagged stupor.

Before learning how to navigate jet lag symptoms, Montreal-based Wendy Helfenbaum fell asleep at inappropriate times all over the world, including in the middle of a loud gymnastics event in Cardiff, Wales and on a ferry to Corfu, Greece while passengers heaved all around her. Wendy’s work has appeared in USA Today, BBC, Hotels Above Par, RANGE, AARP and more.

#3281. The Time of Day You Exercise Could Help Control Blood Sugar

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Any physical activity is good for your metabolic health, but research says timing it right may have more benefits for your glucose levels.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

Although blood sugar control is essential for those with diabetes, it’s also key for maintaining health overall. Research indicates that blood sugar levels can affect a range of physical functions, including hormone regulation, body composition, digestive processes, and even cognitive health.

That’s why keeping control of blood glucose levels is important, and one way to help might include doing moderate- to vigorous-intensity exercise in the evening, according to a study publish in Obesity.

Researchers looked at 186 men and women who were overweight or had obesity and were diagnosed with at least one metabolic impairment, such as high blood pressure or high cholesterol. Over a 14-day period, researchers tracked their physical activity and glucose levels, along with the time of day for exercise. Morning exercise was defined as before noon, with afternoon exercise between noon and 6:00 p.m, and evening exercise after that.

At the end of the two weeks, those who did more than 50 percent of their exercise in the evening had significantly lower glucose levels compared to those who were sedentary, and better glucose regulation than participants who mainly exercised in the mornings or afternoons.

The effects were especially notable for those who’d struggled with regulating their blood sugar before participating in the study, according to co-author Antonia Clavero Jimeno, Ph.D.(c), researcher in the department of physical education and sports at the University of Granada in Spain.

Although the study participants were specifically chosen based on metabolic factors and sedentary behavior, he told Bicycling that previous evidence has shown that the results might be the same for those without those factors, and who already exercise regularly.

“In fact, the impact of both physical activity volume and timing for glycemic control may be amplified in those with higher activity levels,” he said.

That’s backed up by previous research looking at active people who were assessed for blood sugar changes based on when they exercised. In that study, published in 2022, those who performed moderate-to-vigorous activity in the afternoon had 18 percent lower insulin resistance compared to morning exercisers, and the evening group fared even better, with a 25 percent reduction in insulin resistance.

One factor that was not explored in either study was the role of dietary changes, and this is an area that needs more research, said Clavero Jimeno. Determining whether a strategy like time-restricted eating—also called intermittent fasting—would be helpful or harmful to glucose regulation when paired with evening exercise is a next step, and is already being studied by Clavero Jimeno and his team. (Some research published this year suggests intermittent fasting may have promising effects on blood sugar regulation, at least in those with type 2 diabetes.)

“Dietary intake is, of course, recognized as crucial for glycemic control, and may influence the results overall,” Clavero Jimeno said. “But in the absence of that information, this current study does emphasize that if you want better control of your glucose levels, evening exercise, done at a higher intensity, may be beneficial.”

#2668. 1 In 3 Teenage Boys Follow This Problematic Influencer — And A New Study Explains Why

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The power to make informed choices in your life is more important than ever, and you deserve real information. Keep good journalism alive. Become a HuffPost member.

Already a member? Log in to hide these messages.

What goes on at home matters more than you think.

The manosphere ― an online community full of misogynistic rhetoric and retrograde ideas about masculinity ― is known for its attention-grabbing content. But a new study suggests there is much more than inflammatory messaging to blame for teenage boys’ interest in this particular online space.

Researchers from the University of Massachusetts at Amherst and the University of Michigan compiled data from teenage boys to learn more about their thoughts on masculinity, women and the manosphere ― and the results were pretty eye-opening.

The study authors analyzed cell phone responses from 601 teenage boys ages 13 to 17. The survey asked questions about who they followed on social media, masculinity and how their parents or guardians portray gender in the home.

The teens were also asked if they followed two controversial manosphere figures — Andrew Tate, a self-proclaimed misogynist who is currently in a federal detention center facing charges of sex trafficking and rape, and Jordan Peterson, a psychologist who is known to spread anti-feminist, sexist ideas. Approximately 33% of teens followed Tate and 11% followed Peterson.

The teens also shared their three favorite social media influencers. Researchers then compared the teens’ favorite social media influencers with a list of manosphere-aligned influencers to see if there was any overlap. Less than 10% of respondents named a manosphere influencer in their top three.

The boys were then asked how masculine they believed they were, and rated their masculinity level on a scale from 1 to 5. They also answered questions about their mood, including feelings of depression and hostility. Participants’ levels of depression and hostility were roughly the same whether or not they were involved in the manosphere.

Overall, researchers found that the teen boys who said they liked manosphere creators were more likely to hear ideas that reinforced strict gender norms in their home and more likely to classify themselves higher on the masculinity scale. This suggests that what caregivers model at home has a huge impact on kids and their beliefs around topics like gender roles.

Household rules and the ways that we operate really do have an influence.

The influence of a household was stronger than whether teens followed Tate or Peterson on social media. Boys with parents who adhered to strict gender norms were up to three times more likely to hold these same views and fall under the influence of the manosphere.

Jami Dumler, a licensed clinical social worker and director of clinical programs with Thriveworks, was not surprised by this finding. Dumler is not associated with the research.

“Sometimes boys are getting these messages online, but they might also be getting [them] in their own home,” Dumler said. “Maybe not outwardly spoken [to] the extreme extents of Andrew Tate and Jordan Peterson, but thinking about, in your household and family, how do men show their emotions? Is it OK to cry? Is it OK to not have it all together? Are certain high expectations put on men while others are put on women?”

“Are we sending balanced messages in our own space?” Dumler asked.

It starts with the guardians and caregivers, she noted. “Wherever we can improve our modeling, it’s going to go a long way because children soak that in.”

This research did have limitations. The boys were not asked why they followed the influencers online — some may be true fans, while others may be “hate-following” the creators. Researchers also gathered responses only once, so the study would not have captured whether participants’ mindsets changed over time.

If you notice that your teen is following problematic people on social media, it’s important to lead with curiosity rather than judgment, Dumler said.

“If we come in hot and we’re judging this kind of content online, we’re not going to get very far in terms of that opening up, support and next steps,” she said.

The overall goal should be connection and understanding, as well as creating a safe space for your teen to share.

“We know that teens are looking for their own control and their own thoughts and development, and so we want to support that, not take over the steering wheel and drive it for them,” Dumler said.

You can do this by letting them steer the conversation and asking them open-ended questions such as “I’ve seen this topic pop up a lot, but I don’t know much about it. Do you?” or “Can you teach me about this public figure? What does he say?”

“Let them take charge there. And when they do so, you then learn a lot about what it actually means to them versus what we’re assuming based on studies or social media,” Dumler added.

Dumler explained that teenage boys may turn to the manosphere because they’re “seeking out that strong masculine guardianship or model.”

This doesn’t mean they don’t have people to look up to at home or in their community, but social media is designed to grab attention, which is exactly how Peterson and Tate appeal to men and teen boys. The daily things dads, uncles or grandfathers do day to day may get lost because they aren’t attention-grabbing; they’re just part of the routine.

Regular one-on-one conversations with male role models can be a way to facilitate some of these conversations and address any concerns before they become a bigger issue, Dumler said.

“I always encourage starting with setting up a routine one-on-one time,” she noted, which is less intimidating and more productive than a big one-time sit-down conversation.

This could be a weekly drive to practice with grandpa or a monthly donut breakfast with dad. “If we have a certain monthly or weekly routine that my teen knows is going to be coming up, it creates a safer entry,” Dumler added.

It can also help to have these kinds of conversations during “side-to-side” play, meaning while fishing, or while going for a walk, throwing the football around or going for a drive, she said.

“There’s another activity involved. It takes some of the pressure off of the conversation,” Dumler said.

If your teenage boy does share misogynistic beliefs during these conversations, you can agree to disagree. “You don’t always have to get in a direct argument of who’s right or wrong,” she said, as frustrating as that can be.

Teenage boys’ frontal lobes are not yet fully formed, so their ability to critically think is still developing, she explained. Adding in more family time, less screen time and more positivity can all help them develop critical thinking skills and make decisions themselves.

“We don’t have to argue with the definition of whatever their belief on social media is. We’re just setting the distinction of our household rules, and that’s where the [research] highlighted that those household rules and the ways that we operate really do have an influence,” Dumler said.

Teens are looking for confidence and connection, which is why some turn to the manosphere in the first place. “So, if we think about other ways we can help meet that need and really balance social media or other extreme messages, we’re going to be moving in the right direction,” she added.
