#3575. Grande Nef de l'Île-des-Vannes Renovation / Chatillon Architectes

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Text description provided by the architects. Following seven years of closure, Chatillon Architectes implements contemporary solutions and state-of-the-art facilities, bringing this distinctive building back to life, first as an Olympic training site and then for its subsequent return to the local community. The first images of the restored Grande Nef de l'Île-des-Vannes sports complex in Paris have been revealed ahead of its role as an Olympic training facility. Featuring a sweeping parabolic roof and striking translucent side paneling, the unique structure was designed by Anatole Kopp, Lucien Metrich, and Pierre Chazanoff in 1971, and closed in 2018 due to decay and accessibility issues. Adapting the building for contemporary use, Chatillon Architectes has fully restored the structure, installing contemporary energy solutions, structural repairs, universal accessibility upgrades and state-of-the-art facilities, from acoustic paneling to sports flooring to lighting fixtures.

#2787. 3 Sports Science Hacks That Don’t Work

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

It’s more fun for everyone when a study works. I love writing about research suggesting that, say, drinking beet juice or zapping your brain with electricity or simply smiling will make you faster.

But this weakness—which, to be fair, I share with just about everyone—has a cost. If I only write about positive studies, we get the impression that pretty much everything works. Ten scientists might study a new supplement, and nine of them find it doesn’t work, but if we only read about the tenth study, we assume the new supplement is awesome. This is publication bias in action.

So, in recognition of this danger, I’m sharing here the results of three studies that didn’t work—or at least, where my first reaction to the findings was “Oh, that’s too bad.” In reality, all three of these studies were successful, because they answered a question that researchers were wondering about. There’s some valuable data and interesting insights here—just not necessarily the ones we were hoping for.

First up is a test of the idea that ketones boost recovery from hard training or competition. I first wrote about ketones back in 2018 when they were all the rage in the pro cycling peloton as a potential performance-booster. That idea has gone out of fashion, but subsequent research (which I wrote about here) has suggested that the real magic of ketones might be in enhancing recovery and warding off overtraining.

The new study, which appears in Medicine and Science in Sports and Exercise, comes from the same Belgian group at KU Leuven that has pioneered the idea of ketones for recovery. They decided to put their theoretical ideas to the test by having 18 cyclists complete a brutal eight-day simulated cycling stage race, modeled after the Absa Cape Epic mountain bike tour. The cyclists were given either KetoneAid ketones or a placebo after each stage and just before bed each evening.

The researchers hoped that the ketones would accelerate post-exercise recovery, enhance sleep time and quality, and preserve physiological and cognitive function in the face of fatigue. Put all this together, and they hoped the cyclists would go faster on ketones. In the end, none of this happened. The cyclists got tired and slower, but there was no difference between the ketone and placebo groups.

There are a few possible explanations. One is that the sample size was too small to pick up differences. Another is that eight days of racing more than three hours per day didn’t fatigue the cyclists enough for the benefits of ketones to show up. A further possibility is that, despite earlier promising studies, ketones simply don’t make a practical difference in the real world. At this point, we don’t know which of these is correct. But the results should put a ceiling on our expectations: even if ketones work to fight cumulative fatigue, the effect can’t be enormous or it would have shown up here.

Sign up for a ten-part email series from Alex Hutchinson on how to train, fuel, and recover. Each week, Alex covers a new topic, including strength training, supplements, and more!

What used to be called low-level laser therapy (or sometimes red light therapy) has now been rebranded as photobiomodulation, because you can use LEDs instead of lasers. The idea is that certain wavelengths of light in the red and infrared range can interact with your cells to produce beneficial effects. It’s been around for a long time: Hungarian scientists in the 1960s claimed that it accelerated hair growth and wound healing in mice; more recently, Alberto Salazar reportedly shipped a full-body photobiomodulation unit to Brazil for his athletes to use before the Rio Olympics in 2016.

There are a lot of claims about what miracles this technology can perform, but a big one is that the relevant wavelengths of light are absorbed by a specific enzyme in your mitochondria, boosting mitochondrial function and other metabolic pathways. This has been demonstrated in test-tube experiments, but never in humans—so Luc van Loon’s research group at the University of Maastricht decided to test the claim, publishing the results in Medicine and Science in Sports and Exercise.

The study involved 12 volunteers who got three five-minute doses of photobiomodulation with a K-Laser Cube Plus 30 on one leg, and three placebo doses with the light turned off on the other leg. The researchers took muscle biopsies and skin samples to test mitochondrial function and the expression of genes related to muscle metabolism half an hour after the treatment.

The results are easy to sum up: there was no difference between legs, other than a bit of surface warming at the skin. The claims for this kind of technology are so broad (Achilles tendinopathy! lumbar discopathy! pressure ulcers! range of motion!) that a single study can’t possibly debunk all of them. But these results should give you pause.

This study in the Journal of Sports Sciences, from a French group led by Thomas Rupp and Sébastien Racinais, is a “glass half-empty or half-full?” test. They put 22 volunteers through a simulated soccer game, then gave them 25 minutes in either an ice bath (at 57 degrees Fahrenheit) or hot tub (at 108 degrees Fahrenheit) immediately afterwards, a day later, and two days later. They tested soreness, muscle fatigue, and neuromuscular function.

Once again, the results are easy to sum up: there were no differences between the groups. To me, the logical conclusion is that neither of them does anything. It seems highly unlikely that doing a thing, and also doing the opposite of that thing, will both produce positive effects. Whatever cellular mechanism you think is at play here—changes in blood flow, inflammatory signals, antioxidant levels, or whatever—cold and heat should have opposite effects. If they both produce the same results, those changes probably don’t matter.

That’s not how the researchers frame it, though. The actual purpose of the study was to test hot-tubbing for recovery; the ice bath group was the “standard of care” control group. If you assume that ice baths are effective, then the fact that hot-tubbing produces the same result means that it’s “a practical and effective alternative” for recovery, as the paper concludes.

Personally, much as I love how ice baths feel after a hard workout, I’m not sold on their physiological benefits. There’s still debate among scientists, and I’m open to being convinced, but it looks to me like a placebo—which means “just as good as an ice bath” is a half-empty glass.

For more Sweat Science, sign up for the email newsletter and check out my new book The Explorer’s Gene: Why We Seek Big Challenges, New Flavors, and the Blank Spots on the Map.

#2258. The Best Fire Starters of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Old Houses AREN’T Scary: What We’ve Learned & Loved About Them

By Glenda Taylor, Mark Wolfe, Additional research provided by Tony Carrick, Donna Boyle Schwartz

Published Oct 27, 2026 9:00 AM EDT

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Fire starters make it easy for anyone to start a roaring fire. Rather than having to wrestle with damp wood or crumpled-up newspapers to get the first flames going, users can employ a fire starter to achieve this with a single match or the flick of a lighter. The catch is that these outdoor living aids aren’t all created equal.

Some fire starters stand out for their ability to help ignite a fire rapidly and effortlessly, while others are difficult to light or won’t stay lit long enough to ignite a wood fire. We wanted to know which ones were worth buying, so we tested them in our own fireplaces, wood-burning stoves, and fire pits, eventually choosing the Superior Trading Co. All-Natural Fire Starter as our favorite for its nontoxic ingredients and effective performance. Ahead, we’ll cover how the following products earned a place in our lineup of the best fire starters.

Bottom line: Discover the best fire starters of 2026, tested for performance, ease of use, and environmental impact, with options for every need and budget.

In total, we tested 15 different fire starters: 12 small accelerant products, and three ferro rods that could fit into a pocket on a camping or hiking trip. Our main goal was to determine which fire starters performed the best in their relative categories. We didn’t test matches or lighters, but we did use them to light the accelerant fire starters. Not every product we tested made the cut for this guide. Each of the products below earned a spot by being a top performer in our hands-on testing.

These Superior Trading Co. fire starters are safe, easy to use, and effective. We found that just one pod was typically all we needed to get a log fire lit and burning. Made from wood shavings and food-grade wax, the pods are odorless, nontoxic, and environmentally safe, making them ideal for everything from indoor fireplaces to camping bonfires.

It took about 4 seconds for the pod to catch fire when we lit it with a single match. It started burning slowly, but within 5 minutes, the flames were about 8 inches tall, and the pod continued burning for about 15 minutes, which was plenty of time for the logs to catch fire.

The manufacturer lists these fire starters as waterproof, so we dropped a couple in a glass of water before testing them. We couldn’t get them to light immediately, but that was understandable. Once they dried out, they lit quickly and burned well. We used them in a fireplace and a charcoal grill. We placed one Superior Trading Co. fire-starter pod under the charcoal basket in our grill and lit it. By the time the flame from the pod burned down, the charcoal was lit.

Get the Superior Trading Co. fire starters at Amazon or Superior Trading Co.

Forget about newspapers and kindling—we were able to get a real wood fire going using just one small Lightning Nugget. It took three matches to light the nugget, but once lit, it burned brightly with a hot flame about 6 inches high for a little more than 10 minutes.

The nuggets are made from a blended composition of all-natural, recycled, and nontoxic flammable materials, including pitchwood sawdust and paraffin wax. Pitchwood is a type of pine that contains natural resins that are flammable.

When burned, these nuggets give off a slight pinewood scent, making them suitable for fireplaces and campfires. We were hoping for more of an aroma, but it was pretty weak. Each box comes with 50 nuggets, and since it takes just one nugget to light most fires, one box goes a long way. They’re not suitable for use in grills, however, since they are scented.

Get the Lightning Nuggets fire starters at Amazon, Ace Hardware, or Tractor Supply Co.

Buyers don’t have to spend a lot to get quality fire starters. With 24 fire starter squares in a box, this option from Rutland is one of the more affordable fire-starting options.

We placed a couple of Rutland fire starter squares beneath logs in our fireplace. They lit quickly—within 1 or 2 seconds of touching them with a lit match. The flames rapidly engulfed the squares and continued burning for about 8 minutes, which was more than enough time to ignite the logs in the fireplace.

The fire starter squares are made from recycled wood chips, paper fiber, and wax. Although the manufacturer claims they’ll light when wet, we couldn’t get them to light after we dropped them in a glass of water and then took them out. After about 15 minutes, however, they were dry enough to light.

These are nontoxic fire starters, and we couldn’t detect any odor as they burned, making them suitable for starting charcoal fires for grilling food. It took two Rutland squares to get the charcoal in our grill started; the first one burned out before the charcoal caught, so we slipped a second one under the charcoal basket and lit it. The briquettes caught fire a few minutes after that.

Get the Rutland fire starters at Tractor Supply Co.

Nature offers excellent fire starters, but gathering traditional materials such as birch bark, tinder fungus, or cattail fluff is not always practical. One of the best natural fire starters, fatwood, comes from naturally resin-saturated pinewood. These fatwood fire starter sticks from Better Wood Products catch fire quickly, even when wet, and burn longer and hotter than factory-made fire starters. Our 10-pound bundle of fatwood contained 160 sticks, each measuring 8.25 inches long and about .5 inches in diameter.

After submerging two sticks in a bucket of water for 5 minutes, we pulled them out, shook off the water, and held a lit match against them. Within 5 seconds, both sticks were ablaze like giant matchsticks. They appeared to burn more intensely than typical wood shavings or compressed fiber and wax fire starters. Compared with those, these fatwood starters produced a larger flame with more heat—enough that we could feel it from 3 feet away. We were also impressed by the 14-minute burn time, but the downside was an excessive amount of smoke and pungent pine aroma.

Possibly the most intriguing quality of a fatwood fire starter is its versatility. The seller recommends lighting two sticks at a time with a match as an easy way to get a fire going with dry, seasoned firewood. We experimented with smaller amounts and managed to start one with just half a stick. Another time, we made a nest of fine fatwood shavings and lit it with sparks off of the ferro rod. However you light it, this stuff just works.

Get the Better Wood Products fire starters at Amazon.

Getting back to basics by creating a fire with friction no longer means using flint and steel; today’s outdoors enthusiasts trust ferrocerium alloy to supply the sparks. In skilled hands, a quality ferro rod and some dry tinder are all it takes to build up a roaring fire in no time. We preferred the Exotac FireRod XL over competing models, not least because of the massive shower of sparks it produced. We also appreciated its precision-machined aluminum handle with a watertight tinder chamber and the fact that the rod can be unthreaded from the body and replaced with a new one if needed. It did not come with a striker, but the spine of a fixed blade or lock-back folding knife served that role perfectly.

Before the ferro rod could make sparks, we had to scrape away some of the black surface coating. We then dropped it in the water bucket to find out if the tinder chamber was truly watertight. It was. Next, we dried our hands, opened the tinder chamber, and pulled out one of the three included cotton fiber tinder pieces. We loosened the fibers and placed the tinder in a bundle of dry pine needles shaped like a bird’s nest. The very first strike sparked into the tinder and ignited it, which then lit the pine needle nest. We built the fire up with kindling from there.

We’ve tried smaller ferro rods, such as spring-loaded UST BlastMatch, but the FireRod XL’s large rod and chunky handle made it easier to use with gloves, and it produced a larger spark shower to light the tinder. Three of the six fires we started with the FireRod XL only required a single strike. We also appreciated the built-in waterproof tinder chamber inside the handle, as this allowed it to serve as a complete fire-making kit at all times. On the downside, the precision-machined aluminum handle seems to be a costly addition, as the price was multiple times more than that of a similarly sized drilled ferro rod on a lanyard.

Get the Exotac fire starter at Amazon, Target, or Exotac.

These all-natural fire starters blend recycled pine “wood wool” (which is a fancy way of saying slivers of wood cut from logs) and stearin wax (a wax made from plant and animal materials) with no added chemicals. That makes GreenSpark’s fire starters a clean and eco-friendly way to get a fire going. We tested them to start a campfire and to create a traditional log fire in the fireplace.

To get the campfire going, we placed three GreenSpark fire starter squares on top of some small logs and lit them with a lighter. It took 3 or 4 seconds for the starters to catch. Once they were burning well, we added some small pieces of kindling and more logs. The GreenSpark squares sent up flames about 6 inches high and burned for around 8 to 9 minutes. That was plenty of time to ignite the larger logs we put on the fire.

The test in the fireplace had similar results, and we enjoyed a roaring fire there. These fire starters are billed as waterproof, so we dropped them in a glass of water and then retrieved them. We couldn’t light them immediately after we took them out of the water, but as soon as the moisture on the surface dissipated—which took about 10 minutes—we were able to light them again.

They contain all-natural ingredients, so the GreenSpark squares are also suitable for use in grills for cooking food.

Get the GreenSpark fire starters at Amazon.

Nothing lifts the mood in camp faster than a roaring campfire. Black Beard’s fire starter “ropes” are ideal for fire pits because they can be stored indefinitely, are completely weatherproof, and offer enough burn time to coax even wet firewood ablaze.

We tested a three-pack of “ropes” (which is how these come packaged) using several methods, and while a lighter and matches were the most straightforward approach, we also had success using the ferro rod. For our timed test, we cut a 1-inch section from one rope, lit an end, and stood it up like a little candle. It burned strong for 11 minutes.

Next, we cut another piece of the same size, dunked it in a bucket of water for a minute, shook it off, and lodged it beneath a mix of damp, seasoned kindling and greenwood logs that had recently been cut. Success. The residual water sizzled on the fire starter for a few moments as it began to burn. Within 5 minutes the kindling directly above the fire starter had dried and begun to catch fire. After 10 minutes, the kindling was well lit, the greenwood logs were beginning to char and smoke, and we had enough of a fire to build on.

The resulting fire was very smoky—not because of the Black Beard fire starter, but rather because of the uncured firewood. We performed this experiment to find out if the fire starter produced adequate heat long enough to actually dry out damp wood in the bottom of a damp fire pit, and it did.

Get the Black Beard fire starters at Amazon or Walmart.

When charcoal chimneys first became popular, newspapers were much more widely read—and sometimes, burned. Those who get their news online and don’t want to start the barbecue with a trash fire will be glad to learn about these quick-light fire starters from Kingsford. These simple bundles of wood shavings and paraffin wax light easily, burn for a long time, and won’t pollute food fires with questionable chemicals.

When it comes to grilling, Kingsford has been a household name for a long time, so we weren’t surprised that its hardwood fire starters ended up being one of the top performers. The average burn time with these was an impressive 13 minutes. Even after we dunked them in water, they lit within seconds and burned hot once ignited. We only needed one fire starter to get the fire going in our charcoal chimney, but some folks who are less patient may prefer to use two or three.

With an average cost of about $0.31 per fire starter ($9.99 for a pack of 32), this was one of the most affordable options. The only complaint we had was that the packaging fell apart in transit while the fire starters were being shipped to us, so we had to store them in our own plastic zipper bag.

Get the Kingsford fire starters at Amazon.

When it comes to fire starters for camping, ease of use, weather resistance, and burn time are critical factors. Compact packaging is also important. UCO’s Sweetfire fire starters combine the easy strike-starting of a match with the burn time of a waxed fiber fire starter. They come in boxes of 20.

Using this fire starter was just like using a match. We slid open the box, tore off one of the fire starters, and struck it on the side of the box. The “match head” was about four times the size of a conventional match, so it provided more than enough of a flame to get started. The individual fire starters measure 1 inch wide at the base, 2 inches from base to tip, and .5 inches thick—relatively small compared to the competition.

The burn time was pretty good at just under 10 minutes. That was more than enough time to start a fire under ideal conditions, and it worked fine in damp, drizzly conditions as well. It also performed well in our dunk test, starting on the first strike after a 5-second submersion. However, we suspect that the box with the built-in strikers could be a weak link if it absorbs too much moisture. This is a great choice for camp, but be sure to pack them in a zipper bag just in case.

Get the UCO fire starters at Amazon or Public Lands.

When the unexpected happens, a little preparation can make the difference between a rough day and an absolute disaster. Pull Start Fire was designed to start and burn for 30 minutes in all kinds of weather, and it can be lit without matches, lighters, or other devices. Just unwrap it, remove a tab, anchor it to a log, and pull a string. We’ve tried it, and it really was that simple.

This was by far the most expensive fire starter we tested on a per-fire basis. But it was also the easiest to operate in difficult conditions, such as one-handed, with hands in gloves, in high winds and rain, and so on. Each Pull Start Fire was sealed individually and, according to the manufacturer, has an indefinite shelf life. We held it underwater for 60 seconds and the cellophane wrapper didn’t leak. Then we opened the wrapper and followed the directions to start the fire.

This fire starter burns aggressively at first, like a roadside emergency flare, until the materials inside the inner packaging catch fire. In our test, it burned for 32 minutes with no additional fuel. On the second try, we placed it beneath a pile of moist but moderately cured 3-inch-thick firewood logs with no kindling. As the Pull Start Fire burned, it dried the wood directly above it. Within 10 minutes some of the damp logs began to burn, and after 20 minutes they were well engulfed. Bottom line, if we were the character in Jack London’s story To Build a Fire, we would rather have a couple of Pull Start Fires than a pack of matches.

Get the Pull Start Fire fire starters at Amazon, Lowe’s, or Lehman’s.

The fire starters that earned a spot in our lineup are all relatively inexpensive, and most are made from natural ingredients. For those who want something that not only ignites and burns reliably but looks good, too, consider making pine cone fire starters.

Making pine cone starters is fairly simple. It requires melting beeswax in the top of a double boiler and then dipping the cones two or three times until they have a nice wax layer. For fun, try rolling them in Epsom salt after the final dip while they’re still warm to lend them a frosty look. For scented pine cone fire starters, add natural essential oils to the melted wax, such as pine or cinnamon.

We considered a variety of factors when deciding which fire starters to test. We selected starters that would burn long enough to ignite wood or charcoal without needing to add other burnable materials, such as wadded-up paper or cardboard. A fire starter should also be easy to use, so we chose options that light easily with a match or lighter—with the exception of the ferro rods we tested, which function a bit differently.

During our testing, we followed the manufacturers’ instructions, placing one or two fire starters as suggested and using them to start the recommended fire types. For many of the tests, we used a traditional fireplace, but we also used a fire pit, a chiminea, and a wood-burning stove if the directions recommended using the fire starters with those appliances. We also tested a few fire starters to see how well they worked for lighting charcoal—but only if they were listed as safe to use for grilling food.

If a fire starter was advertised as waterproof, we dropped it in a glass of water and then attempted to light it. We determined that many waterproof fire starters aren’t genuinely waterproof but rather water-resistant. In other words, they needed a little time to dry out before lighting.

We timed how long it took for a fire starter to catch fire from a match or lighter, how tall its flames were, and how long it burned before extinguishing. We discovered a fire starter needed to remain lit for 5 to 9 minutes, at minimum, to fully ignite the rest of the wood and logs in a fireplace.

The only fire starter we tested differently was the ferro rod, which is intended to ignite finely textured, highly flammable tinder via sparks. We tested it on cotton-fiber tinder that was included with the rod, and then we built up the fire with progressively larger twigs and kindling.

During the testing phase, we awarded points based on how well each fire starter performed the tasks. After testing, we added and averaged the scores and compared packaging, prices, dimensions, and other factors to categorize the products for different applications.

Aside from considering the various types of fire starters, there are other features that shoppers will want to consider, such as ease of use and convenience. While any fire starter will make starting a fire easier than using tinder, not all fire starters are suitable for all types of fires.

In general, fire starters fall into two camps: flame/spark producers or accelerants. Shoppers already know about lighters and matches, but they might not be familiar with other types of flame/spark producers, such as ferro rods.

Accelerants are usually what buyers are usually looking for when shopping for fire starters, and these products are used in conjunction with typical fire-building materials, such as seasoned logs. An accelerant fire starter is placed beneath the other materials and then ignited with a match, lighter, ferro rod, or other flame/spark producers. Here’s the rundown on all of them.

A lighter consists of a metal or plastic container filled with a flammable liquid or compressed gas. A lighter lights by simultaneously creating a spark with the flint wheel while releasing a controlled amount of the fuel with a button. Unlike other methods, a lighter creates an actual flame, making it significantly easier to start a fire.

Matches are the tried-and-true method of starting a fire. Like a lighter, a match creates an actual flame rather than a spark, making it an easier way to start a fire. However, many matches are susceptible to water damage.

A ferro rod is a pencil-shaped piece of ferrocerium alloy. When a steel or carbide striker is scraped or struck against the rod, tiny shavings of ferrocerium oxidize and ignite as a result of the friction, burning at a high temperature of more than 5,000 degrees Fahrenheit.

Flint and steel consist of a steel striker and a piece of natural flint. When the two are struck together, they produce heated pieces of steel in the form of sparks that ignite a piece of cloth or tinder, starting the fire.

A magnesium fire starter typically consists of a small block of soft magnesium with a ferro rod embedded into one edge. The magnesium, which burns at an intense 2,500 degrees Fahrenheit, is an accelerant that works with the tinder to quickly build a fire. With a knife or the striker that came with the kit, scrape a quarter-size pile of magnesium into the tinder pile, then use the embedded ferro rod and striker to light the tinder. This will ignite the magnesium fragments, which will help set fire to twigs and kindling.

Accelerants are the most popular fire-starter products, and we tested a number of these. Nuggets and squares consisting of flammable oils, wax, and recycled paper and wood are the easiest to use; however, they require a flame ignition source, such as a match or a lighter. Once lit, these fire starters typically create tall, hot flames that light the materials above them.

As handy as fire starters may be, their usefulness is, to a degree, dependent on their convenience. Look for fire starters that are lightweight and compact, ranging from 1 to 6 inches in length. Typically sold in cases of 12 or 24, these pocket-size pouches, pods, or cubes can be stored in plain sight or easily tucked out of sight. Small, lightweight, neatly packed products are also more portable.

Most fire starters consist of wax, oil, and dense recycled wood, which holds up well in wet weather. Some fire starters are waterproof—drop a ferro rod in water, for example, and it won’t be affected. Just dry it off and it will still throw a spark that can ignite dry material. Fire starters also tend to burn at high temperatures of 800 degrees Fahrenheit or more, which allows them to better withstand windy and damp conditions.

Fire starters are meant to simplify the sometimes painstaking process of starting a fire by providing a persistent flame that can ignite nearby kindling. To do so, the fire starter must burn long enough to establish the fire. The longer the burn time, the more likely users can achieve a robust blaze. Our tests determined that most burn for an average of 3 to 15 minutes.

Earlier types of fire starters were typically infused with flammable compounds, which gave them a chemical smell. But the best fire starters in this day and age are nearly odorless. Many people appreciate the less noxious types of fire starters. However, fire starters that contain natural essential oils can lend a pleasing scent to the product that enhances the user’s experience without being toxic.

The best fire starters tend to be made of environmentally friendly materials, be it wood waste (sawdust or shavings), natural nontoxic wax, recycled paper, or waste textile fibers. Using fire starters typically results in faster and more complete ignition, which reduces airborne soot and smoke. Don’t miss our DIY fire-starter option above to find out how to make eco-friendly fire-starting accelerants from pine cones and beeswax.

A key to using a fire starter effectively is understanding how to arrange logs and kindling for the fire. Ahead, learn about how to build a fire correctly and use a fire starter to light it quickly.

To use a fire starter, begin by setting up your wood. If building a wood fire, you’ll need some kindling, which will ignite more quickly than full-size logs. Place the fire starter under the kindling or charcoal, leaving room so that you can reach the fire starter with a match or lighter. Light the fire starter and wait as it begins to burn and ignite the kindling.

To start a fire in a fire pit, gather wood, kindling, and a fire starter. Stack small pieces of kindling on top of one another, about four layers high, until you’ve formed a shape resembling a log cabin. Place the fire starter in the middle of your log cabin, pile kindling loosely in the middle, and then light the fire starter.

Begin by placing a layer of kindling and two or three small pieces of firewood on the fireplace grate, then place your fire starter in the space beneath the grate. Lighting the fire starter will ignite the kindling above it, which will, in turn, ignite the logs. Add larger logs as needed to keep the fire burning.

Fire starters are made up of materials that include recycled wood fibers, paper, oil, and wax that do not expire; hence, most fire starters do not have a shelf life. They may be attractive to insects, rodents, or other pests, so it is best to store them in airtight containers. Also, since heat may degrade the waxes and oils that are often used in these products, be sure to store them away from excessive heat.

#665. Inside the New D.C. Restaurant That Only Sells Five Curry Steak Frites Every Night

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

In a Georgetown alleyway, Alfie’s serves northern Thai dishes that are built around local ingredients

The hottest new spot in Washington, D.C. focuses on the traditional heat-packed cooking methods of northern Thailand, while still using local ingredients from around the DMV. Alfie’s, from chefs Justin Ahn and Alex McCoy, started as a pop-up in Petworth in 2017 before traveling around the world, serving diners in New York and London. This year, the restaurant put down permanent roots in a Georgetown alleyway. The co-owners led us through a day in the new kitchen, preparing everything from hand-cut green papaya in som tam salad to the limited-quantity Thai steak frites with a massaman curry peppercorn sauce.In the video, the kitchen team breaks down rib-eye steaks for their take on a steak au poivre, which they only make five of each night. The steaks are carefully seared for each order, with the au poivre massaman curry sauce is finished in the pan with the leftover beef fat. Classic “bar fries” are served up with the massive 26-ounce rib-eye.

Elsewhere in the kitchen, another chef carves green papayas. The irregular slices of papaya get tossed in a funky, spicy dressing for each order. Ko calls it the “most labor intensive dish on the line;” each order is carefully mixed with fresh tomatoes, peanuts, and a palm syrup and tamarind-heavy dressing. A rich pork belly curry is simmering in the background. Gaeng hung lay (a traditional northern Thai curry) cooks down large pieces of pork belly to get that fatty layer, with pickled garlic and pineapple balancing out the rich flavors of curry paste, tamarind, and fish sauce.

Right before service, the chefs begin prepping herbs and sticky rice that has been soaking overnight and is quickly steamed for that malleable, glutinous texture. McCoy talks about how being a chef is more of a lifestyle than a job, but there’s immense payoff when you get to see diners enjoy your hard work every night. He hopes that Alfie’s will become a place where “you can just forget where you are, have a good time, and just lose yourself in the experience and the food.”

#3419. Frank Gagliano, Legendary Coach of Olympians and Sub-4 Milers, Dies at 89

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

A former football player, “Gags” became one of track and field’s most prolific coaches over a career that spanned 60+ years.

Frank Gagliano, the famed middle-distance and distance coach who guided more than 40 sub-4:00 milers, 15 Olympians, and a world championship medalist, has died. Gags, as he was affectionately known, was 89.

Gagliano is survived by his wife, Roberta, and four children, along with multiple grandchildren.

One of track and field and distance running’s most prolific coaches, Gagliano never officially retired, coaching athletes until around 2023. Gagliano influenced the sport at the scholastic, collegiate, and professional levels. He coached an NCAA championship team at Manhattan College, then made stops at Rutgers and Georgetown before working with pro groups in Washington, D.C.; Palo Alto, California; Eugene, Oregon; and New York. He forged deep connections with athletes at each stop.

“He made you feel like you were the only athlete he cared about,” said Diljeet Taylor, who ran with Gagliano in Palo Alto and is currently the women’s coach at Brigham Young University. “Then you realized he did that with pretty much everyone he coached. It’s an amazing thing, really.”

Francis X. Gagliano was born on March 20, 1937, in the Bronx. Growing up on East 237th Street with his parents, Perry and Clara, Gagliano’s childhood was filled with athletics. Dee Lycett, Gags’s only sister, said the house was a revolving door of athletes. “We grew up in an Italian household,” she told Runner’s World in a 2016 profile. “My mother made sure there was lots of food.”

After a successful high school football career as quarterback with Mount Saint Michael Academy, Gagliano attended Richmond College in Virginia. “Five years and four summers,” he liked to joke about how long it took him to graduate. He spent two of those summers at Officer Candidates School in Quantico, Virginia, however, and he spent eight years as a reservist in the Marine Corps beginning in 1956. He was never called up.

After graduating from Richmond, Gagliano was signed by the Canadian Football League’s Hamilton Tiger-Cats. In 1960, he was sent down to the London Lords, a minor league team. Selling coffee and soda at a construction site during the day and playing football at night while splitting time at quarterback was not the life Gagliano wanted, so he moved back to the U.S.

There was also someone he missed back home. Roberta “Robbie” Arnold, whom Gagliano had met as a junior at Richmond, had stayed in touch with Gagliano during his time in Canada. They were married within a year of his return to the U.S., in 1961.

He took a job teaching English and math and coaching track at Roselle Catholic High School in Roselle, New Jersey, that same year. School officials promised him he would become the football coach once the school got a team.

Gagliano never coached football at Roselle Catholic, where even today there is no football team, and the world of track and field had a fiery new coach who would stick around for a while. He left Roselle Catholic in 1969 for an assistant track coach job at Manhattan College and took the head coach position at Rutgers in 1974.

“His early years are the wonder years,” said Ron Speirs in the 2016 Runner’s World article, who ran for Gagliano and Rutgers and would become his first sub-4:00 miler in 1977. “It’s a wonder he survived.”

Vin Lanana, a friend and famed track coach in his own right, remembered the first time he saw Gagliano when Gags was at Manhattan. “He was coaching the throwers and I heard this bellowing voice yelling at somebody out on the field. He was intense.”

The intensity led to results. Manhattan won the 1973 NCAA indoor championship, and Gagliano coached Rutgers steeplechaser Mike Roche to the Olympic team in 1976. Gagliano was the head coach of Georgetown from 1983 to 1999, guiding multiple Penn Relays championship teams and Olympians.

In 1993, while still coaching at Georgetown, Gagliano started the Reebok Enclave, his first foray into coaching a professional group with athletes like Steve Holman and Rich Kenah, who would run 1:43.38 for 800 meters and win bronze at the 1997 indoor and outdoor world track and field championships. Gagliano took the success of the Enclave and turned it into a job in Palo Alto, California, with the Nike Farm Team in 2001. Under his guidance, Nicole Teter set the American record in the 800 meters indoors. Five years later, Lanana convinced Gagliano to give Eugene, Oregon, a try, and Gags was off to one more professional group.

Nick Symmonds winning the 800 meters at the 2008 Olympic Trials to lead an Oregon sweep was the lasting image from Gags’s time in Oregon—Gagliano coached Symmonds and third-place finisher Christian Smith, while the University of Oregon’s Andrew Wheating finished second. That time was cut short. In 2009, Gagliano’s daughter in law fell ill, and he returned to the East Coast to help his family.

He couldn’t keep from coaching, however, and in 2010 began working with Erin Donohue, a 2008 Olympian in the 1500 meters. More and more athletes sought out Gags’s tutelage on the East Coast. The group ballooned and eventually became the New Jersey-New York Track Club (NJNYTC), Gagliano’s final coaching stop.

Every Monday and Friday from 2010 to 2016, he would drive from his home in Rye, New York, to Rutgers University to coach the group. Julie Culley and Donn Cabral made the 2012 and 2016 Olympic teams, respectively.

His coaching was extremely successful with the women of NJNYTC, who had at least one runner finish in the top three at the U.S. outdoor championships from 2011 to 2015. Lanana credited that success to Gagliano’s desire to see an athlete improve, no matter which level they were at. “Sometimes people will say, ‘I’m coach of the men, and I coach the women also.’ I don’t think that enters Gags’s mind space,” Lananna told Runner’s World in 2016. “He genuinely appreciates and values the performances of an athlete, period.”

Gagliano laughed when asked about his success with the women. “I just treat them as human beings,” he says. “It’s the same for men and women: workouts and communication and motivation.”

Plus, Gags cared. For many of the athletes he coached, he became family. Gags and Robbie were two of the first people many called when they had big life news. If an athlete was passing through Rye, Gagliano would have a sandwich from the deli waiting for them. Robbie made sure to send out Christmas cards every year, even including your dog on the address if she had met them. Even once an athlete was done running, Gags would leave his quick, gruff voicemails. “Hi. Gag. Call me.” And the athletes would always call back.

Gagliano, who had hinted that he would take a step back from coaching after the 2016 Olympic Trials, continued to bring on new athletes for the next Olympic cycle. NJNYTC moved to Westchester County, New York, and Gags couldn’t help but stay involved. The COVID-19 pandemic pushed back the 2020 Olympic Trials, and NJNYTC lost its Hoka One One sponsorship at the end of the year. Still, Gagliano met with the few athletes who stuck around to train in 2021.

“No, I’m not surprised [that I’m still coaching],” Gagliano said on the Morning Shakeout podcast in 2019, “because there’s two things: My heart and my mind is in it big time. And as long as those two continue to be in it … I love it, and I’m not ready to pack it in at all. Actually, I have a lot of fire in my belly.”

He would go on to coach until around 2023.

Gagliano coached hundreds, if not thousands, of athletes. From world championship medalists to a walk-on at Georgetown, Gagliano didn’t care who you were, he simply wanted to help runners get faster. He often did. His impact was more than that, however. As Diljeet Taylor said, Gagliano made his athletes feel special—like family.

#1004. How Tom Rhys Harries Got Ripped for Clayface Despite a Torn ACL

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

In DC’s Clayface, the film’s titular anti-hero is a guy whose body learns to do some pretty weird stuff. Taking on the role of the Batman villain, Tom Rhys Harries plays a character able to shape-shift at will, often with horrific results (no spoilers here).

But before he could think about taking on the role of actor Matt Hagen (whose brutal beating by the mob eventually leads him to become his malleable alter ego), Rhys Harries had to contend with the limitations of his own body.

“Tom approached me three months before shooting began,” says Dan Avasilcai, sports injuries therapist and strength coach to the likes of Ralph Fiennes, and Harris Dickinson.

The training plan included all the standard stuff movie stars do to get in shape, but with one massive caveat. “Tom dropped a massive bomb,” says Avasilcai. "Just weeks prior, he had completely torn the ACL in his right leg.”

As a coach and sports injuries therapist, Avasilcai was up for the challenge of building a solid physique while navigating severe muscle atrophy and a major injury.

Having worked together on the other Odyssey movie, The Return, a few years back, the pair had a level of trust that let them hit the ground running (or, limping). Still, things didn’t look great when they reconnected for the new project. “The muscle atrophy was already highly visible in my initial assessment,” Avasilcai says, adding that Rhys Harries’ right leg was noticeably thinner and that the injury was impacting how he walked.

To stabilize the leg without risking delaying the filming schedule, they set about working on controlled isolation machines where movement patterns could be strictly dictated. For added safety, they used a leg brace for certain exercises.

“Being in the gym allowed us to control every variable,” says Avasilcai. Initially, they worked through the following sets during each session:

As Rhys Harries’ strength and confidence recovered, they were able to introduce compound movements and lifts (like controlled squats and lunges to build size) without putting too much stress on the ACL. At the same time, they worked on his upper body with heavy resistance workouts.

As for the diet, Avasilcai reveals that he was under a secret directive from the studio not to let Rhys Harries get so lean that he couldn’t still fit into the Clayface prosthetics. A typical day’s eating for Rhys Harries might include two eggs and 75 grams of salmon at 7:30 a.m., a mid-morning protein bar, a chicken salad lunch, chicken, veg and rice mid-afternoon, a protein shake, then an 8 p.m. dinner of steak and vegetables. In the end, he was down to 6% body fat.

“Coaching him has been an absolute privilege,” says Avasilcai. “Preparing for this role required a deep level of trust. Even while managing a torn ACL, his dedication and drive never wavered—especially on leg days.”

#3455. 6 Lactate Threshold Workouts to Build Speed Endurance and Race-Day Confidence

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Train your body to run faster for longer with these key runs.

Lactate threshold workouts are one of the most important parts of a distance-running training plan. Putting your body through this type of exercise will help you run faster and run farther.

“Lactate threshold training means building confidence,” says New York City-based USATF- and RRCA-certified run coach Kai Ng. “It means getting used to being comfortable with being uncomfortable.”

To get you familiar with these workouts and how to implement them into your training plan, Runner’s World connected with three certified run coaches and collected their top recommended threshold workouts that improve your speed endurance.

Working to improve your lactate threshold makes more difficult paces feel less exhausting, and therefore, makes you a more efficient and faster runner.

Alex Morrow, RRCA- and USATF-certified run coach and founder of Resolute Running in Birmingham, Alabama, likens the human body and its ability to flush out waste created while training at lactate threshold to a bucket with a hole in the bottom. “As you run, waste [the lactate you produce while running] is being thrown into that bucket,” Morrow explains. “As long as you’re running at the right pace, the waste going in the bucket drains right out the bottom, and you’re fine. When you pass that lactate threshold—you put too much waste in the bucket—it overflows and you slow down. Lactate threshold training creates a bigger bucket and a bigger hole at the bottom of that bucket.”

By building up your body’s speed endurance, you also boost your self-confidence as you maintain your pace during these tough workouts, and gradually go farther and farther while holding the pace. Ng says that experiencing this dose of self-confidence during a threshold workout will give you the reassurance that, come race day, you’ll be able to go out there and crush your goal speed.

Need more convincing to add these to your schedule? “Lactate threshold is the most important determinant of success in endurance-related activities and events, and the main goal of endurance training programs should be the improvement of this parameter,” according to an article published by the University of New Mexico by Len Kravitz, PhD, CSCS and Lance Dalleck, PhD.

Your lactate threshold is the exercise intensity level at which lactate—a byproduct of exercise that forms when creating energy from glucose, especially without sufficient oxygen—accumulates in the blood faster than you can remove it. At its core, it’s the line between low- and high-intensity exercise.

To find your lactate threshold pace, you could head to a lab. But for a more budget-friendly option, you can calculate this pace using a practical field test. Following a warmup, you’ll run at your best possible intensity for 30 minutes and use your average running speed during that effort to calculate your ideal threshold pace.

Generally speaking, runners find their lactate threshold pace to be about a 7 or 8 out of 10 effort on a rate of perceived exertion (RPE) scale, an effort broadly described as comfortably hard. The coaches we spoke with also describe this effort as one you can sustain for one hour, but not longer.

The key is going at an effort you can sustain—it’s not all out. “There’s something in us that we all want to go faster,” Tia Accetta, Run Tuscon co-founder and RRCA-certified run coach, tells Runner’s World. “But, with this lactate threshold training, don’t.”

First, it’s important to note the difference between tempo runs and lactate threshold workouts. Ng describes the term tempo run as an overarching umbrella when discussing workouts designed for specific paces. “Tempo runs don’t have to be at threshold [pace],” Ng explains. “But lactate threshold runs are one of the many types of tempo runs. Under the umbrella, you can also run [tempo runs] at marathon pace or maybe half marathon pace.”

To add threshold workouts to your training, we asked experts for their favorites. From short repetitions to 15-plus-mile days, these are the threshold runs that build speed endurance for every type of runner.

Why it works: Intervals work well for those new to threshold work, because the breaks between reps give runners time to reflect on how the previous rep went and also allow for an adjustment of pace in future reps.

Morrow also explains that many runners who are just starting to train at lactate threshold pace tend to struggle holding the pace for 20 to 30 minutes. So he eases them into it with intervals.

The versatility of these workouts means they benefit those training for 5Ks up to marathons.

Why it works: “Breaking down tempo runs into longer time-based intervals is a good way to practice,” says Accetta.

She adds that for 5K runners training for the shorter distance, just two of these eight-minute intervals could constitute a workout, whereas for marathon runners, four or even five eight-minute intervals at lactate threshold pace gets them a better payoff.

Why it works: Once you’re comfortable with lactate threshold pace and want to sprinkle longer, more sustained efforts into your training plan, all three coaches recommend this steady-state threshold run. “It’s simple, easy, and everybody loves doing it,” says Morrow. “It also teaches you how to handle that low-grade discomfort, which is what distance running is all about.”

Eliminating the breaks between intervals forces runners to battle through minor fatigue, as Morrow describes, and stick with their comfortably-hard pace.

Ng adds that all workouts are designed to simulate parts of a whole, and in this case, training your body to run at lactate threshold pace for 20 to 30 minutes teaches you how to push through those tough miles of any race.

Why it works: At a quick glance, progression workouts seem daunting, considering they cover the most mileage. However, changing paces throughout a long workout can be one of the best ways to train your body to maintain a pace during the later stages of a race like the marathon, says Accetta.

Keep in mind the first workout below requires a minimum of running about 15 miles, so you definitely want to build up to this progression. It’s especially effective for experienced marathoners, as it teaches you to run through fatigue and discomfort—both necessary for race day. “It’s not always fun, but it’s extremely effective,” says Morrow.

Why it works: “To me, this is just a really good representation of fatigued legs and getting tired, but still maintaining a threshold effort in the middle,” Accetta says. “I feel like everyone, after they do it, they have a good dose of confidence.”

This nine-mile workout, while employing a more gradual increase and decrease in pace than Morrow’s progression workout above, is considerably shorter in overall distance. You’ll still receive the benefits of simulating midrace fatigue, while sustaining your lactate threshold pace, prepping you for race day.

Why it works: Yes, you can incorporate hills into your lactate threshold workout. Morrow says he recommends hill repeats to runners who may want to take a step back from heavy mileage or don’t feel confident enough to tackle a full steady-state lactate threshold workout.

He reminds his runners doing this exercise that the uphill effort should amount to the ideal 20 minutes recommended for threshold workouts. The jog back downhill is simply a break, and should not be calculated into the amount of effort given.

Morrow’s recommended threshold hill repeat workout gives runners the endurance benefits of threshold training, while adding in positive aspects of hill workouts, including building leg and core strength.

Because of the hill, you don’t necessarily need to run this workout at threshold pace, but rather threshold effort. Basically, you’ll run slower because you’re going uphill, but you’ll still aim to give the same effort that you feel during your lactate threshold workouts on flat ground.

“We’re always looking for that sweet spot of 20 to 30 minutes of effort,” says Morrow. “Whether that’s short intervals, long intervals, it doesn’t really matter. Get that 20 to 30 minutes.”

As the weeks of training go by and time at threshold pace transitions from being comfortably hard to just comfortable, that’s when you know you’re achieving the desired result.

“The longer the break, the more likely you are to go out too fast,” says Accetta. “Technically, you shouldn’t need a break if you’re keeping your conservative pace. You want to go into the next [rep] still feeling a little bit fatigued.”

Lactate threshold training is all about maintaining your pace for a set amount of time, so trimming your break time down so you feel fresh—but not fully recovered—is essential to a quality threshold interval workout.

Lactate threshold workouts are hard. Not hard in the sense of high-intensity effort, but hard on your mind and its perception of your body’s ability to set and maintain your comfortably hard pace.

“Trust your legs more than your mind,” Ng says. “Your mind is going to be in your way the whole time. It’s going to tell you to stop, but your job is to fight it. When that thought comes to your head, I want you to hang in there for one more minute and I guarantee you’re going to be fine. The only time you should stop is when your legs stop.”

Make sure you’re putting these workouts into the correct phase of your training plan in order to maximize the benefits. Don’t start knocking out lactate threshold workouts two weeks from your race.

Improving your lactate threshold takes time, so in order to experience the most success from these workouts, Morrow says to incorporate them once a week into the strength and endurance-building phase of your plan, or about two to four weeks into a 16-week marathon training plan.

Matt Rudisill is an Associate Service Editor who has been with Runner's World since 2025. A Nittany Lion through-and-through, Matt graduated from Penn State in 2022 with a degree in journalism and worked in communications for the university's athletic department for three years as the main contact and photographer for its nationally-ranked cross country and track & field teams. Matt was also heavily involved in communications efforts for Penn State football, men's basketball, and women's gymnastics. In his role with Runner's World, Matt has interviewed Olympians, world champions, and countless experts in the field to create service content that helps runners of all ages and experience levels train smarter and race faster. When he’s not out jogging, Matt can be found tweeting bad takes about the Phillies or watching movies.

#1291. The Brooks Running Shoes to Power Your Soccer Training

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Find the right pair for sprint work, endurance runs, and active recovery days.

Anyone who’s ever huffed and puffed their way through a pickup soccer game after not playing for a while knows how quickly the sport can expose your conditioning. A match requires repeated bursts of speed with few opportunities to catch your breath, making stamina essential from the first minute through the 90th or beyond.

The average elite soccer player covers about 6.4 miles per game. But even if you’re just a weekend rec-leaguer, cross-training can help you cover more ground without fading late in the match. Running—including sprints, endurance work, and easy jogs—should be a regular part of your routine, and the right shoes can help you get more out of every workout.

Not sure which pair you need? Brooks—a leader in performance running shoes—offers an option for every type of run. Whether you’re doing sprint repeats, heading out for a 10K, or keeping things easy on an off day, here’s how to choose the right model for your workout.

You know what you don’t want to think about when you’re doing sprint drills? Your feet—or at least how your feet feel in your shoes. During workouts built around acceleration and all-out speed, you need a shoe that delivers support, flexibility, and comfort. For the Glycerin Flex, that’s check, check, and check.

Support comes in the form of Brooks’ proprietary nitrogen-infused DNA TUNED midsole foam, which features large cells in the heel to cushion your landings and smaller cells in the forefoot for responsive toe-offs. The combination helps you land comfortably and push off with power as you pick up the pace.

For sprint work, you also need a shoe that moves with you. The Glycerin Flex’s FlexZone technology combines a midfoot flex groove with a podular forefoot design to provide flexibility and support where you need it. A thin layer of sticky rubber on the outsole adds traction as you work on your speed.

You cover plenty of ground every time you take the field for a practice or match, making endurance work essential if you want to keep going through the final whistle. Longer runs can have a place in your conditioning on the days you aren’t wearing your cleats, as long as you build gradually and don’t overdo the mileage.

That’s where the Brooks Ghost Amp comes in. This new take on the brand’s popular Ghost model combines comfort with an energized ride for days when you want to take on a few more miles. Key among its features is SpringRamp forefoot technology, which helps you push off the balls of your feet with a little extra oomph.

Also new is the shoe’s nitrogen-infused DNA HYPE cushioning, which combines two types of foam: lightweight PEBA, which provides high energy return and a springy feel, and EVA, which offers cushioning and stability. Together, they deliver a balance of softness and responsiveness.

Up top, the lightweight warp-knit upper provides breathability and comfort, with enough structure to help secure your foot when you pick up the pace. The eye-catching color options add a little more personality to your training miles.

Recovery days are an essential part of any athlete’s training, especially soccer players. An easy walk or jog can help you stay loose, ease stiffness, and give your mind and body a reset.

But slowing the pace doesn’t mean forgetting about your footwear. On active recovery days, you still want a shoe that provides support and keeps you comfortable. The Glycerin Max 2 does both. Though it’s also built for longer runs, this plush shoe is a natural fit for easier days.

The defining feature of the Glycerin Max 2 is its dramatic GlideRoll Rocker technology. Its sculpted bevel and toe spring help your foot move smoothly from heel to toe. The shoe also features a high stack of DNA TUNED foam, which provides generous cushioning underfoot to keep your legs feeling fresh and protect your body against impact.

Active recovery may not directly put the ball in the net, but the work you do between matches can help you feel ready for the next one. And the right shoe can make that work more comfortable.

#3622. Stefano Boeri Architetti to transform industrial site into residential neighbourhood in Tbilisi

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Italian studio Stefano Boeri Architetti has revealed plans to overhaul a former factory on an industrial site in Tbilisi, Georgia, and add a series of colourful housing blocks alongside it.

The studio recently won the competition to design the Loco Park masterplan, which will add housing, green spaces and community spaces to the Georgian capital.

Aiming to celebrate the site’s industrial heritage, Stefano Boeri Architetti will renovate an existing Soviet-era locomotive factory, rebuilding some of its former architectural features with a contemporary spin.

“The new Loco Park neighbourhood will combine the memory of the large Soviet-era locomotive factory with a vision of a contemporary, vibrant space that is green and open to everyone,” said Stefano Boeri Architetti founder Stefano Boeri.

“The buildings of Loco Park will be islands in an urban archipelago, distinct yet connected by a network of tree-lined pedestrian public spaces for the whole city to enjoy.”

A series of new buildings will be built on the site, providing a mix of homes and community spaces dispersed in an open green space populated with sports facilities, parks and boulevards.

Apartments will occupy the upper levels of the buildings, while shops, cafes and workshops will be located on the ground floors.

Landscaping will connect with the surrounding city, providing public space for the wider community in Tbilisi.

Renders of the Loco Park masterplan show a colourful series of block buildings of different rectangular forms, which Stefano Boeri Architetti said are designed to have their “own identity while remaining integrated into the context”.

Stefano Boeri Architetti carried out colour mapping research in Tbilisi to identify colours commonly found in the city’s architecture, which influenced the brick finishes chosen to clad the buildings, including blue-grey, terracotta, green, taupe and white.

“We set out to capture Loco Park’s identity through a simple, balanced and contemporary design, inspired by Tbilisi’s extraordinarily rich colour palette, local materials and traces of the area’s industrial history,” said Stefano Boeri Architetti partner Pietro Chiodi.

“Colour and brick thus give each building its own identity while bringing them together into a coherent whole.”

The buildings will also be distinguished by varying roofline silhouettes, including vaulted, sawtooth and pitched roofs, designed to recall the shapes of traditional industrial buildings.

Terraced roofs will be topped with planting and, elsewhere in the development, greenhouses and communal spaces will be created as spaces for residents to socialise.

Other projects recently unveiled by Stefano Boeri Architetti include plans to transform a historic transportation depot in Rome into a greenery-covered public space, and designs for a monastery in Milan with a sail-like roof.

The images are by Stefano Boeri Architetti.

#2312. 15 Things Nobody Tells You About Pawpaws

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

As soon as late summer edges toward fall in New York, I begin to lick my pawpaw-loving lips. As this native North American fruit gains mainstream recognition it has become easier to source both the fresh fruit as well as pawpaw saplings. Despite the rising tide of awareness, many people are still unfamiliar with them and have yet to taste a fresh pawpaw. And if you have lusted after the perfumed fruit but have not met one, there are few things about these green-mango lookalikes you may not know.

The pawpaw under discussion is the native North American fruit belonging to the genus Asimina. The cold-hardy common pawpaw is A. triloba, but there are over a dozen other species, with regional distributions that cleave to the eastern and Mid-Western parts of the United States, from southern Canada down to Florida.

In regions and countries with Commonwealth and British colonial histories (think Australia, England, India, South Africa, the West Indies, and more) the common name “pawpaw” refers to what anyone from the Americas calls papaya. Soft-skinned and subtropical, small or football-sized, that pawpaw, Carica papaya, has yellow to deep rose flesh and is filled in the center with little black, peppery seeds. It is available in many grocery stores, year-round.

The botanical genus name Asimina is derived from the Shawnee word for pawpaw: ha’simini. For the Shawnee, September is the month of the pawpaw moon, ha’siminikiisfwa, according to Joel Barnes, Archives Director for the Shawnee Tribe (via a West Virginia Public Radio interview in a 2020).

Until the 19th century, before they were forcibly removed by the US government, the Shawnee’s ancestral lands were in pawpaw-rich Appalachia, including current southeast Ohio.

The three-day Ohio Pawpaw Festival in southeast Ohio in September is a celebration of everything pawpaw. There are pawpaw tastings, and dozens of pawpaw dishes to choose from. Chris Chmiel is one of the founders of the festival and the co-owner (with Michelle Gorman, his wife) of Integration Acres, which has sold the fresh fruit and frozen purée, and other native wild things to the public since 1996. (The first pawpaws I tasted were shipped from Ohio’s woodlands to my Brooklyn front door in 2016.)

On 18 September, 1806, William Clark wrote, in the detailed journal that Thomas Jefferson dictated the expedition keep, that “…our party entirely out of provisions Subsisting on poppaws…the party appear perfectly contented and tell us that they can live very well on the pappaws [sic].”

There are many similar entries (reading the journal is equal parts recoil and wonder; but it is a compelling chronicle of plants encountered).

The aroma of pawpaws is strong—they will perfume a room. Their texture when ripe is creamy. Different cultivars vary in flavor: Some pawpaws taste like a kaleidoscope of passionfruit and pineapple, lychee, and mango. Many tasters mention banana; personally, I detect none, but perhaps it is the pawpaw’s soft consistency that inspires the comparison. Others taste like caramel, lightly scorched. Think crème brûlée, with tropical overtones. A fruit may taste intense, or mild. The little slippery sac around each large seed is slightly bitter, as is the flesh closest to the skin.

Unless they are very close to ripe on the branch, pawpaws do not ripen well once picked (they are not like avocados). Visiting your local tree/s after a windstorm or heavy rain is a great way to find windfalls. Their skin may bruise or discolor, but this seldom means that the fruit itself is damaged. Rather, it’s a sign of ripeness.

Pawpaws are  close cousins of custard apple (cherimoya) and soursops. All these large-seeded, soft-fleshed, deeply aromatic fruit belong to the family Annonaceae. The pawpaw is the cold-hardiest among them.

There’s no reason why you would, but don’t chew up and swallow pawpaw seeds. Eating the skin has the same effect.

While the evidence is anecdotal, there is enough chatter in online forums to suggest that some people react badly to eating pawpaws. The result is dramatic diarrhea and, sometimes, nausea. Eat pawpaws in moderation (I cut myself off at three in one sitting. Is that moderate?). Also, some pawpaw purveyors, like Integration Acres, recommend against drying the pulp for use in fruit leather, since it can cause gastrointestinal distress. The reasons for this are not understood (yet).

Some researchers posit that eating pawpaws regularly could be detrimental to the eater. The seeds and pulp of pawpaws contain acetogenins, including annonacin, which is toxic to cortical neurons. Could regular pawpaw ingestion cause neurotoxins to build up over time? A connection has been drawn between pawpaws and their cousins, custard apples, which are popular in the Caribbean, where clusters of Parkinson’s in Guadalupe have been documented, possibly linked to a diet where the custard apples feature regularly. (Annonacin was also thought to play a role in cancer-cell death. That published paper has since been retracted.)

The common pawpaw is hardy from USDA growing zones 5 through 8 (according to Cornell).  It’s the tropical-tasting fruit many cool-climate gardeners can grow. Bradley Farms in New Paltz, New York (zone 5), brings pawpaws to greenmarkets in October. While pawpaws in the wild tend to grow in clonal colonies in the woods and on woodland edges, pawpaw trees will thrive in full sun as long as they have ample moisture in summer. They like hot, humid summers and cold winters.

Sowing pawpaw seed is most successful when the seeds are planted before they dry out. Even three days of drying can reduce germination rates to 20%, according to the University of Kentucky. (If you can’t plant them at once, keep them in a baggy with potting soil until you can). Sowing them in fall (right after you eat them) allows them to cold-stratify naturally, outdoors.

If you’re mosying around a pawpaw patch and are tempted to remove a sapling, think twice:  Pawpaws have long taproots and respond poorly to being yanked out.  Unless you have emerald-green thumbs, either grow them from seed or find a nursery that sells pawpaw trees. More and more do.

N.B.: This post was first published October 2023.
