#512. In Lofoten, Norway, a Table Is Set Between Peaks and Fjords

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

“Now you know what it’s like to be in the eye of a storm,” said our hiking guide, Audun Rasmussen, with a grin.

I tried to nod, but the wind whipped it into a wobble. We were nearly 2,000 feet up a steep, multi-edged mountain face, and simultaneously getting pelted by rain. If views over picturesque towns and freshwater lakes below are what is typically promised in this part of Northern Norway, today there’d be none. Instead, fog clung to every nook and crevice of the glacial hulks surrounding us.

A room at Holmen Lofoten framing the wild ocean and craggy shoreline

There was a sense of achievement, certainly. Rain had turned much of our trails into sinking mud, and rock faces to gripless stone. We had used our hands to push past birch and skidded on moss. And we would still have to make our way back down. But for now, we were in the clouds, hiding under rocky overhangs and finding reprieve in reindeer-meat sandwiches, dry fish bites, and chocolate-covered wafers called Kvikk Lunsj that translates to “a quick lunch."

It’s ascents like these, over rock foundations that vastly predate the Jurassic period, that draw close to a million visitors each year to Lofoten Islands. The tiny Norwegian archipelago, 68 degrees north of the Arctic Circle and two flights from Oslo, is a distinctive and dramatic landscape with spiky mountains rising from the Norwegian Sea and sandy beaches fringed by fjords. In summer, buoyed by endless daylight, adventurers arrive from Norway’s mainland to camp, climb, and swim.

But summer was past its twilight when I arrived in late September, and I had come to discover an entirely different side of Lofoten: one found not on its peaks, but on its plates.

Lofoten's rocky shorelines feature a nutrient-dense ecosystem and seabird colonies.

In a wind-whipped corner of Sørvågen, a village in the southwestern reaches of the archipelago, a cluster of mustard-colored cabins on stilts juts out into the sea. Once home to a cod factory, these buildings have been transformed into Holmen Lofoten, a unique culinary destination and hotel that has welcomed food lovers and adventurers from around the world since 2007. The traditional fishing cabins, known as rorbuer in Norwegian, are distinguished by their corrugated roofs, some of which are topped with insulating grass. Walkways connect the buildings to each other and to wooden decks facing the sea. Inside the guest rooms, large picture windows frame the shoreline. With its simple local furnishings and hand-hewn charm, everything about this place feels elemental.

A cluster of traditional fishing cabins make up Holmen Lofoten, a hotel and culinary destination.

Locally sourced furnishings at Holmen are kept deliberately simple so the views take centerstage.

During my four-day stay, Holmen would play host to Kitchen on the Edge of the World, an extraordinary culinary adventure launched in 2018 that welcomes acclaimed chefs into its kitchen four times a year, and brings guests around the table—and out into the surrounding wild. This, the 30th edition, would be helmed by Matt Orlando, a California-born, Copenhagen-based chef who has worked at some of the world’s most influential kitchens like The Fat Duck in London and Per Se in New York. For our part, we would feast and drink to our hearts’ content, with the landscape that nurtures every dish spread out just beyond the windows.

Ingunn Rasmussen, the warm, chatty matriarch behind Kitchen on the Edge (and hiking guide Audun's sister), took over the property in October 2001 and now runs it with her son, Håvar Holmen. “I wanted to build a space that felt like home, where we could all log off and reconnect with our natural roots,” she said when she picked me up from the airport. A fisherman's daughter, she grew up around this very site—in fact, one of the cabins still bears her sister’s childhood scribbles on the wall. After a period abroad, Ingunn returned determined to breathe life back into this corner of Lofoten. What followed was a meticulous restoration focused on preserving the original structures and historical details, and eventually, the addition of a couple of new buildings in the same vernacular to accommodate larger suites.

In Lofoten, there are often four seasons in an hour.

Traditional bunk style beds in a larger room at Holmen

On the first night of the multi-day event, we gathered in the living room above the kitchen. Like all of Holmen’s shared spaces, it is defined by rustic interiors, exposed beams, and raw, sculptural elements—foraged flora, taxidermy, found objects—that echo the weathered landscape outside. That evening’s dinner would be helmed by Holmen’s own chefs, a young and talented assemblage from as far as Sicily and Piauí, in the northeast of Brazil.

Trays circulated with shrimp croustades and cocktails that looked like Negronis but told a more local story: the vermouth was infused with foraged blackcurrant leaves, and the Campari steeped with dried berries and yarrow flowers. Each sip seemed to capture the autumnal palette I’d seen blanketing the hillside on our drive in. The nonalcoholic version was just as heady: an elderflower tonic with a syrup made from freshly picked wild hops from nearby. “Holmen was created because we want to show our guests the Lofoten Islands,” said Ingunn. “And food is a critical part of understanding it.”

The kitchen is the heart of Holmen, and guests are welcome to pull up a stool and chat with the chefs at any hour.

I wandered over to Mia Hjorth Hansen, co-owner of Gedulgt, a cocktail bar in Aarhus, Denmark, and resident mixologist at Kitchen on the Edge. Shelves around her workstation held glass jars filled with foraged and dried ingredients—cornflower, dandelion, and fireweed—that she was transforming into elixirs. While visiting chefs rarely return, Hansen is an exception: this is her twelfth Kitchen on the Edge. Still, there's been a through-line among the chefs. Whether Ana Roš, the renowned Slovenian chef behind the three-Michelin-starred Hiša Franko, or Simon Rogan, the UK pioneer of the farm-to-table movement, each has shared a commitment to land stewardship. “We take time picking them,” says Ingunn, “because we want [chefs] who will be considerate about the people and wildlife that make this [our] home.”

The first night of the event is always designed to be an icebreaker, and Ingunn warned us we’d be surprised by how quickly we’d grow comfortable sharing close quarters over the four days. We were a motley crew, including a geologist from Brunei, a committed adventurer from Dubai, and a Melbourne retiree who travels for intimate food events like this one. Our conversations followed us outside for dessert, onto a deck where we savored ice cream made with black currant leaves and lovage, topped with mascarpone made from Lofoten cream and finished with a meringue charred over binchotan, a Japanese white charcoal. A tracking app suggested the Northern Lights were tantalizingly close, and Holmen kept watch on the skies. It was a taste of what was to come: an intimate experience shaped by live-fire cooking and an embrace of the Arctic elements.

Chef Matt Orlando working his magic by the tradition wood-fired oven

The next morning, encouraged by Ingunn's invitation to explore every corner of the property, I wandered into the kitchen to watch Orlando and his sous chef, Tom Siebeler, prepare for their first dinner. “Anything that comes out of that wood-fired oven is going to taste amazing,” Orlando told me, working his way through a pile of reindeer ribs from a sustainable farm run by Sámi herders just north of Lofoten. Originally expecting chops, he was improvising garnishes to suit the ribs. Reindeer is one of many ingredients Holmen takes care to source locally, alongside cheeses, Lofotlam (a Geographical Indication-protected local lamb), and vegetables. “For us, knowing where things come from matters, even with the objects we use," Ingunn said. The exquisite tableware we were dining on, for instance, was made by local ceramicist Gunvor Tangrand, whose organic forms pay homage to the surroundings and textured surfaces incorporate old fishing yarn.

Orlando had arrived in Lofoten a few days prior with a case full of his favorite building blocks—barley, Danish yellow peas, several kinds of miso—but was most excited to work with what he could find in the immediate surroundings. He invited me to step outside to forage for his dinner menu, and we returned with chickweed, corn shoots, and sheep sorrel. “I love cooking in remote places,” he said. “Thinking on the fly and exploring the potential in things you’ve never worked with is where the coolest ideas come from.”

The writer foraging for herbs with chef Matt Orlando

Fishbone noodles reflect Matt Orlando's zero-waste philosophy.

Orlando, like Ingunn, is a bit of a visionary. Some years ago, he opened Amass, a circular restaurant in Copenhagen’s industrial Refshaleøen neighborhood, where friendly service and an approachable, low-waste tasting menu were paired with a harborside produce garden diners could wander through. Amass has since closed, but last year, Orlando opened Esse in a restored Nordhavn warehouse, and it received its first Michelin star just a few months later. Signature dishes, including his fermented potato bread, made the move, along with his commitment to environmental responsibility. At Holmen, that philosophy is a natural fit.

Later that night, as we gathered around communal tables adorned with wildflowers and candlelight, we squealed with delight when the potato bread arrived with allium butter, then again for the fishbone noodles, a masterful creation made by pressure-cooking fish bones and puréeing them until smooth. The collagen-rich mixture gives the noodles their slippery texture, which Orlando complements with smoked chili, sunflower-seed miso, and fermented chili cream. Served family-style, the meal was messy and fun, with utensils set aside so we could savor the main course, the reindeer, with our hands. As a pescatarian, I happily dug into my dish: hedgehog mushrooms grilled with chili-strawberry barbecue sauce and served with Lofoten chimichurri.

Foraging for Rowanberries in the hillsides around Holmen

The weather changes quickly in the North. Quiet and calm one minute, then wild and Baroque the next. “Four seasons in an hour,” joked Holmen. It’s this unique confluence of climatic conditions that makes Lofoten such a productive landscape: the interaction between the warmer Gulf Stream and cold Arctic waters supports marine life; the long hours of sunlight send plants into hyperdrive; and the sea spray nourishes lamb grazing pastures. “This land and sea have nourished us for generations,” says Ingunn, who leads guests on foraging walks, returning with fireweed flowers for immune-boosting tea, cloudberries for jam, or lovage for a vibrant, savory butter.

But to really understand this landscape is to understand its enduring connection to one particular food source: skrei, or Arctic cod. Here, tepee-style wooden drying racks known as hjeller are as much a visual fixture as Lofoten’s mountains; their distinctive frames have dotted the terrain for more than a thousand years. The racks are empty when I visit, but come January they will be stacked with skrei that have been caught at the end of their annual migration, from the Barents Sea to Lofoten’s milder waters where they spawn. Transformed into tørrfisk (wind-dried stockfish), this seasonal bounty has sustained the local economy for centuries. However, like many other coastal societies shaped by the migration and economics of Atlantic cod, Lofoten has been no stranger to ecosystem pressures over the decades, from shifting migration patterns to offshore fishing fleets.

Perhaps, it is no surprise then, that a more diversified agricultural movement is taking root. One rainy afternoon, I departed from the cozy confines of Holmen and drove an hour north to the fishing village of Napp, home to Lofoten Seaweed Company, whose products supply Holmen’s kitchens—and line our bathroom shelves. Co-founded by Angelita Eriksen and Tamara Singer, this entirely women-led team dons their wetsuits and hand-harvests seven types of local seaweed, including sugar kelp and nori, from the strong tidal currents of straits like Nappstraumen, transforming them into prized seasonings, even toiletries. Singer showed us around the processing facility inside a traditional rorbu, where Eriksen once cut out cod tongues—"a common way for kids in this region to make a bit of pocket money," Singer explained—and where they now sort, store, and age seaweed for top kitchens like Noma and Geranium in Copenhagen.

Holmen’s prep table, heaped with treats gathered from the wild landscape outside

Norwegian scallops with ocean truffle and shaved hazelnuts

They’re not the only ones. Across the islands, artisanal cheesemakers and farmers growing root vegetables and edible flowers are cultivating the region’s rich ecosystem. Back at Holmen, I spoke with local Bjørg-Elin Kärn, who specializes in food and agriculture systems at Love Food, an organization that connects small-scale producers with local restaurants. “Lamb, stockfish, and seaweed are all remarkably sustainable ways of producing food,” she said. “But conservation only works when the people living on the land and by the sea can make a good living. When a restaurant chooses these products, it helps keep that whole system alive.”

Matt Orlando with Holmen Lofoten's kitchen team

Kärn also works to steer tourism toward local food and agriculture, rather than simply encouraging visitors to “hitch tents and climb mountains.” We talked about the growing toll of [the wrong kind of] mountain tourism—a sore point among residents. Holmen’s own mountaineering guide Audun prefers not to share the names or locations of ascents anymore. He fears that overexposure on social media will draw the wrong kind of attention, not least from underprepared climbers who underestimate both the difficulty of the routes and the whims of the weather. A few years ago, Lofoten banned wild camping, restricting campers to designated grounds.

It is clear that tourism has come knocking on Lofoten’s door. In 2025, inbound visitor growth in Northern Norway as a whole, and Lofoten in particular, outpaced the rest of the country, bringing with it a familiar tension: how to welcome visitors while protecting what makes a place worth visiting in the first place. “Our hope is that people come to Lofoten for the right reasons,” Ingunn told me. “Ultimately, that will be what helps us protect the islands and our way of life.” Food, increasingly an anchor to how people travel, offers a way to bridge that gap, inviting visitors in while helping them understand the place they’ve come to experience. “Here, you can’t separate food production and landscape,” said Kärn. “Much of what visitors come to see, whether they know it or not, is shaped by food.”

In this way, Kitchen on the Edge hopes to sound a call for travelers to rethink why and how they visit places like Lofoten. Hospitality that takes guests beyond the plate and into the fields isn’t a new concept, but here it’s more than a perk: it’s a way of life.

One morning, when the weather cleared just enough, we piled into inflatable boats that cut through choppy waters over to an uninhabited island. Once a thriving fishing village, the island was abandoned when motorboats became commonplace in the mid-20th century, and fishing communities no longer needed to live close to spawning grounds. Today, nature has reclaimed this idyllic spot—where Ingunn spent childhood summer days picking berries and swimming—with waterfalls and carpets of reindeer moss.

While others traced the shore on foot, I sat by the jetty and took it all in: the mountains disappearing into the sea, the pair of white-tailed eagles circling overhead, the tiny berries and subarctic flora underfoot. There’s something that happens to your brain when you’re constantly zooming out to take in the big picture, then narrowing your focus to the smallest details. I later learned there’s a name for it: attentional elasticity, a psychological framework that helps you anchor yourself in the present moment. It might be why I felt such a stillness, despite the fog of jet lag and the pull of the action around me.

The writer standing on an atmospheric edge in Å, a picturesque village at the southernmost tip of Moskenes island

...to Henningsvær, a fairytale fishing village two hours north of Holmen. Its main street is lined with shops selling handblown glass and cozy knits, and colorful boats bob along its winding waterways. Check into Trevarefabrikken, a photogenic boutique hotel and cultural hub in a mossy-yellow building that once housed a cod liver oil factory. Take an early morning stroll to the football field, famous for its dramatic setting, then browse contemporary art at the gallery in the old caviar factory. Warm up with fish soup at Klatrekafeen before hitting the seafront sauna back at the hotel.

The hotel crew built a campfire around which we ate fish soup, taking turns making pinnebrød by winding dough around birch sticks and cooking it over the flames. A flask of aquavit—holy water around these parts—made an appearance.

At Holmen, encounters with nature became part of the daily rhythm. We foraged not just for food but for art, too, gathering moss, mushrooms, and branches heavy with berries to create wild tablescapes with Becky Hindley, a British botanical artist. In a woodworking cabin into which the sunlight poked through optimistically, we spent hours with Lee John Phillips, a woodcarver from Wales, fashioning spoons out of local birch. It’s a particular kind of luxury that’s rooted in knowing how to build a life from what the land gives you.

As in any home, the kitchen here is the heart of the house, so we kept returning to it: to watch chefs pry open Icelandic queen scallops and slice basketfuls of chanterelles and hedgehog mushrooms. Orlando was always within reach, sharing recipes and starting conversations about food systems. Unlike other modern culinary festivals, there was no peeking behind the curtain, because, in fact, there is no curtain. “This is my dream for a restaurant,” Orlando said on the night of his final presentation, as we savored grilled pollock with chard shishito butter and sipped a remarkable oat milk drink flavored with green cardamom, rose, and spiced apple. “To be able to have diners weave in and out and chat as I cook.”

As it happened, the clear blue skies we were hoping for never showed for the four days I was there. But the astonishing dishes served beside a crackling oven, the easy camaraderie around the dinner table, and the ever-present possibility of the Northern Lights left me more awake to the world than I’d felt in months.

Outdoor and open-fire cooking is a cornerstone of the experience at Holmen Lofoten.

We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

#1356. <b><strong>7 Myths You’ve Heard About Creatine That Aren’t Actually True, According to Experts</strong></b>

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Don’t believe everything you’ve heard about the supp.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If there’s one supplement on everyone’s lips, it’s creatine. But, with all the talk about this well-studied supp that was once for gym bros and is now beloved by, well, everyone, it can be hard to separate fact from fiction.

Creatine is a compound your body makes naturally from three amino acids called arginine, glycine, and methionine, but you can also get small amounts from meat and fish, says Kristin Kirkpatrick, RDN, a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness and Preventive Medicine. “I like to describe it as the power plant for your cells,” she says. “It helps regenerate adenosine triphosphate (ATP), the energy molecule your muscles and brain rely on for quick, high-intensity work.” Without it, your movements won’t be as explosive in the gym, and you may take longer to recover, as Women’s Health reported previously.

The body produces about half of the creatine it needs, and the rest has to come from diet—or supplements. But as creatine’s popularity has exploded, so has the misinformation surrounding it. We asked experts to debunk some of the most persistent myths about the supplement. Here’s what they said.

Meet the experts: Jay Luthar, MD, is an internal, integrative, and lifestyle medicine physician and a professor at Harvard Medical School. Kristin Kirkpatrick, RDN, is a dietitian at Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness and Preventive Medicine.

You may have heard that when you first start taking creatine, you should do what’s called a loading phase—which is taking about four times the regular dose for up to a week—to saturate your muscle stores with the supp.

A standard dose of creatine is 3 to 5 grams per day; a loading phase calls for about 20 grams per day, split into four doses, Kirkpatrick explains. Why four doses? “Essentially, there’s a ceiling on how much creatine the gut can absorb and deliver to muscle at any one time,” says Jay Luthar, MD, an internal, integrative, and lifestyle medicine physician and professor at Harvard Medical School. “Taking 20 grams at once doesn’t mean 20 grams reaches your muscles; it means a portion gets excreted, and you likely experience GI discomfort in the process.”

The idea is that a loading phase fills your muscles' creatine stores faster—in about a week—but it’s totally unnecessary for most people. An athlete who wants results before an upcoming competition may benefit, for example, but if you don’t have anything timely on the calendar, you can skip the load completely, Kirkpatrick says. Doing so might also save you from bloating and stomach upset and other side effects that most often cause people to stop taking the supp. “A steady daily dose is simpler, easier on your body, and just as effective over time,” Kirkpatrick says.

This one is partly true—but stay with us. Creatine works by drawing more water into your muscles, which means that it’s possible you may experience some bloat at first. Key words: At first. Research in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition from 2021 found that creatine does not cause long-term water retention.

“In the initial few weeks, creatine draws water into muscle cells,” Dr. Luthar says. “That means the bump on the scale is evident, but it’s water weight—not fat. Once muscle saturation is reached and you transition to a maintenance dose, this stabilizes.” As for how long that takes? About two to four weeks, he says.

Also keep in mind that your dosage and creatine type matter. “The bloating and stomach discomfort people complain about are usually tied to large doses of creatine monohydrate, especially during a loading phase,” says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine monohydrate isn't very soluble, so large amounts can sit in the gut and cause GI distress.” If this is a concern, Kirkpatrick recommends creatine HCl, which is much more soluble and better absorbed, so it works at a smaller dose. That means less bloating, less stomach upset, and less water retention, with the same benefits creatine is known for.

For years, creatine was relegated to lifters, but now there is research showing benefits far beyond the gym. “We now have strong evidence that creatine supports healthy aging and muscle preservation during weight loss,” says Kirkpatrick.

For example, a new study in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition found that adults ages 45 to 65 gained lean tissue and strength on creatine without a structured exercise program. “That's why I see interest [in creatine] from people who have never lifted a weight: midlife women, older adults, people on GLP-1 medications, and anyone thinking about longevity,” says Kirkpatrick.

Plus, endurance athletes can see some perks from the supplement too. Emphasis on some. Although creatine doesn't appear to improve aerobic capacity, meaning it won't make you a faster marathoner on its own, endurance athletes can still see benefits when their sport includes explosive movements like sprints, surges, or hill climbs, as well as in the strength training they do to support injury prevention, per a 2023 systematic review and meta-analysis in Sports Medicine.

“Creatine’s primary mechanism is supporting short-burst, high-intensity energy production, which isn’t the primary energy system in steady-state endurance events,” Dr. Luthar says. “That can make it useful if a marathon runner, for example, is doing weekly sprint workouts to improve speed. It might have less of an impact on a weekly long run or three-hour cycle ride.” There’s also interesting emerging evidence that creatine helps reduce recovery time between workouts, shares Dr. Luthar. For endurance athletes and lifters alike, this could be another potential benefit.

Some of the most exciting creatine research is happening outside the weight room, especially for women. First, a few facts to keep in mind, per Kirkpatrick: Women naturally store less creatine than men, and beginning around age 30, we lose 3 to 8 percent of our muscle mass every decade, with the loss accelerating after 50. The good news is that creatine supplementation has been shown to support strength, muscle, and recovery in women at every life stage, particularly when paired with resistance training, according to a 2021 study published in Nutrients.

Build muscle with the WH+ Strength After 50 Plan.

Another emerging area of creatine research in women deals with menopause. Perimenopausal women have measurably lower brain creatine levels, and those deficits are linked to difficulty concentrating, according to recent research in Nutrients. And, although a lot more studies are needed, it’s possible supplementation could help with that. A 2025 randomized controlled trial published in the Journal of the American Nutrition Association found that eight weeks of low-dose creatine HCl improved cognitive function and brain energy metabolism in perimenopausal and menopausal women.

Estrogen loss associated with menopause also accelerates muscle loss (which is why women start to see a steeper decline in their 50s), so creatine could potentially help both mentally and physically, as noted by a 2025 study published in the Journal of the Society of Sports Nutrition International. Just keep in mind: Though creatine has benefits for women, that doesn’t mean that we need gender-specific formulations.

Consistency matters far more than the timing of your creatine intake, says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine works by gradually filling and maintaining your muscles' stores, so what counts is taking it every day, including rest days.”

You don’t need to be super exact on when you take it every day, either. Creatine works through muscle saturation maintained over time, so if you miss your usual window by a few hours, there won’t be a huge effect on stores, says Dr. Luthar.

Kirkpatrick’s advice is to stack creatine onto something you already do every day—just maybe not your morning coffee. When mixed with hotter liquids, creatine powder may become ineffective, so it’s best to stick to room temperature or cool liquids.

This myth comes from a bit of a misunderstanding. Creatine gets broken down into creatinine, which is often measured in routine bloodwork as an estimate of kidney function. Elevated levels of creatinine can indicate an issue with kidney function. But, if you’re taking a creatine supplement, you may have elevated levels of creatinine simply because you’re taking in extra amounts—not because your kidneys aren’t functioning well. For that reason, Kirkpatrick says to tell your doctor you take creatine before bloodwork, so a slightly higher creatinine number is interpreted correctly.

When researchers looked into the relationship between creatine and kidney health, they found that creatine did not harm kidney function, per a 2026 meta-analysis of 26 randomized controlled trials published in International Urology and Nephrology.

Overall—for a supplement—creatine is pretty low stakes. “It's one of the most researched supplements available, with a strong safety record,” Kirkpatrick says. A 2025 analysis published in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition evaluated 685 human clinical trials and found no significant difference in reported side effects between creatine and placebo. That said, you should talk to a doctor before taking any new supplement, especially if you have kidney or liver disease, diabetes, or bipolar disorder, or are pregnant or breastfeeding. (For individuals diagnosed with bipolar disorder, Kirkpatrick says there is some preliminary research suggesting that creatine supplementation may increase the risk of manic or hypomanic episodes.)

You’ll also want to choose a brand that is third-party tested, since supplements aren’t regulated like medications. (You can find our recs here.)

No supplement is a must—and that includes creatine. Kirkpatrick says you can absolutely build strength and muscle without it. “Consistent resistance training, enough protein, adequate sleep, and a well-balanced diet will always do the heavy lifting,” says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine is just the add-on, not the foundation.”

That said, Kirkpatrick thinks it deserves the love it gets, particularly when we look at the growing body of research surrounding its benefits. “What makes creatine particularly exciting is the strong evidence supporting its role in muscle strength, performance, and recovery, along with emerging research exploring potential benefits for brain health and cognitive function,” says Kirkpatrick. “For most healthy adults, creatine is considered safe when taken at recommended doses. While the research is especially compelling for muscle health, I think the potential applications for healthy aging make this an exciting area of nutritional science.”

Dr. Luthar agrees and says that creatine is backed by decades of sports nutrition research, including a fair number of studies with human participants. Still, the supplement has taken social media by storm and the hype is taking on a life of its own. “Some of the benefits posted online are exaggerated or incorrect, which is why it’s best to discuss creatine or any supplement with a health care provider before taking it,” he says.

Ashley Martens, C.S.C.S. is a wellness writer based in Chicago. With a lifelong passion for all things health and wellness, Ashley enjoys writing about topics to help people live happier and healthier lives. With a foundation in fitness, food, and nutrition, Ashley covers it all including sexual health and travel topics. Ashley is also a NASM-certified personal trainer and group fitness instructor.

#3513. Running All the Marathon Majors Didn’t Live Up to the Hype. It Made Me Rethink Why I Run.

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

This article originally appeared in the Fall 2025 issue of Runner’s World.

I RUN PAST Buckingham Palace and, after 26 miles, can finally see it: my sixth World Marathon Major finish line, the moment many runners dream about. After running through the arch, I am shepherded through the chute with hundreds of other marathoners. A volunteer hastily puts a London Marathon medal around my neck, and another adds the Six Star Medal. I hobble behind other finishers in a queue waiting to get their photo taken in front of an Abbott World Marathon Majors poster. A fleeting thought comes to mind when it’s my turn to pose for the photographer: Is this it?

The next day, I sit in Heathrow Airport, scribbling on a blank page in my training journal: I just did something amazing—two marathons in one week. But I feel a little hollow. It’s just all so…pedantic? Silly? I’m going home with this gigantic medal and another even more gigantic medal—and for what?

I was hesitant about running the London Marathon. Even though I wanted to race it someday, I was daunted by the idea of it being six days after Boston, which is held on Patriots’ Day on the third Monday in April. I’ve qualified and raced every Boston Marathon since 2013 without fail. Skipping a year just to focus on running London was nonnegotiable.

My partner Paul and I had already gone through an IVF cycle and were planning on going through another round. I was starting to become more mindful of how races interrupted my menstrual cycle and how taxing the effort was on my body months after. Pregnancy and motherhood would also eventually set some limitations on racing and travel. Runner’s World Content Hype Director Aly Ellis, who was also running Boston and London, spoke frankly: “We’re at that age where we want to start families; you have to take these opportunities now.”

Though the Abbott World Marathon Majors series was on my radar, I honestly didn’t really care about earning that colossal medal at first. The series began in 2006 with five marathons: Boston, New York City, London, Chicago, and Berlin. It wasn’t until the Tokyo Marathon was added as a major in 2013 that runners who completed all six would be awarded a special medal. I thought, maybe, I’d eventually win entry to these races (all marathons except Boston have lotteries) and afford to travel to those places, but it seemed like an extravagant goal.

By 2022, I had run all of the stateside majors. Riding off of a sub-3:00 high in Chicago, I researched the Berlin website and saw there was another way to gain entry into that marathon: qualifying. I entered the 2023 race with a time under 3:10, the cutoff time for women ages 18 to 44, and got in. Then, Asics, the Tokyo Marathon sponsor, offered me a 2024 bib. New Balance, the brand sponsoring London, followed with an invitation to participate in this year’s race. Suddenly, earning my sixth star seemed like it would happen sooner rather than later.

And then it happened, and instead of feeling accomplished, I felt empty.

Maybe I felt depressed because I wanted a do-over. I didn’t wear the second bib Abbott provided that read: “Today is the day! Cheer me on” with all six majors listed, ending with London 2025 in big, bold red letters. I left it in my hotel room, unable to stop the safety pins from popping off the back of my sports bra. As I raced and saw fellow Six Star hopefuls with the bib on their backs, I felt both camaraderie and regret that I wasn’t as recognizable for attempting to complete the same feat.

Maybe I felt alone. As I was shuffled through the Abbott booth, straining a smile at the camera and walking around with two heavy medals clanging against my chest, I had no one to share the moment with.

The Six Star hardware had also lost its luster this year. Abbott had announced the inclusion of Sydney as a seventh star—but there would be no Seven Star Medal. At press time, the Shanghai and Cape Town marathons have also been evaluated as additions to the majors. In a Black Mirror turn, eventually you may be able to go the deluxe route, earning a Nine Star Medal. Which begs the question: When does it end?

For me, at least, money is always a factor. Participating in the Majors is an expensive commitment. Runners who aren’t NYRR members had to pay $315 for last year’s NYC Marathon. Travel and housing costs can’t be left out of that equation when it comes to international races. Majors are becoming more difficult to get into. The London 2026 ballot broke a world record with over 1.1 million applications. Those accepted are a mere fraction of that number; this year’s race had approximately 56,000 finishers.

You wouldn’t notice these race caps attending these events, however: Major races are event-filled weekends, with live shows that sell out fast, multiple shakeout runs vying for shared space on narrow sidewalks, and overcrowded activewear pop-ups that run out of extra-smalls.

I know I sound ornery. The fact that these events have reached music festival proportions is a good thing for our sport. But I can’t help feeling that the Nine Star Medal will be more possible for the privileged—those who can afford it and, yes, those with connections. I was able to gain access to two of the six races thanks to my job.

Ironically, while I was tipsy on a holiday cocktail last December, my fingers started tapping my info on the Sydney ballot form, thinking my chances of scoring a bib were slim. (I’ve never won a marathon lottery.) “Could you imagine if I got in?” I asked Paul, sitting in the middle of the restaurant while eating dinner, my eyes glued to my phone’s screen.

Weeks after my night of being the worst dinner date ever, I received an email from Race Roster with the “Claim this registration” button.

At this point, it doesn’t matter to me whether or not Sydney is a major. For years, I’ve loved running races—marathons especially—because it’s something athletic I’m actually good at, unlike ball sports like tennis, volleyball, or basketball that I’m very bad at. Now running is a reason to travel, the actual event a footnote in my itinerary. I honestly never thought I’d visit Japan or Germany. I fed monkeys in Kyoto. Ate apfelstrudel in the Literaturhaus Berlin garden. And I got to experience it with Paul, who supports my spur-of-the-moment race registrations, then cheers for me at the finish line. We celebrated my PR together in Chicago, shared the free pint every runner gets after crossing the finish line in Berlin, and toasted with sake after Tokyo. In London, I sat alone in the New Balance VIP tent, sipping on a gigantic lager I couldn’t even finish.

After an air traffic control delay prolonged my six-hour flight to Newark to 14 hours, I used the extra time to reassess. Even though Paul wasn’t there, I had so many people who helped me see that race through: Aly sitting next to me on the bus to the start, the runner offering his hand to me after I fell on the course, fish and chips with a grad school friend postrace.

I realized that sometimes, when you’re a small part of completing something big, you start to forget the reason you’re really doing it. For me, it was never for the hardware. It was about proving myself. And I did—several times. Now these races hold more meaning when I share them. And I’ll do that with Paul in Sydney.

Recommended Experience: No experience necessary! Anyone can accomplish this training plan.

Get the PlanRecommended Experience: This plan is for a new runner who is just getting back into exercise.

Get the PlanRecommended Experience: For a new runner who has been exercising regularly for at least a year, or someone who has completed a few 5Ks or 10Ks and can complete 6 miles in one workout. This plan peaks at 28 miles in a week with a 12-mile long run.

Get the PlanRecommended Experience: For runners comfortable exercising for at least an hour at a moderate effort. This plan peaks at 32 miles in a week with a 14-mile long run.

#1376. Is There An Optimal Time To Go On A Walk? Here’s What The Science Says

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Going on a walk comes with a lot of benefits, both mental and physical. If done at the right pace and intensity, walking can be a good way to get in some zone 2 cardio, giving your heart some love. But, it can also elevate your mood, help you sleep, and even boost your immunity.

But get this: When you decide to take your daily stroll might impact exactly what the benefits are, say experts. Here’s how to decide when to lace up your sneakers to get in your daily steps.

Laura A. Richardson, PhD, a professor of applied exercise science and movement science at the University of Michigan School of Kinesiology. Michael Russo, MD, is a board-certified bariatric surgeon at MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center.

Morning walkers, rejoice! There is a lot of benefit to walking in the A.M. The first is simply that getting your steps in early in your day means that it’s a lot less likely something will keep you from getting it done.

Beyond that, getting out of bed and into the sunlight first thing can have positive impacts on your rest later. Morning exposure to the sun’s rays can help promote better quality sleep that night, regardless of how much total sunlight you get throughout the day, according to a 2024 study in Journal of Health Psychology. This is because it potentially helps regulate your circadian rhythm.

Plus, walking in the morning was found to improve blood pressure, insulin concentration, and insulin resistance compared to walking in the afternoon in patients with metabolic syndrome, per a 2023 study in the Journal of Physiology.

One thing that morning exercise doesn’t seem to have an edge on? Body composition, according to the aforementioned 2023 study, as well as a separate study in Obesity. One reason you *might* hear that morning walking could have a weight loss benefit is because many people walk in the morning in a fasted state, which means that your body is using fat stores for energy instead of carbohydrates, says Laura A. Richardson, a professor of applied exercise science and movement science at the University of Michigan School of Kinesiology.

And, you guessed it: Using fat as an energy source could be helpful for weight loss. “Working out in a fasted state when your glycogen stores are minimized may help you burn more fat,” says Michael Russo, MD, a bariatric surgeon at MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center. But, he says, it could also have the opposite effect because you feel fatigued or low-energy, making your workout less effective. “Also, if your protein intake is low, your body can burn muscle for energy in a fasted state,” he adds. So, fasted walking may not be for everyone—and it certainly won’t make or break your weight loss journey.

Maybe you’re not a morning person—or maybe you get a bigger burst of energy to get active in the afternoon. You wouldn’t be alone. Typically, by the afternoon, after you’ve had breakfast and lunch and hopefully some hydration, you might have more energy and better nutrition to really make the most of your exercise, says Richardson.

Plus, walking after a meal comes with some serious digestion benefits. Particularly if you’re pre-diabetic or have diabetes, walking after a meal can be a good choice. “Exercising after a meal really does help in aiding what we call digestion,” says Richardson. “Really what it's doing is helping to stabilize our blood sugar. As you're moving, you increase blood flow because your skeletal muscles are contracting and those muscles are starting to eat or take in all of that extra sugar, or glucose, from your meal.”

As for how quickly after a meal you should lace up your sneakers? Do it ASAP for the most benefit, according to a 2023 study in Sports Medicine.

Midday to afternoon workouts done at moderate to vigorous activity (which forms of walking, like power walking, incline walking, and rucking all reach) also were found to “substantially” decrease all-cause and cardiovascular disease mortality risk compared to morning ones, according to a 2023 study in Nature Communications.

Just like walking post-lunch is good for digestion, so is walking after dinner in the evening. It also might make you less likely to consume additional food after dinner, says Richardson. By getting up and moving around after dinner, you won’t be headed to your couch or bed, which could help prevent snacking.

The only thing you need to consider with any kind of exercise later in the day is how it impacts your sleep, Richardson notes. It really comes down to the individual person, though. One study found that both morning and evening exercise advance the sleep-wake cycle and production of melatonin in night owls—meaning that walking at any time of day is beneficial to them—but evening exercise delays these things for early birds, according to a 2023 review in Frontiers in Pharmacology.

Basically, evening exercise doesn’t need to be a dealbreaker for everyone. “If you’re able to sleep, then don’t worry about it,” says Richardson. If you’re considering scheduling your walks for the evening, just check in on how it affects your slumber.

Overall, walking in general has tons of benefits for your health, so don’t let the time of day deter you from doing it. “Really, the fundamental thought should be to choose a time that you can be consistent with,” says Richardson. This will help you build a habit and a schedule around the exercise. And, sticking to the same time every day has its own benefits on regulating your circadian rhythm whether it’s morning, afternoon, or night, she says.

Plus, while we have some research, there’s still a lot more to learn, especially when it comes to women. “Most of our research is done on men,” says Richardson. “It's important for us to realize that we really don't understand the difference of timing on women because so many of our studies have primarily been examining men.” All the more reason to explore on your own: See how walking at different times a day makes you feel.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#1382. Terry Bradshaw Will Never Tell You He’s Retiring

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The 78-year-old NFL legend opens up about depression, cancer, and why he has no interest in a farewell tribute.

TERRY BRADSHAW IS feeling achy today. But it’s not because of his 14-year NFL career, his 40-plus-year career as a broadcaster, or the daily work of manning his Texas ranch. No, what’s really been wearing him out is all the autographs he’s been signing.

“I signed 4,300 stickers in two weeks. I've got 2,500 more sent to me, and I've signed 750 since last night and this morning,” Bradshaw tells Men’s Health. “My finger on my index hand is turning to the left.”

Unfortunately for Bradshaw, people want your signature when you’re a Hall of Famer, stage performer, Fox Sports analyst, and—once again—author. Looking for Terry, Bradshaw’s second memoir, arrived earlier this month, 25 years after his last book. And the 78-year-old has experienced plenty in that span.

He got married (for a fourth time) to his longtime partner Tammy in 2014, survived two cancer diagnoses and even launched his own bourbon. Bradshaw’s book also gets into his struggles with depression and his “dumb” public image—which has followed him since his playing days.

“If you think you know me, you really don’t,” he says. But as the curtain begins to close on his broadcasting career, Bradshaw isn’t interested in taking a victory lap.

The Steelers legend told us that he recently signed a two-year extension with Fox, but expects to be replaced after the network shows the Super Bowl in 2029. He’s hoping that there’s no fancy send-off—not that he expects that wish to be honored.

“I don’t go for tributes,” he says. “I don’t need that in my life.”

For now, Bradshaw remains in the public eye, and he was happy to talk through his new book after a much-needed dose of rainfall to his farm.

MEN’S HEALTH: This is your first memoir since 2001. Why was now the time to put out another one?

TERRY BRADSHAW: I enjoy doing books, unlike probably a lot of people. The first couple of books were fine. The last three I've really enjoyed doing [with coauthor David Fisher].

You kind of park the vanity a little bit, and you say to yourself, "You know what? I’ve got a few things that have happened to me in the last 20-plus years. I'd like to share it with people.” And I'm thinking those that buy the book will learn from the book. Those dealing with depression, those who have had failed marriages… As my wife says, I'm an open book.

MH: A main theme of the book is opening up, and how there’s been this perception of you that isn’t accurate. What’s the biggest way people misunderstand you?

TB: That's my fault. Look, first of all, I know what this question is [laughs]. It's my fault, because I play into the dumb image. You want to have a shocker here? I enjoy it! It's fun for me.

I choose to do hardline football analysis, but with a smile on my face, and I try to entertain. And maybe sometimes I try too hard to entertain. Sometimes you can go too far, you can definitely be too much, and people get tired of that. So I measure my steps, I kind of let things come to me.

MH: You wrote about endearing yourself to people with humor, and how you want them to have good interactions with you. How much of a personal responsibility do you feel to make other people happy?

TB: It's of paramount importance. I hate to use the word, Jake, celebrities. I enjoy people. I enjoy making them smile. I enjoy making their day, because a lot of times, giving an autograph and taking a picture in an airport makes their day.

To tell you the truth, too many celebrities value themselves a little bit too highly. When we're all stripped of our titles and the movies and the songs we've written, the Super Bowls we won—we’re all just people.

MH: You’ve also talked at length about your experience with depression. How important was it for you to be known as someone who spoke about mental health?

TB: The depression [diagnosis] explained all the dark, dark, dark times, even now still, that I go through and experience. And you go through all the times, what started it, what kicked it in. Divorce will kick it in, injury will kick it in, things that are important to you'll kick it. Plus, let's be honest, I take medication, and even with the medication, I still will [sometimes] start sinking.

You have no idea how big a difference Tammy has made. Because if I didn't have her, I'd be by myself. I'd drink too much, I'd get drunk, or I’d get sad, and I'd buy a horse, or I'd purchase something. I was always looking for some fix, and now that's not the case. But to answer your question, I'm not afraid to share my illness or my weaknesses—as some people would want to say it is, but it isn’t a weakness, it's an illness—with other people.

I know I’ve made a difference in men's lives because I have them come up to me. They come up quietly. It's mainly in a grocery store, and I'll be going down the aisle shopping, and they'll slip over to me [quietly], "Hey man, I know about your depression. It got me to a doctor and my wife, and I really want to thank you for sharing all that.”

MH: How difficult was it to accept help for it?

TB: Well, like I told you earlier, events in your life will trigger it. I had gone through a divorce, and I had two little girls. That sent me in a hole because I felt like I lost my kids.

I was in a hole for almost two years. The emotional drag that it had on my life got to a point where it was unbearable, and that's when I decided, I said I gotta figure out what's going on here.

MH: Regarding your cancer diagnoses (which came in 2021 and 2022), you wrote that you weren’t actually sure if surviving skin and bladder cancer made you appreciate life more. Has your stance changed on that?

TB: Getting a report you had cancer was like: “Alright, there's a challenge.” But it didn't scare me. Of course, I thought about it. I didn't understand how serious bladder cancer was. My approach to it was always this way: as a Christian, if I die, I'm going to heaven. So, I'm good. I don't want to die, and I don't know how good I'm going to be at dying.

Let me tell you this: the hardest thing about cancer for me is going back for the checkups and waiting for the call that says you're okay. Now you’ve got another six months before you do it again. But you're okay. And everybody I talk to that are cancer-free, they all say the same thing. The scariest thing now is, “Oh man, going back, what's the report gonna be?”

MH: There’s a feeling you describe in the book of having to constantly prove yourself on the field. Do you feel more confident now as a broadcaster than you did at the end of your playing career?

TB: Oh, I am totally confident on the air, absolutely. Supremely confident. I think I'm going through now what everybody that's in their 70s goes through. I'm 78. I just signed a two-year extension, and I'm pretty sure no matter how well I perform over the next two years, they're going to want to replace me.

I’m not going to announce my retirement, because in two years when we do the Super Bowl, after my contract is expiring, I don't want anybody to do a tribute. So I'm never going to say I'm going to retire. Because, look, they may say, "Hey, you're still doing a good job. We'll sign you for another year.” That could easily happen. They're pissing into the wind if they think I’m gonna tell them I’m retiring.

TB: What if I say, “I don't know why you're doing that for, I'm not retiring.” But I mean, I'm not going anywhere either. What am I going to do, at 80? Go to ESPN? [laughs].

MH: When you reflect on your post-playing career, is there anything you regret having not done?

TB: I’m afraid of heights. I used to be afraid of flying. I’m not a daredevil. I will not paraglide, parasail, whatever you call it. I'm not jumping out of a plane. I'm not snorkeling. I'm not going underwater with a tank of air. Sharks are down there, they will bite you. I don't go in the water [laughs]. I live a really… it's not an uneventful life, certainly, nowhere near. But everything in the entertainment world, I have done.

MH: What does a successful last couple of years at Fox look like to you? Just continuing to make people smile?

TB: Absolutely. Keep entertaining, give strong opinions when I feel it.

I have another book in me, and I'll say more in the next book. I'm already planning it. It won't be a tell-all book. But it's a strong opinion book.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#1114. The Real-Life Diet of Bijan Robinson, Who Takes Magnesium to Sleep Better

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

In the third quarter of a recent Sunday Night Football game, Falcons running back Bijan Robinson took a handoff from quarterback Michael Penix Jr. and added another magnificent entry to his already considerable highlight reel. While running a stretch play to his left, Robinson cut inside toward Ravens cornerback Marlon Humphrey, who sized up an easy tackle for a modest gain. That is, until a split-second later, when the running back planted his right foot back into the turf, juked hard outside, and left Humphrey on skates, flailing for his jersey. Across the field, all wide receiver Drake London could do was shake his head and cackle at his teammate’s unfair agility.

The play was a small window into the freak athleticism the 24-year old Robinson is capable of unleashing every time he touches the ball. And so far this season, he has lived up to the expectations of his exponentially-growing gifts, allowing fantasy owners to revel in his inevitable production and laugh at their weekly opponents like London. As the engine of an otherwise inconsistent Atlanta offense, Robinson is averaging over 100 rush yards per game and on pace to amass career highs in total yards and touchdowns—enough to enter him into the early MVP discussion.

Indeed, there is plenty to smile about for Robinson, especially now that he’s using Invisalign to straighten his teeth. It’s just one of several decisions he’s recently made about his health as he contends with the punishing reality of being an elite NFL running back. It was only six years ago that he avoided disaster after a terrifying mid-air collision while at Texas that jeopardized his neck and spine. That’s partially why since entering the NFL, Robinson has worked with the Falcons training staff to build a meticulous gameplan for his strength and conditioning to set himself up for success. “They’re very intentional throughout the week on how I need to be used leading up to the game,” Robinson says. “They’re very specific on what's needed and how I can grow my body and how I get stronger, faster—everything.”

Below, Robinson details more of that work, including the recovery tips he learned from running back Christian McCaffrey, the supplements he takes before bed, and his go-to Taco Bell order when he’s fixing for a quick meal.

Bijan Robinson: Yeah, I feel great right now. Everything that I’ve been doing for my recovery up to this point and from previous seasons has been pretty cool. I’ve been taking care of my body every single week. It’s cool to have the guys at the facility recommend people to me. I’ve been using this guy, Dr. T. He’s a chiropractor, and he does cupping and all types of stuff. But to see how much it's enhanced compared to last year and the year before—and all the resources that I have now to sustain the optimal level of performance on the field—has been cool. I think now it’s about me getting the rookies into tip-top shape, because I know they've been having a long year, and they're trying to find their body routines. I’m just trying to help them out.

Yeah, it’s all the soreness, that lactic acid that gets built up in my system. I’m making sure that there's something that can release it and that I can just eliminate that soreness. Or if there's something going on in my shoulders that's super tight—that's something that just releases everything. And it's something that doesn't hurt, but you can feel the impact of your soreness. You can feel what's going on in whatever body part you have. So, cupping, needling, acupuncture—that will spark the jump in my muscle and release that soreness as fast as possible.

No, I've been doing it since my rookie year. I kind of stayed away from it in college. I just did more massage-based things at a facility in college. Even Hyperice, the Theragun, all that kind of stuff. But I think since entering the NFL, I've been really much bigger into the needling and cupping.

I would say my hips. My hips are always my source [of soreness], because I cut a lot. I do a lot of cutting, a lot of change direction, and that requires a lot of things in my hips. I think people think changing direction is more about your knees or your hamstrings and your lower back, but your hips are what controls everything up and everything on the lower half of your body. I have tighter hip sides. That's just how it works for me in my game. And then sometimes my shoulders are a little sore. But yeah, when I get out of bed, I’ve got to stretch my hips. It has always been the thing for me.

Yeah, so that'll be the off day. I'll do the cold tub and hot tub, and then I'll go in with the trainers, and they'll use the needles and cupping wherever I feel the most sore that week. It might change from week to week. And then, on a Tuesday, that's when Dr. T. will come and he'll do more of the chiropractic work. And then if something is bothering me or I want to loosen something out a little bit more, then he'll use one of his tools to remove it. That’s been something I've been doing this whole season, and it's been great for me.

Yeah, it's different, but not a big difference. The offseason is the more grueling, hard training. Everyday you’re doing things that just are abnormal—running hills, doing all types of change in direction. That's when the higher weights come in for lifting, squatting, and benching, because you're really preparing for a long season. That's why you want to train harder in the offseason. And then the season is more staying at that pace, where you're not going super heavy, you're not going super light, but you're more in the middle, maintaining strength in-season and not losing it.

You have to. I do a lot of core exercises during the week. And that's another thing. Your hips are your biggest thing, but then my core comes right after that. And your core operates everywhere in the upper body. It operates how you change your action, operates how you break tackles, and make people miss. You focus on one thing each day. Your upper body workouts—it's little movements in your shoulders, in your lower back, your core. And then on that Wednesday is when we focus all on the lower body and making sure that you're not just adding weight, adding strength, but stretching your Achilles, your feet, to operate at the highest level.

Sometimes you can do everything at once, but I don't think that's the best way to go about it. I think you should focus one day on the upper body. That's on Mondays. Give your body a rest on Tuesday. And then on Wednesdays, that's when you attack the lower body.

That's every day. I go into the training room and we'll do ankle exercises—just mobility, making sure that my ankles aren't stiff. A lot of people have stiff ankles because they don't stretch them out much. We do bands, a lot of heel lifts, and then balancing on one ankle, on both ankles, on the BOSU ball and the little balance circuit that we have. It only takes five minutes to do it. But those little things add up.

I started this in high school. But it got more elevated in Texas because they had so much more equipment. And then in the NFL, we’ve just kept on that same process. It’s been good.

Yeah, the pool is the biggest thing. He and I did water recovery with the cold tub, hot tub, the HydroWorx with the treadmill in the pool. All those things really enhanced my recovery plan. We did that every single day. After a hard workout, we would go in the pool, and we would do these pool recovery workouts that help your blood flow. Ever since, I've been doing it with him, I've been at the facility and we have the HydroWorx now. Doing that HydroWorx has been key after games because of all that soreness that's been built up. You want to release it as fast as possible the next day.

It just depends. After a workout, you typically want to do hot tub first to release it a little bit, and then go to the cold tub, then you go to the hot tub again, and then you'll go to the cold tub to finish it off.

So you're doing karaokes, high knees, a slight jog, but people really don't want to do that after a game. I've had to teach my mind, like, “This is what's gonna help when the game's over.” It'll take about 15 minutes, but you're really running in the pool. During the process, you're like, “Man, why am I doing this? I just played a game, I don't wanna do this.” But then when it's over, you feel great. It’s been a great thing for me.

So, I have a hyperbaric chamber at my house. But the hypobaric chamber, the Stratosphere that me and McCaffrey were doing in LA, that thing is unbelievable. I haven't done it since we trained together, but it's pretty special.

Oh no, I definitely want one. But you need somebody to control it for you. When I can figure that out, and if they can come out here and help me do it, I'm gonna do it. Those things are great.

So, you're going 18,000 feet in the air—and you get there in, like, five, six seconds. It's pretty unbelievable. But that form of recovery is great. And then you can [ride] the [stationary] bike in there. You can exercise in there. And then that just enhances it even more.

Yeah. In your blood flow, and definitely in your brain oxygen. You feel it quickly. I think, a week into it, on the field, my breathing was unbelievable. Like, I was breathing, and feeling like, “I can run for days.” That's the biggest thing with that Stratosphere. I hope they get it into all teams, everywhere, because everybody needs it.

I don't have a specific diet, but I do stay away from fast foods. My girlfriend cooks everything. And she just does it for fun. I don't tell her to cook, but she just wants to do it. And then, I eat at the facility in the mornings and for lunch. I just make sure that all those foods are hand prepared, really cooked. I know fast food is great sometimes. I might cheat and just, like, go to Taco Bell or somewhere, but—

It's not really a cheat day. It's just more of a day where I want to grab something real quick. I do that very minimally. It's been a long time since I’ve actually had McDonald’s or something. But that's the biggest thing in the diet: home-cooked meals.

I'm gonna get five soft tacos, and then like, two cinnamon twists, and a Baja Blast Freeze.

I’m not a caffeine guy. The biggest thing that I've learned is drinking water out of glass. Like, I've really been drinking all my water out of glass. I haven't been drinking water out of plastic at all. Because, one: drinking water out of glass is the most healthy thing for your body. And then two: you make sure all the toxins in those plastic bottles that get built up in the water are out of your body.

We don't because we're sponsored by Smartwater, but I just bring one. We have filtered water in the facility. I'll have the glass bottle with me, and then I'll just pour it in the bottle. It's been great.

Yeah, so especially on those prime-time games, we get back home at 3 or 4 a.m. I’m making sure I'm sleeping on the plane if I can. That’s the biggest thing. The team does a really good job of giving us a sleep routine for when we should sleep and how many hours of sleep we should have, especially on short weeks. I have this bed where it'll heat your body or cool your body, and if it detects that you're sweating in your sleep, it'll make the bed cool. Our dietitian is a sleep master, too, so he'll give us all the right timings of when to sleep and how to sleep and the vitamins to take before sleep. Magnesium has been a huge thing for me before I go to bed. I take three or four tablets of magnesium and it really just helps my body out a lot.

Oh yeah, especially, on some of the prime-time games. You just think about it so much. And then you have all day to think about it. But yeah, some games you just struggle a little bit. That's why I try to get ready for bed at, like, 9:30, 10 p.m. I know if my body is thinking about it, then I’ll at least be asleep by 11 or so.

Yeah, I smile a lot, so it’s just about having that confidence, and when I talk to people, when I do interviews or commercial stuff, making sure that my teeth are at a very optimal level. Not having wires and brackets has really been the biggest thing for me. I started using the clear liners, and it's been great for me. It's been a month and you can already see a shift happening. That's what makes the partnership perfect for me. It just creates discipline in myself, making sure I'm wearing those 22 hours throughout the day.

[I just want to] make sure I'm executing at a high level at all facets—understanding the plan that we have for the week and making sure that our brand of football is being played at the highest level. It's important using the gifts that God gives me at a high level, but taking it one week at a time, taking the one game at a time, and knowing that I’ve gotta be at my best self for this opponent that's coming in. Or if we're going there, understanding what's at hand. That's how you become your best self. Not looking ahead, but just being where my feet are.

My girlfriend and I will go watch movies in theaters at the iPic. We try to go maybe once a week, once every other week. And it's a private theater, so it's really, really cool. We just saw Tony. I think that was a really good movie. And then I'll play video games with my teammates. We'll be on 2K, we'll be on FC, the soccer game. I'm starting to read a lot. I like audiobooks. My girlfriend and I were reading this neurological faith book about life, which has been super cool. And then I just got a cat. I got a Munchkin cat.

Her name is Moochies. So that's another thing I've been super involved with. And she's super small, super tiny. I do a lot of different things when I'm not so focused on football. I just continue to expand myself.

#3529. How Many Marathons Is Too Many?

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Experts explain how often to race 26.2 without risking burnout, injury, or declines in performance.

It’s easy to catch the marathon bug. Influencers and everyday runners flood social media talking about their training, their accomplishments, and their speedy splits. And when your options range from exciting destination races and multiday challenge events to the coveted World Marathon Majors, it’s easy to get caught up in signing up for 26.2 after 26.2.

What does a race calendar that brings out your best performances—while still supporting your recovery and life outside of running—actually look like?

We talked to an exercise physiologist and a sports psychology researcher to find out the physical and mental effects of marathoning and how to determine the number of races per year that keeps you motivated and feeling accomplished without overwhelm or burnout.

It’s absolutely possible to go overboard on marathoning, Janet Hamilton, CSCS, an exercise physiologist and the founder and head coach of Running Strong, tells Runner’s World. Many factors play into the number of races per year that best serves an individual, she explains.

One of the most important to consider? Your overall goal.

“Do you have some sort of a time goal, like you want to qualify for Boston? Or do you just want to be able to say you’ve completed one every month? Those two goals are very different,” she explains.

Racing a marathon at an all-out effort places much more stress on the body than jogging one at your easy pace. Of course, both efforts still require recovery, adds Hamilton. “But if you have a specific performance goal, it behooves you to train with that purpose in mind,” she says.

This means setting aside a full cycle of training to properly base-build, add in speed, gradually taper, and finally, race. To achieve your best result, you can’t rush any part of this process.

Hamilton’s guidance for most runners who want to race well is to aim for a maximum of two, maybe three, marathons per year. “Can you get away with more than that? Sure. Some people complete streaks of marathoning every weekend,” she says. “But that can be really hard on your body, physiologically speaking.”

Significant physiological changes occur following a long-duration hard-effort exercise bout such as the marathon, explains Hamilton. This can include weakened immune function—part of the reason many runners come down with a postrace cold—elevation of enzymes that indicate cardiac damage, increased levels of the stress hormone cortisol, and a decrease in testosterone levels.

“Generally, your body will come back in balance with a proper recovery cycle,” Hamilton says. But if you never give yourself a break from racing, it becomes much more challenging for you to fully recuperate. “Over time, this takes a toll on your body. And you might not always be able to necessarily feel it.”

Some signs, she explains, would only show up in specific blood tests or diagnostic scans, such as an elevated cardiac calcification score, which can increase the risk for heart problems. “We can certainly do blood tests to look for markers of creatine kinase [an indicator of tissue trauma from microtears in the muscle], but how many of us get blood tested after every marathon?”

Fortunately, you don’t need a lab test to notice every red flag. Some more obvious symptoms that you’re overdoing it include persistent fatigue, getting sick more often, trouble sleeping, low mood, decreased motivation, and nagging aches or pains that don’t resolve with rest. You also might find that your usual pace on easy runs feels noticeably harder to maintain.

More and more exercise does not necessarily mean better and better physical fitness, Hamilton says. “At some point, that correlation between volume and health kind of starts to fall apart,” she says. And that not only goes for physical health but for mental health, as well.

According to a study published in Acta Psychologica, multimarathoners—defined as individuals who had completed at least 100 official marathons—had higher depression and anxiety scores compared with the general population, though still below clinical thresholds. “We found that high levels of physical activity don’t necessarily equate to good mental health,” says the study’s lead author, Leo Lundy, PhD.

Of the 576 runners in the study, about 30 percent had few or no symptoms of anxiety and depression, he explains. However, about 25 percent experienced higher levels than average. “What was particularly worrying was that 8 percent—which is a significant percentage—showed signs of severe depression or high anxiety, way above what you’d expect in the general public,” says Lundy.

This doesn’t mean that multimarathoning causes these symptoms; it does, however, show an association. And to understand the full picture, we need to examine the type of individual who is drawn to multimarathoning in the first place, Lundy explains.

“When you look at the type of person that's attracted to long-distance running, two areas of the personality were significant in the research,” he says. “One was conscientiousness: These individuals love to follow the rules. The other was emotional stability,” which assessed how calm or even-keeled they generally tended to be.

In psychometric tests, marathoners tended to score very high in conscientiousness but lower in emotional stability, which could correlate to higher levels of depression and anxiety, explains Lundy.

Enter: the marathon. This race provides exactly the environment that this personality type needs to thrive. “The marathon is very structured: You follow the rules, you turn up on time, and you know what’s going to happen. It’s all very predictable in that regard,” he says.

Add in the meaningful social connection that comes from gathering with fellow runners and the sense of achievement from completing a full 26.2 miles, and the picture becomes clearer: These benefits all help marathoners manage those lower levels of emotional stability. And by providing a reliable framework for them to succeed and belong, the race itself becomes a valuable coping mechanism for underlying anxiety and depression.

However, racing marathon after marathon can also mean sustained physical fatigue, competition pressure, increased injury risk, and sacrificing other aspects of life. So, Lundy explains, if a runner relies too heavily on marathoning and continues despite mounting physical, emotional, or lifestyle costs, it becomes a source of stress. As a result, it may contribute to the very symptoms a runner was trying to relieve.

Both mentally and physically, the amount of effort you put into 26.2 matters when taking on multiple marathons each year. But for some runners, especially those scoring high in conscientiousness, it can be difficult to toe the line and not give their best effort.

“As a coach, I consider the individual runner’s personality: How well will they be able to adhere to just doing it at a training effort when that gun goes off?” says Hamilton. Many runners will want to push the pace beyond an easy jog.

So if someone really wants to do a series of mostly easy marathons, she still advises the majority of athletes to complete no more than four marathons per year.

Take Lundy himself, for example, who, at age 62, has completed more than four hundred marathons, including seven just this year. Lundy says he still enjoys running marathons regularly, but emphasizes this crucial element: Fast performances are not the goal.

“The most successful multi-marathoners that I’ve come across are people that run at a sub-maximal level. They really are quite slow, quite determined, some running into the seven-hour ranges,” he says. “They’re running for lots of other reasons besides performance.”

Some of those reasons? Just to finish, to enjoy the social environment, to travel, to stay active and healthy. “I’ve run marathons in close to 25 countries; most of my friends are in running communities, so I see them regularly, and achieving new milestones continues to make me happy. That’s really what drives me,” Lundy says.

While the marathon gives you a specific goal, you don’t have to keep chasing finish lines to reap many of the same benefits Lundy mentions. Running in general supports cardiovascular and cognitive health while also providing a sense of fulfillment, connection, and belonging, he says.

So, you might decide to race 26.2 once, many times, or maybe not at all—the right number of marathons for you is the number that keeps you feeling strong, healthy, and happy enough to keep on running.

Morgan is a gear editor who has been with Runner’s World since 2017. She started as an intern ghostwriting The Warmup, a bygone version of the daily RW newsletter. Now, she tests and reviews anything you might find on runners’ feet-from crew socks and compression boots to carbon-plated super shoes.

A lifelong runner and shoe geek, Morgan has been chasing the perfect pair of kicks since she joined her grade school cross-country team. Since then, she ran as a Division I walk-on for the cross-country and track & field teams at the University of Delaware, where she studied English and Biology. She has one full marathon under her belt, and has raced more halfs and 5Ks than she can count.
