#1457. Tom Rhys Harries Is Ready for His Star Moment. He Just Doesn’t Really Want to Talk About It.

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Though he’s on the cusp of a career breakthrough in the DC Universe’s body horror flick Clayface, he’s already got his sights set on the next thing.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

This article was featured in MH Flex, our monthly newsletter recommending the one in-depth interview you need to flex your pop culture knowledge. Sign up here to get it first.

A FEW DAYS before we speak, Tom Rhys Harries turned 36 years old—and by his own admission, he didn’t make too big a deal out of it. Rhys Harries prefers not to dwell on the past and, beyond that, only spends so much time thinking about the present; his focus is often on what comes next. Still, he’s facing down an October full of milestones, and with them a press cycle full of questions about the comic book character he’s brought to life. A few short weeks after his birthday, the biggest project of his acting career will hit theaters: Clayface, the most experimental outing yet in James Gunn’s quasi-rebooted DC Universe and Rhys Harries’s debut as a blockbuster leading man.

The film is the sort of project fans have been begging Marvel and DC alike to take on for ages: a lower-budget exploration of a character (in this case, one best known as a Batman villain) through a real genre lens. And make no mistake, Clayface commits to the horror genre wholeheartedly. It is a gnarly, hard-R-rated body horror joint that had the audience at our small early screening squirming (Rhys Harries quietly joined in the back, the first time he’d seen the film with a proper audience of any kind). At the center of a cast featuring Naomi Ackie, Max Minghella, and Eddie Marsan is Rhys Harries as Matt Hagen, an actor on the cusp of superstardom whose career is derailed by a vicious attack from Gotham City mobsters. An experimental treatment provided by Ackie’s character begins to restore balance to his life, only to spur on a further descent into mutation and madness.

There are certainly parallels between Matt’s budding stardom and the position Rhys Harries finds himself in now, one where any future restaurant birthday dinners might come with a side of craning necks and iPhone camera flashes. It’s the sort of perpetual perception that pushes Matt’s life further and further off course in the film.

But Rhys Harries seems, for his part, unfazed by all this. When we speak on a sunny Sunday morning in Los Angeles, he’s in his home getting ready for a day of press. In talking about the film and his craft, he’s straightforward and thoughtful, completely devoid of frivolity or sound bite answers. It is—and I mean this as a compliment—very difficult to imagine him answering a series of canned “This or That” questions for a TikToker or YouTube channel. He lights up when talking about literature, motorcycles, the work of other filmmakers, and seems hesitant to center himself even when talking about the film he’s in. It does, as you get to know Rhys Harries a bit better, make sense. He grew up aspiring not to the Oscars but to pro rugby, a sport of uninterrupted play. If you find yourself on the wrong end of a tackle, nobody’s stopping to check on you. The game keeps moving forward, bloody noses and all.

Ahead of the release of Clayface, Rhys Harries tapped in with Men’s Health to discuss his leading man debut, body horror, and why fixing motorcycles helps to keep his head on straight.

MEN’S HEALTH: Was the screening last week your first time seeing Clayface with an audience?

TOM RHYS HARRIES: Yes, it was. I’ve seen it before with just the director [James Watkins] and Naomi [Ackie]. But even with last week’s screening being kind of a small room, yeah, it was my first time.

MH: What was that like? I know a few of us were reacting pretty viscerally to it at times.

TRH: There’s something specific about watching a movie like this in that environment. It would be completely different in a full theater, but there’s something kind of exposing about being in a room with, like, six or seven people. You can’t necessarily feel like you have the license to be vocal, even if you can’t help it at times.

MH: In the last week you’ve celebrated a birthday, seen Clayface with an audience for the first time, and in just a couple of weeks it’s going to hit theaters and most likely change a lot of things for you. What’s the experience of all this happening at once been like?

TRH: I don’t really get too fussed on my birthday. The older I get, I’m like, All right, that’s that. I feel like that with work as well: I’m pretty pragmatic, and I want things to go well for everyone involved. But I kind of want it to be done and out of the way—I like forward motion.

MH: The Matt Hagen/Clayface character—in a film filled with graphic, violent content—is an interesting choice for your blockbuster debut, but it’s been far from an overnight success for you. You’ve worked steadily for the last several years.

TRH: No, but there’s an undeniable aspect of reach here with these studio films. That’s a real thing. Regardless of whether people engage with it and it resonates, you do have wider reach with something like this.

MH: It’s a compelling decision, though—your breakout role coming as an actor who just got his first Oscar nomination and is really starting to cement himself as a movie star. It puts the concept of a leading man and performance under a microscope. What compelled you about Matt as a character and the film’s point of view on performance?

TRH: It’s a pretty multifaceted role. I felt like I was at a point in my career and life where I could meet the requirements of the role and a project like this. I say that with the caveat of knowing that I don’t think I would have been able to handle it when I was younger. I started acting kind of late—I wasn’t a child actor or anything. The role didn’t feel like something that was naturally in my wheelhouse. I had to spend a long time trying to feel as if I understood just a semblance of what it was about. I’m fairly inquisitive and curious, so I have to scratch at something for a long time before I feel like I have a handle on it. Maybe that speaks to an insecurity in me, or maybe it pertains to everyone. But there’s an element of it within us all, that question of Am I qualified to be doing this? It makes you think about yourself and overlaps, in this case, with acting quite a bit. But when you’re in a position like this, there’s just nowhere to hide. I felt like in taking this on I was kind of asking myself, What kind of actor do I want to be? What interests me? You keep going back to that and reviewing it and trying to push yourself, and that’s what I did here. I never want to rest on my laurels, even if that can be pretty seductive.

MH: In exploring those questions, what answers did you find? What interests you as a performer?

TRH: I think about this quite a lot, and ultimately I just feel that being able to do this for a living is such a privilege. And so I always want to ask myself what value I’m bringing to the table. How can I make what I’m doing bigger than this one thing? It has to exist in service to other people. I want to work with actors and writers and directors on projects that have something to say, because my job is to facilitate that and aid that.

MH: That feels not too far removed from something Matt is navigating in the film—how to exist in this rarefied air. What was your first impression of Matt when you read the script, and how did your take on him change as shooting progressed?

TRH: It felt clear early on that the film is concerned with vanity. You’ve got someone who’s navigating the world he’s found himself in and is playing to his strengths. I think what Matt desperately wants is to be additive and good. I know that sounds fairly simple, but when you have these extraordinary worlds and circumstances of comic book and science fiction film, they always work best when they have a firm grasp on the metaphor they’re trying to convey and have a root in something human. We’re able to look at them from a distance and then, at the appropriate time, clock the relevance of what it means to be human in our own world.

MH: There’s a decision the film makes in its approach to Matt that is particularly compelling. In act one he’s the subject of a smear campaign involving allegations of inappropriate behavior with women. The film never makes it clear how much of it is made up vs. how much of it has some root in reality. It adds a particular charge to certain scenes involving Naomi Ackie’s character. Throughout filming, how conscious were you of how Matt would be perceived in this regard, and how much were you tilting the balance in either direction?

TRH: I’m not really a fan of spoon-feeding an audience. One of my favorite films is Prisoners with Jake Gyllenhaal. He plays a cop who’s kind of got the bit between his teeth, but he also has this intricate tattoo that just peeks out from his shirt collar. It’s never explained, but alludes to a past that’s not as straight-laced as his present. Anything like that, within the medium of storytelling, gives the audience license to take ownership and imagine circumstances and color things out however they feel they should be.

Then again, I don’t know how much my thoughts on the issue matter. When people watch the film, they’ll get a snapshot of a man who’s arrived at this pivotal point in his life. Everything he’s done and everything that’s happened to him will be informing the decisions he makes and how he moves through the world. As an artist, I just try to load it up with as much tension as possible.

MH: You are, and pardon me here, a really handsome guy who took on a role of a handsome guy who gets his face sliced up and then spends a whole lot of the movie under some prosthetic or another. It’s a bit of an unconventional way to spend your blockbuster comic book movie debut, playing around with heavy makeup and gore effects rather than posing with your jawline at the perfect angle. Was this something that drew you to the production, and was there some level of intention in taking on a role that subverts traditional leading man good looks?

TRH: Honestly? I don’t think so. I really struggle with the calculation of thinking about what my next move will be. What I do know is sometimes I’ll read something and be surprised by it, energized by it—I’ll have some sort of reaction and realize it’s something I want to engage with. It just boils down to the story and if there’s something in it that resonates with me for whatever reason.

MH: It’s clear that you have this workmanlike approach to acting. You mentioned earlier not growing up in the industry, and I know you spent most of your young adulthood wanting to be a rugby player. Did growing up around that influence this no-nonsense approach you have now?

TRH: I love doing manual things. Acting, as a profession, is so subjective and internal. There’s no right or wrong approach or solution to it. I haven’t done it in a beat because I’ve been traveling so much, but I ride motorbikes. I love tweaking and tinkering with them because there’s no ambiguity to whether a bike functions or not. It runs or it doesn’t run. If you’ve done an adjustment and something feels off, then you know it’s broken. I’m drawn to counterbalancing things in that way.

It’s easy to get too intellectual or heady and pontificate on the art. And I think ultimately you’re just looking for if something feels right or doesn’t. A lot of the best and brightest actors I’ve worked with are able to just vibe something out. They wouldn’t necessarily be able to tell you how they got there. It’s just trust and instinct and feeling. And I like to counterbalance that experience by, like, pressure-washing the patio, because either you’ve cleaned it or you haven’t. There’s nothing to pontificate on there.

MH: You mentioned the practicality of working on bikes and how they either work or they don’t. Is that a pull from Zen and the Art of Motorcycle Maintenance?

TRH: Absolutely, yeah. I’m really fortunate to have people who have sort of been mentors to me or are just a little older, and one of my go-to touchstone people gave me that book when I was about 21. I also—I don’t want to get into the weeds with this because it could sound so pretentious—but I came across a Nietzsche book around the same time because I was doing a play with Nietzsche in it. So it was another weird counterbalance of the sort of gentle pondering of Zen and then having this book of nihilistic philosophy in my life all at once. I don’t know what kind of damage that must have done to me, reading those at such a young age.

It kind of draws back to Clayface, because so much of this movie is about what’s hardwired into our systems and what surfaces when we’re exposed to certain things at certain ages. Matt was exposed to violence at a really young age, and I believe violence is taught. That really interested me—he gains this new set of powers and with them the capacity to express himself in ways he’s never had access to before. So, on some level, he’s reverting back to a 7-year-old child and the violence he was taught then.

TRH: I don’t have one currently, but I’ve had a bunch over the years. My favorite was this matte black Harley that I was kicking about on the outskirts of London. I was in my 20s and saw someone drive through the streets of London on one, and then eight or nine years later, I could finally afford one of my own. I’ve also got loads of vintage Harley shirts and merchandise, but I feel like a fraud, because I haven’t owned one for a hot second now. I feel a bit fraudulent at the moment! I do have a license and I have had a Harley though, I promise.

#2579. The Promise & Peril Of Mom Friends

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

You may love them or hate them, but, for a huge chunk of time, you need them.

When my kids were younger, I had a few groups of women I considered my “mom friends” — the mothers of my children’s friends. Even if you don’t have mom friends, you’re probably aware of mom groups; the media loves them, the more gossipy and scandalous, the better. You’ll see it in the first-hand reports of toxic celebrity-mom group chats and mega-hits like Big Little Lies, which features rich moms, working-class moms, and a murder. These friendships are a focal point of our cultural imagination, where society can project all our ideas and expectations of women raising children in these times. But far from the media and the memes, mom groups are complicated. A source of comfort and camaraderie, yes, but also awkwardness — and sometimes even distress.

The good stuff first: there’s a lot to love about friendships with other mothers. They’re a built-in support network, there when you need them. There is always someone available to pick up your kid when you’re stuck in traffic or give a cuddle when they’re scared at a sleep over. They have an extra pair of shin pads at the soccer tournament when you forgot to pack them, or a nut-free snack on the field trip for your highly-allergic toddler. Underneath the relief these encounters engender lies a deeper and implicit exchange of trust. It says: Please take care of my kid. Please make sure they are cared for, supported, seen, loved. When we’re at our best, we’re working towards a shared goal: helping to raise each other’s children. Building and sustaining the village. There’s joy in that; a bit of magic, too.

These friendships also provide a rare space where we can acknowledge the realities of motherhood, the impossible things asked of women raising children. Whatever it is that’s weighing you down — a partner who calls caring for his own children “babysitting,” unhelpful administrators for a kid struggling at school, a job that won’t give you paid leave — other mothers have undoubtedly been there. They can confirm for you the ways in which society pretends to revere mothers in theory but crushes them in practice, and can help you find laughter in the absurdity of it all.

And yet. These friendships, so tender and interdependent in some ways, can also be a source of unease. It is sometimes tricky to figure out just how much of ourselves to share, and how much we really want to know about the other women in our proverbial village. Some of what we find out inadvertently is funny, or endearing, or the kind of information that brings us closer. On the school group chat we’ll find another mom who has the same sarcastic sense of humor, or who also hates the same teacher that we do. We find out which mom is a Scientologist, which one brags incessantly about her kids. Occasionally we’ll find out intimate things when someone seeks advice about a breast lump or impending separation that she hasn’t told the kids about yet.

But there are other times when scratching below the surface of these friendships and seeing what’s underneath is more complicated. For me, that often involves learning things about other mothers that I wish I hadn’t known. Because knowing requires a decision: what then? What do I do? Or say? Or not say? And, on the flip side, how much do I want to reveal about myself and my own opinions?

My kids go to an international school with a very diverse group of families from all over the world, with wildly different priorities and values than my own. I’m not just talking about voting differently (though there’s that, too); there are moms at school who are anti-immigrant or make jokes about gay people or have blinding amounts of wealth. Some of them yell at their nannies in front of me. Some don’t make their kids wear seatbelts. They think and talk and parent differently than I do. And I’m sure some of them don’t like or agree with me, find me self-righteous or judgmental, with no sense of humor. (Fair on 1 and 2, wrong on 3!) I’m sure I’m not everyone’s cup of tea, either.

For years in situations like these, I struggled to figure out where to draw the line, if at all; if my kids were physically safe, was there anything else I truly needed to consider before I agreed to that playdate or sleepover? Were there questions to ask? And if I didn’t like what I learned, what was I supposed to do rearrange carpool? Encourage my kids to find other friends, so that I didn’t have to socialize and rely on people I didn’t agree with? Is that possible? Perhaps more importantly, is it worth it?

When one of my kids was little and starting a new school, I met a mom at back-to-school night. I mentioned my work with human rights for migrant workers; she went on what I can only describe as a diatribe, without irony, about how domestic workers (the poorest and least protected of the working poor) are entitled and demanding and need to be paid less. And then she promptly invited my kid to a birthday party. Should I have declined the invitation? Said no and argued all night about international labor standards? Said yes and argued about labor standards? Did I even want to engage? In that case, it turned out, I did not. I wanted to go to a birthday party and eat cupcakes and exchange Lego gifts and not have to always be my full self, not have to ponder whether to respond when someone makes an off-color comment, risking either conflict or complicity. I wanted to make small talk, to think about what we have in common (our kids and their friendship), not what separates us (everything else).

Now that my kids are older, the complications of mom friendships have lost some of their intensity. In part, it’s because I don’t rely on other mothers as much as I used to; the days of chaperones and carpools have passed. And my kids have grown, have developed their own opinions and worldviews, largely unaffected by their friends’ parents, despite my fears. Like most teenagers, they’re discerning; they’ve identified a few adults they admire and have written off the rest. And I’ve seen that one of the benefits of living in a community with people who are so different from us is that my family has had more explicit conversations about topics like wealth and race and privilege than we might have had in a social world where people’s behaviors and choices looked like ours.

I realized, too, that my judgements of other moms for the ways they vote or talk or act, or my concerns about their judgements of me — they didn’t matter that much. After all, we weren’t close enough to really change each other’s minds about anything of substance. We weren’t real friends, we were mom friends. I can recognize this category of relationships for what they were: intimate and interdependent, and somehow still distant. I can acknowledge that these mom friends lived and talked in ways that were troubling to me, and that I also appreciated the role they played in my kids’ lives. And I’m reminded, of course, that this is what having a proverbial village is all about; back when our foremothers raised kids in actual villages, they weren’t all best friends, they probably didn’t even like each other that much.

They just had to know that they were there.

Saba Brelvi is a Muslim American writer from California. She holds a BA from Brown University and an MPH from Johns Hopkins University and has spent more than two decades working in the nonprofit sector. She currently resides in Abu Dhabi with her family. The Squatters is her debut novel.

For the kid who will always take first place in your heart.

When you’re pregnant, it’s easy to get bogged down in choosing the perfect baby name. How does it sound with your last name? Does it honor your family, or have a special meaning? #3280. Texas A&M Study Shows Creatine Adding Muscle Mass Gains Without Lifting

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

For better brain cognition through to superior strength and power, creatine is one of those supplements that seems to actually work. Thanks to its cell volumizing and energizing abilities, creatine allows us to lift heavier, recover faster, and focus better under stress. Now, a new study from Texas A&M University is touting that creatine can construct more muscle even without a structured resistance training program.

The study, published in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition, suggests that creatine could help adults to maintain lean tissue and strength, even without regular exercise. Of course, maintaining adequate levels of muscle mass is particularly important as we age because muscle loss, also known as sarcopenia, can begin as early as 35 years, and typically sees lean mass decline at a rate of 1 to 2 percent, per year, for the average person. In fact, those who fail to engage with regular resistance training could lose as much as 4 to 6 pounds of muscle per decade.

New meta-analyses show this tried and true supp can benefit both men and women—but you still have to...

For 12 weeks, men and women between the ages of 45 and 65 were provided with creatine or a maltodextrin placebo, with each group being split into those who wanted to participate in a supervised, structured exercise program, and those who didn’t want to commit to the organized sessions. As the participants filled out questionnaires during the study, data showed that the structured exercise group racked up more moderate-to-vigorous activity than those who weren’t locked in.

Despite lower levels of activity, those who took two 5g doses of creatine per day, and were not in the structured exercise program, increased their lean tissue by around 2.4 pounds over the 12-week study period. Conversely, those who took the placebo lost almost half a pound. “Creatine Monohydrate supplementation without exercise training and diet intervention increased lean tissue mass, strength, and muscular endurance while promoting favorable changes in selected blood lipids, HbA1c, and selected markers of cognitive function and memory,” read the report.

The results on muscle mass were even more profound in the structured exercise group, however. Creatine users who undertook the supervised sessions gained 2.8 pounds of lean mass on average. Creatine was also shown to preserve muscle even under the conditions of fat loss, because participants in the exercise program were given a modest calorie deficit. After 12 weeks, this group had gained almost three pounds of lean tissue despite dropping fat, while the exercise group that took a placebo showed little change in lean tissue. Still, both the structured and non-structured groups, on creatine, saw a greater reduction in body fat percentage than that seen by exercise or diet alone.

“Creatine Monohydrate supplementation with and without exercise and diet intervention may help middle-aged and older adults maintain muscle mass and strength while favorably affecting selected markers of health and cognitive function,” concluded the study.

#1465. Athletes From Every Sport Are Talking About ‘RED-S.’ Is Workout Hustle Culture to Blame?

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

YOU NEED TO fuel your body properly for peak performance—that's not news. But hitting the right balance of fuel to energy output can be tough, especially if you're trying to lose weight in the process, are on a GLP-1 medication, and/or recently switched up your workouts. That's where you fall into danger of RED-S, a scary sounding condition where your body reaches a dangerous stage of energy depletion

Also known as “Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport,” RED-S can do everything from slow down your progress to mess with your body on a cellular level. "The simplest explanation is it's an energy mismatch," says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab.

RED-S has always been a potential issue with athletes, but the rise of GLP-1s and a NO DAYS OFF workout hustle culture has pushed the condition into the fore.

Trainers say every active person should know the symptoms of RED-S—and what might cause them to show up.

RED-S HAPPENS WHEN you're not fueling your body properly. "It essentially means running your body's engine on empty," explains Dani Singer, CPT, founder of Fit2Go Personal Training. "It's a mismatch between the energy you take in through nutrition and the energy you expend through exercise and daily living."

While a slight calorie deficit is needed to lose weight, RED-S is more severe than cutting a few calories. It's chronically under-fueling while also trying to train at a high level. "You are demanding a level of output from your body that you aren't giving it the fuel to sustain," Singer says.

A FEW THINGS can go down. "Deep, lingering fatigue is usually the first red flag," Singer says. "You'll feel like you're moving through molasses." At the same time, because your body isn't dedicating energy to recovery, Singer says you become "highly susceptible" to overuse injuries and stress fractures due to decreased bone mineral density.

"You'll also likely see thermoregulation issues—always feeling cold—compromised immunity, hormonal imbalances, and ironically, a complete plateau in your fitness progress," Singer says. "You can't build muscle or acquire new physical skills if your body doesn't have the building blocks to do so."

With RED-S, your body does what it can with reduce energy it has. "It really prioritizes key metabolic functions and the things that require your body to function [in daily life]," Matheny says. He compares RED-S to being in low power mode—your body is going to try to support basic functions, while forgetting about things like muscle growth and fitness performance.

"We are constantly taught that 'eat less, move more' is the holy grail of fitness, but your body needs energy not just to exercise, but to simply keep the lights on for things like breathing, hormone production, and cellular repair," Singer says. "When you severely under-fuel while keeping your physical output high, your body goes into triage mode."

THERE ARE LIKELY a few things behind this. "For the general fitness population, it often comes from a toxic combination of diet culture and high-intensity workout trends demanding maximum output on minimum calories," Singer says.

And then there are GLP-1s. "These medications are incredibly effective at blunting the natural hunger cue," Singer explains. "A user might only be taking in 800 to 1,000 calories a day simply because they feel completely full." If someone on a GLP-1 decides to pair their weight loss journey with a rigorous new workout routine, they may not realize how little fuel is in the tank, Singer says.

BECAUSE EVERY PERSON’S energy needs are different, avoiding or finding a solution to RED-S can be complex. "The threshold in literature is 30 calories per kilogram of body weight—that's the lowest level," Matheny says. (That means a 180-pound man should be taking in 2,448 calories per day at a minimum.)

Singer also recommends flipping the way you look at food. "We have to stop viewing food purely as a metric to minimize and start viewing it as fuel for the capabilities we want to build," he says. Singer suggests being "brutally honest" with your baseline food intake, making sure you're eating enough to support your activity.

"From there, listen to the biofeedback your body gives you," he says. "If you are constantly exhausted, sore for days after a basic workout, or losing your motivation to train, you are likely under-fueled." Singer recommends prioritizing protein for recovery, along with getting in enough carbs to fuel the work you're asking of your body. "A good routine should improve how you move and feel in daily life, not break you down," he says.

If you're struggling to get the balance right, consider talking with a sports-minded registered dietitian. They should be able to help you figure out what you're going wrong and put you on the path to fueling your body properly in the future.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#3803. What to expect from Centre Pompidou’s vast new conservation-focused site in the Parisian suburbs

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The front of the Centre Pompidou Francilien, with an exterior created using Douglas fir, overlooks a lake and a park

The Centre Pompidou has unveiled a new, large site in a southern suburb of Paris focused on conservation, restoration and storage. Centre Pompidou Francilien – fabrique de l’art in Massy, which will also host exhibitions and residencies, has been open for three “Prélude!” weekends this month ahead of its inauguration in March.

The three-storey rectangular building spans 30,000 sq m. Most of the site is devoted to storage (13,000 sq m) and to conservation and restoration ateliers (4,000 sq m), which are inaccessible to the public. Around 3,000 sq m is open to visitors, including 2,200 sq m of exhibition space.

The impetus for the creation of the institution stemmed from a wish to have a single facility in which to store works from its expansive collection, when they are not on display at its main site, Centre Pompidou, or on loan. This importance of this function has been heightened in light of the Centre Pompidou closing for renovations until 2030.

Centre Pompidou’s holdings comprise some 150,000 Modern and contemporary works of art—more than any other institution in Europe and second globally to New York’s Museum of Modern Art. The works are owned by the French State and held by the Musée national d’art modern, which is located at the Centre Pompidou site.

Over the next two years, 645 trucks will transport the collection, currently dispersed across eight rented spaces, to Centre Pompidou Francilien. Once unloaded, each work will be examined, treated and decontaminated, if necessary, in the studios before being placed in the reserves. There are 20 large storage areas in total; large-scale sculptures and installations will be kept on the ground floor, lighter works on the first and second floor.

The site will also serve as a centre for the dispersal of the around 6,000 works that Centre Pompidou loans each year, an operation that will be facilitated by the site’s proximity to Orly airport.

Speaking to The Art Newspaper, Alexia Fabre, the director of the Centre Pompidou Francilien, emphasises the importance of the workshop facilities. “It is a revolution for the expertise of the Centre Pompidou; it means all our teams working on restoration, framing and conservation will be in the same place,” she says.

Centre Pompidou Francilien was designed by the architect Philippe Chiambaretta and his Parisian agency, PCA-STREAM. The front of the building, with an exterior created using Douglas fir, overlooks a lake and a park. This side serves as the entrance for the public, providing access to exhibition spaces and the “Belvédère” roof terrace, which hosts works of art. An external escalator pays tribute to the well-known outdoor escalator at the Centre Pompidou.

An external escalator nods to the outdoor escalator at the Centre Pompidou’s main site in central Paris

The back of the building, adjacent to a rugby pitch, is clad in a double layer of concrete, holding the artworks “like a safe”. “This part is like a hospital, with ambulances coming and going with all the artworks,” Chiambaretta says.

His firm was inspired, he adds, by the approach taken to the design of the Centre Pompidou, which opened in 1977. “We weren’t after a spectacular, wow effect, but something like [the architects] Renzo Piano and Richard Rogers created with the Centre Pompidou—very simple and logical,” Chiambaretta says. “We dived into how they aspired to create a centre for all the arts —cinema, art, music, dance, literature—and the collection is like the [culmination] of 50 years of this philosophy,” Chiambaretta adds.

Greeting visitors on the ground floor is an atelier for families to make objects with recycled materials by Flora Koel and a café. A closed glass door offers a glimpse of works by artist such as Jean Dubuffet and Germaine Richier that are housed in a storage room.

On the first floor are exhibition spaces, which will host two shows a year, as well as a large conference room, workshops and a residency studio. The inaugural exhibition in March, 150,000!, will focus on the history of the collection, tracing acquisitions, the arrival of works of art at the new site and conservation issues that have arisen. There will also be conferences and workshops exploring how works are restored.

The preview weekends are set to continue despite planned protests this Saturday (24 October), which follow widespread demonstrations across France that escalated into violence last weekend.

Works have been put on view in these spaces for this period. They include Elsa Werth’s text-based installation that developed from staff at the institution asking local residents to respond to titles of works of art; a video by Hicham Berrada depicting chemicals being mixed together to create an aquatic landscape; and Xavier Veilhan’s black-and-white film Vent Moderne (2015) in which dancers are shown performing in Modernist villas, their movements responding to the architecture.

A still from Xavier Veilhan's Vent Moderne (2015) Courtesy of Xavier Veilhan's studio

The institution is partnering with the Paris-Saclay university campus. “We want to work with [scientific teams at] the universities on climate issues concerning restoration, and develop dialogues between artists and scientists,” Fabre explains.

Fabre is keen to forge relationships with and promote access for the local population of Massy, a historically working-class neighbourhood that has been undergoing gentrification. School groups will be able to book to visit before the centre opens to the public. Admission fees to Centre Pompidou Francilien are low relative to other large institutions in Paris: €10, €6 reduced ticket (students, senior citizens, large group bookings), and exhibitions are free up to the age of 26 and for job seekers.

The project has cost €105 million, with €42 million provided by local authorities, including the Île-de-France region (€20 million), the department of Essonne (€10 million), the Paris-Saclay scientific research hub (€9 million) and the town of Massy (€3 million). The French state and the Centre Pompidou are financing the remaining sum, through a loan agreement with Vinci Construction. The town of Massy donated the site, which is a former football pitch.

François Durovray, the president of the departmental council of Essonne, wrote on X that the unveiling was a “historic day for Essonne” and that the site was a “fantastic promise of openness, discovery and transmission”.

The institution will be closed for its own 50th anniversary—but will work with the city's museums, including the Louvre, to stage projects during the period

While Paris museum undergoes renovations, Europe's biggest modern and contemporary art collection will go on tour—keeping curators employed

Housed in a huge former Citroën garage, the space will be home to modern and contemporary art, including loans from the French museum

#835. One of Earth’s most powerful supervolcanoes is refilling with fresh magma

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

One of Earth's most powerful supervolcanoes is slowly refilling its enormous underground magma reservoir, thousands of years after producing one of the largest volcanic eruptions in recent geological history. Scientists at Kobe University have discovered evidence that fresh magma is accumulating beneath Japan's Kikai caldera, which unleashed a catastrophic eruption about 7,300 years ago.

The discovery offers a rare look at how giant volcanic systems rebuild their magma supplies after massive eruptions. It could also provide important clues about the behavior of other supervolcanoes, including Yellowstone in the United States and Toba in Indonesia, potentially helping scientists improve their ability to recognize warning signs of future eruptions.

How Supervolcanoes Store Enormous Amounts of Magma

Some volcanic eruptions are so immense that they dramatically reshape the landscape. The amount of magma released can be staggering, enough to bury all of New York City's Central Park beneath a layer roughly 12 kilometers (7.5 miles) thick.

When such enormous volumes of magma escape from underground, the rock above the emptied reservoir can collapse, leaving behind a broad, relatively shallow depression known as a caldera. These features are associated with some of the most powerful eruptions in Earth's history.

Famous examples include Yellowstone, Toba, and Japan's Kikai caldera, most of which lies beneath the ocean. Kikai last experienced a giant eruption approximately 7,300 years ago, producing the largest known volcanic eruption of the Holocene, the geological epoch that began roughly 11,700 years ago and continues today.

Although scientists know that giant caldera volcanoes can erupt more than once, they still have an incomplete understanding of how the enormous quantities of magma required for these events accumulate underground. This makes it extremely difficult to anticipate when another major eruption might occur.

"We must understand how such large quantities of magma can accumulate to understand how giant caldera eruptions occur," says Kobe University geophysicist Nobukazu Seama.

Scientists Discover a Massive Magma Reservoir Beneath Japan

Kikai's underwater location might seem like an obstacle to studying the volcano, but it actually gives researchers an important advantage. The surrounding seafloor allows scientists to carry out extensive geological surveys across the volcanic structure.

"The underwater location allows us to implement systematic, large-scale surveys," Seama explains.

To investigate what lies beneath Kikai, researchers from Kobe University collaborated with the Japan Agency for Marine-Earth Science and Technology (JAMSTEC). They used powerful airgun arrays to generate seismic pulses that traveled through the crust beneath the ocean floor.

Instruments called ocean bottom seismometers recorded how these waves moved through the underground rock. Because seismic waves travel differently through solid rock and regions containing molten material, scientists can analyze their behavior to investigate structures that cannot be observed directly.

The findings, published in Communications Earth & Environment, revealed a substantial region containing a high proportion of magma directly beneath the site of Kikai's massive eruption 7,300 years ago.

By examining the seismic measurements, the team was able to determine the approximate size and shape of the underground reservoir. Its position and extent suggest that it occupies the same magma storage system involved in the ancient eruption.

"Due to its extent and location it is clear that this is in fact the same magma reservoir as in the previous eruption," Seama says.

Fresh Magma Is Recharging the Ancient Supervolcano

Perhaps the most intriguing finding is that the magma currently beneath Kikai does not appear to be simply leftover material from its catastrophic eruption thousands of years ago.

Previous investigations revealed that a new lava dome has been developing near the center of the caldera over approximately the past 3,900 years. A lava dome forms when relatively thick, viscous lava accumulates around a volcanic vent rather than flowing freely across the surrounding landscape.

Chemical analyses also showed that volcanic material from this dome and other more recent activity differs in composition from the material released during Kikai's giant eruption.

That difference provides an important clue. It suggests that the magma now stored beneath the volcano was supplied by new injections of molten rock from deeper within Earth's crust or mantle, rather than remaining unchanged since the previous eruption.

"This means that the magma that is now present in the magma reservoir under the lava dome is likely newly injected magma," summarizes Seama.

Together, the seismic observations and chemical evidence help researchers develop a clearer picture of how a giant volcanic system can gradually rebuild its underground magma supply following a massive eruption.

This replenishment process does not mean that another catastrophic eruption is imminent. Instead, it reveals how a magma reservoir can be recharged over thousands of years, an important process that scientists need to understand before they can reliably assess the long-term behavior of supervolcanoes.

What the Discovery Could Reveal About Yellowstone

The findings could have implications far beyond Japan.

Researchers believe the process identified beneath Kikai may help explain why other enormous caldera systems, including Yellowstone and Toba, also contain large magma reservoirs relatively close to Earth's surface.

"This magma re-injection model is consistent with the existence of large shallow magma reservoirs beneath other giant calderas like Yellowstone and Toba," says Seama.

The proposed model suggests that, following a giant eruption, new magma can gradually enter and replenish an existing underground reservoir. Understanding how this happens could reveal important patterns in the long-term cycles of magma accumulation, volcanic activity, and major eruptions.

Researchers now hope to improve the seismic techniques used at Kikai so they can investigate the movement and storage of magma in greater detail.

"We want to refine the methods that have proved to be so useful in this study to more deeply understand the re-injection processes. Our ultimate goal is to become better able to monitor the crucial indicators of future giant eruptions."

By revealing how fresh magma accumulates beneath a volcano responsible for one of Earth's most extraordinary eruptions, the research brings scientists closer to understanding the processes that eventually make such colossal events possible.

This research was funded by the Ministry of Education, Culture, Sports, Science and Technology (MEXT) (The Third Earthquake and Volcano Hazards Observation and Research Program (Earthquake and Volcano Hazard Reduction Research)) and the Japan Society for the Promotion of Science (grant 20H00199). It was conducted in collaboration with researchers from the Japan Agency for Marine-Earth Science and Technology (JAMSTEC).

Materials provided by Kobe University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#1532. Is the Secret to Ultrarunning Success Downing Crazy High Carbs?

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Carbs are rising. Records are falling. How David Roche found a new way to fuel to go the distance.

Welcome to the Wild World of Trail Running! We're diving deep on the races, training tips, and gear you need to know to get outside off paved roads to run a whole new way. Click here for more stories about the trail explosion.

This story was originally published on July 17, 2025.

IN THE SUMMER of 2024, runner and coach David Roche made a stunning announcement. He’d won a slot in the Leadville Trail 100 Mile, one of the oldest and most iconic races in the sport of Ultramarathon, which includes any race over 26.2 miles.

Not only is Leadville one of the most storied races, but it also had one of the oldest course records, which many people believe was unbeatable. But Roche was not one of them. He said he could beat the nearly two decade-old time of 15 hours, 42 minutes and 59 seconds.

The announcement was shocking for many reasons, but one stood out: Roche had never raced a hundred miles.

There were many doubters, including Roche himself. “I was so full of doubt and questioning,” he says now. “I had no idea if it was actually possible. But I knew based on the numbers that I could put out that if I was able to execute the way I thought could, then not only was the record possible, it could go down by a substantial margin.”

There were several reasons for Roche’s confidence. Ultrarunning is witnessing a number of innovations that are pushing times down: New carbon-plated trail shoes, advances in understanding the benefits of heat training, and the adoption of consuming sodium bicarbonate by elite runners to name a few.

But one innovation has garnered the most attention: The growing use of a “high carb” approach to training and racing, a tactic that started in cycling and is now moving into the rest of the endurance world, including ultrarunning.

Roche, as a coach and a runner, is one of high-carb’s leading proponents, and at Leadville he planned to consume between 120 to 140 grams of carbohydrates—around 500 calories—per hour. When he looked at his spreadsheet, it predicted he would beat the record by more than 15 minutes.

THE START LINE of one of ultrarunning’s most legendary races was, by some measures, an unlikely place for Roche to end up. He grew up in farming country on the eastern shore of Maryland. In high school he played football, weighed in at 200 pounds, and even played for Columbia University, where he studied environmental science. But after his freshman year, he quit football and started biking and running. He never imagined himself as a ultrarunner

“I used to think that my best event would be 800 meters and anything above that, I would get progressively worse, because I come from a football background. I always assumed it wasn't going to be my skill set.”

After college, when Roche moved to North Carolina to study environmental law at Duke University, he started running on the trails around Durham. He also met his wife Megan, who was studying neuroscience and playing field hockey. The two started frequenting the trails around the city together and both got more into running. Roche started doing trail races, and started coaching. In 2018, after Megan finished her medical program at Stanford, the two moved to Boulder, Colorado, where they both coach full time.

Roche had always advised his athletes to “shoot their shot” and go for big goals. But life was catching up. The couple had their first son in 2022, and time was slipping by. So in early 2023, Megan gave him a wakeup call. “She was like ‘David look: You're 35 and who knows how many years you have at the top level. Start to do what you say and shoot your shot.”

That April Roche signed up for the Canyons 100k in Auburn, California, which is a “Golden Ticket” race for the Western States 100 Mile, the most prestigious race in ultrarunning. The race went well, until he took a gel from an aid station that didn’t agree with him. He immediately vomited, then couldn’t keep anything down and ended up walking for six miles, finishing in fifth place. He knew he needed to figure out exactly what nutrition would work.

At the time, Roche was reading about, and experimenting with, higher levels of carbohydrate intake which had become standard in elite cycling, even though it was assumed that the body could only process 60 to 90 grams per hour. More fuel allows you to sustain a higher effort for longer.

Trying different gels and drink mixes, he pushed his carbs up to 120, 130 and 140 grams per hour. And in December of 2023, he entered the McDowell Mountain Frenzy 50 Miler and won. By the summer of 2024, he was ready for the Silver Rush 50 mile in Leadville.

He won by 25 minutes, and earned a slot in the Leadville 100 mile.

ROCHE KEPT TRAINING, both his legs and his stomach, taking in carbs at levels many regard as controversial. But even as he did, getting to the start line of Leadville was itself a kind of victory for other reasons.

In training, Roche only runs five days a week (and at lower mileage than most elite ultrarunners, with a focus on speed). Some days he rides his bike, which is what he was doing in April of 2024 when a car turned into him and sent him flying into a fence. He woke up with a concussion and a broken wrist. But as soon as he was recovered enough, he kept training (running with his arm in a sling) and keeping up the carbs.

“I trained like a competitive eater,” Roche says, learning to take in the amount of fluid and gels he’d need. “People in the competitive eating world actually practice with fluids, because they don't want to eat hot dogs every day. So I would use fluids to practice the bloat and teach my body not to reject that amount, because I knew I'd have to take in up to 56 ounces per hour and not reject it.”

By August, Roche was ready in his body. In his head, slightly less so.

“I still had so much doubt about whether it was possible or even a worthwhile goal to chase, with kids and work all the stuff going on,” he says now.

But Megan did want him to race, and so at 4 a.m. in Leadville, when the starting gun went off, he pushed those doubts aside and focused on the numbers. Not long after the start, he checked his heart rate.

“I knew how many calories-ish I burned at certain heart rates,” Roche says. “And when I looked at my heart rate, it was saying: ‘Look, you can go faster and still replace your calories.’ So a few miles into the race I was like, ‘All right, I guess it's time to race now.”

Roche made a rule for himself that he had to run every step of the way, with no walking. He describes the race as “incredibly fun,” which is not something people usually say about 100-milers.

“We saw him at Twin Lakes (mile 40) the first time and he looked great,” says his pacer Teddy Bross. “When came back into Twin Lakes (mile 60), in all honesty, he looked about the same.”

Bross ran the last 40 miles with Roche, and says there was one touch-and-go moment on the “power lines” climb where Roche started getting nauseous. But he was able to slow down, breathe and get it under control. Then the two runners sped on.

“On flats and downhills, I think we were somewhere around seven minutes a mile,” says Bross. “We were cooking,”

As they approached the finish line, Roche’s gap over second place widened. By the time he crossed it, he’d beaten his own prediction by one minute.

His time of 15:26:34 beat the Leadville record by more than 16 minutes.

THERE ARE MANY opinions on the so-called “high carb revolution,” or “carb mania,” as Alex Hutchinson, author of Endure: Mind, Body, and the Curiously Elastic Limits of Human Performance, calls it.

“The high-carb stuff is very much an open question scientifically,” Hutchinson says, “There's some logic, some anecdotes, and a tiny bit of data, but there are also lots of reasons to be skeptical. The thing with ultrarunning is that it's pretty much impossible to run randomized trials where people do all-out hundred-milers over and over. Everyone is making some guesses and doing some self-experimentation, but some people are more willing to try longshots.”

Andy Jones-Wilkins, a coach who has been running ultras since the 1990s, has seen a lot of trends come and go. He calls the push for high-carb fueling an "incredible marketing opportunity” which companies are taking advantage of, but cautions that it might not be the best strategy for mid- and back-of-the-pack runners.

“For athletes who are maybe a little older, in their 40s or above, somewhere in the range of 60 to 70 grams of carbs is per hour is plenty, especially if you're going to be going for more than six, seven eight hours.” The main issue, he says, is gastrointestinal distress, since slower runners aren’t working at the same intensity as elites, and just don’t need as many carbs.

“We know that ultras give people enough GI tract problems,” says Jones-Wilkins, “And to add to that by encouraging people to pump in over 100 grams of carbs, I would just urge people to tread carefully and experiment, because there's all kinds of stuff that can happen when you're eating a gel version of Thanksgiving dinner, then trying to run.”

Jason Koop, author of Training Essentials for Ultrarunning, says that while high-carb fueling seems new, it’s not. “The pro peloton [in cycling] has been doing this for years,” Koops says. “So anybody who thinks they're doing something new is either fooling themselves or hasn’t been paying attention. The origin of it is in very high-performance cycling, of which extremely few humans on the planet actually have a need to—or even can—ingest that amount of carbohydrate.”

Tyler Fox is a coach and a runner (coached by Roche) who’s had some success at the “lower end of high carb,” And while he does recommend higher carb intake for his athletes, it’s with a lot of caveats, including an athlete’s fitness and ability to maintain intensity.

“I think the narrative should be ‘fueling the work,’” Fox says. “Because if you're not able to sustain things from a mechanical perspective you're limited later by your ability to put out power.” According to Fox, in a 10-hour race, if you can run at a high intensity, you're going to be able to use more carbs, “but if you run at a conservative, less taxing output, your burn rate is going to be lower. And that's where you could run into high carb backfiring.”

In other words, if the carbs aren't being burned, they’ll find a way out though other channels, and you're risking a blowout on the side of the trail. Still, Fox sees the upside to higher carb intake with nuance. “I'm not suggesting that it's exclusively for higher level athletes” Fox says. “It does allow you to do more work, if you're prepared for it. But your training has to support it.”

Anna Gibson, a track and sub-ultra trail runner also coached by Roche, is sold on the benefits of high-carb fueling, and definitely has the training to support it. Last year the former NCAA athlete ran a 1500 meter PR, and competed in the Olympic trials. “In my last race I was putting down 110 grams of carbs per hour,” Gibson says. “It definitely took a lot of gut training to get to that point. But I credit that with my increase in performance.”

That race was the Gorge Waterfalls 30k, in which she won, broke the course record and finished second outright.

Despite all the unknowns, there’s no doubt that Roche’s output is enough to burn whatever he can cram in. “I think David has a clear understanding of what the science shows,” says Hutchinson, “and, just as importantly, what it doesn't show. Some people might look at that evidence and say, 'Nah, not quite enough to convince me to try it.' But David's an optimist: he weighs the odds and says, 'So you're telling me there's a chance!'

“I used to always think that I just sucked at the end of long races,” he says, “I can remember so many 50ks where I was like, 'Well, if I'm dying at mile 26, why would I go farther than this? Clearly I'm fighting my physiology.’

“Then I essentially went from normal fueling to high fueling and all of a sudden I become one of the best. So at the end of the day, it's really just: You give me 75 gels and I can give you the world.”

BACK HOME AFTER the Leadville 50, Roche’s recovery was fast. He says that after a day of feeling terrible, he improved quickly. In a couple weeks he was back to running form, which he attributes to his high-carb approach.

“I think the magic of carbs isn't race day performance necessarily. It's how much it helps you adapt and recover from training styles that will level up your fitness over time. So I'm going to be able to respond better to this workout, then come back stronger from it and stack up training in a way that makes me 36-years-old going on 22. “

Yet even as he recovered, Roche started to wonder if he got lucky in Leadville. “I thought, ‘What if it was all a fluke? What if I didn't actually learn anything about high-carb fueling or training or anything? What if I just had a good day at the right time and basically it was the stars aligning?”

To banish his doubts, Roche signed up for the Javelina Jundred in Arizona, just two months after Leadville. The race would be hot—nearly 100 degrees—so he started heat training, and practicing getting more fluids in.

At Javelina, Roche flew through the course, winning in 12:45:04 and missing the course record by less than two minutes. “The difference between Leadville and Javelina,” Roche says, “is that at Leadville, even in the moment, it felt like something magical was happening. It felt like something I don't know if I'm ever going to be able to repeat. But at Javelina I felt like I could do this any Saturday.”

The victory also earned him a “golden ticket” to the prestigious Western States 100 Mile.

Roche continued training and racing through the spring of 2025, with some of the usual ultrarunning ups and dowsn. In March he sprained an ankle in the Big Alta 50k, and had to drop out. But in May, he raced the Quad Rock, 50 Miler, winning by over 40 minutes and setting a new course record. The win gave him a “huge boost in confidence” for Western States in June.

WHETHER HIGH-CARB fueling will work for most runners remains to be seen. While there is some indication it might be different from previous fads like barefoot running, fat-adapted training and so on, runners should still proceed with care.

“Your average ultrarunner,” says Koop, “and even your good ultrarunner, needs to be cautious about titrating this up just because they saw somebody on Instagram do it. In many cases it's not necessary based on the energetic demands, and in most cases it’s not even pragmatic, because they can't take in that much carbohydrate.”

Jones-Wilkins agrees. “My prediction is that we’ll see kind of a settling into the fact that high carb works for the elites, but it doesn't work for the vast majority of people. They'll settle somewhere in the middle. But I urge athletes to tread carefully and experiment with what works for them. Because what works for me might not work for you and might not work for the next guy.”

ON JUNE 28, Roche lined up with some of the best in the world including legendary mountain runner Kilian Jornet, who won the race in 2011. This time, he wasn’t making any bold predictions.

“I’m a big-time underdog,” Roche said before the race, “even an underdog for top five. And that's the way I like it. I know I can run fast there relative to historic times. It's just a question of how fast everybody else goes.”

They went out very fast, with Roche in the lead up initial four-mile climb. Then as they moved through the mountains, a pack of at least twelve elite men pushed a sub-record pace. They hung together for the first 30 miles, all running within a minute or two of each other.

Roche was in the mix, hovering around fifth place, running shoulder-to-shoulder with Jornet and the others. The race was going according to plan. By mile 40, in the heat of the day, things started to spread out. At mile 40, Roche came in right behind race leaders Chris Myers and (eventual winner) Caleb Olson. At mile 56, he was solidly in fifth place.

“I felt great almost the whole time, even when I came into Michigan Bluff right behind Jeff. I was in a great spot. In retrospect, I was in the ideal spot.”

But soon after leaving the Michigan Bluff aid station, on the downhill, Roche’s race also started going downhill. He suspects he took in too many electrolytes given his heat training. But for whatever reason, his vision got blurry and he had some minor GI distress. So he dialed down the carbs, which he now thinks was a mistake.

“I went lower when I should have gone higher,” he says. “The gels were going down fine. But even after that I was able to recover once my sodium levels got better. I moved up and felt great. Then the lack of gels hit me, and that lead to some problems on the mental end.

Roche started fixating on his health, catastrophizing, and was suddenly filled with anxiety about seeing his kids again. He hesitates to call it a panic attack, but when his pacer, Teddy Bross (who is a physician) gave him some breathing exercises, they helped. Looking back he feels like he prepared his body, but not his mind.

“I think because of life being so white knuckled with two young kids and everything,” he says, “I didn't do the basic stuff I always ask athletes to do as a coach—like make it okay to get to that place where it feels kind of scary. Basically, I didn't nail down the mental side as much as I needed to going into a 100 mile.” He ended up dropping at 62 miles.

But as every ultrarunner knows, DNFs are just as important as getting on the podium, if not moreso. “The magic of ultrarunning,” Roche says, “is that you get stripped down to your core and from that you can grow back stronger. So I'm excited to get back out there and do another 100. And if I do qualify [for Western States] again, you’d better believe I'm coming back.”

Editor’s note: After the initial publication of this article, Roche reset his Leadville 100 record in August 2025, running a time of 15:12:30.

Frank Bures is the author of Pushing the River: An Epic Battle, a Lost History, a Near Death, and Other True Canoeing Stories. His work has appeared in publications including Harper’s, The Atlantic, Outside, and Bicycling, and he previously wrote about altruistic kidney donors for Men’s Health. He lives, runs and coaches in Minneapolis.

#1579. The Best Lace-Up Boots for Men, According to Style Editors

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

From Timberland to Steve Madden, here’s styles that you need your hands on.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I’M A BIG believer that every guy needs at least one good pair of lace-up boots in his fall shoe lineup. Some have the ability to handle a rainy commute; others provide comfort during a long day on your feet. And no, they all don’t give off camping trip vibes. I love how much character a good pair can bring to an outfit, whether I’m wearing mine with straight-leg jeans and a leather jacket or dressing things up with tailored trousers and a chunky knit sweater. With all the different styles out there, from rugged work boots to sleeker leather models, there’s something for just about every guy and his wardrobe. If you’re looking to add a pair to your fall rotation, my fellow fashion guys and I at Men’s Health have rounded up the best lace-up boots worth your money.

If I had to recommend one pair of lace-up boots that can do a little bit of everything, I’d start with Timberland’s classic, wheat-colored boots. The brand has been making boots since 1973, and this silhouette has grown from practical workwear into a menswear icon. Often nicknamed the “Butters,” they’re instantly recognizable for their golden nubuck leather, high-top profile, and chunky lug sole. From my teens up until now, they’ve earned a spot in my rotation. And boots that have that kind of staying power, or work with as many outfits.

Another part of the reason they’ve been around for so long is because Timberlands move between style categories. They’re rooted in workwear, but their influence runs deep in hip-hop, streetwear, and sports culture, too. Timothée Chalamet has even put them to work on a red carpet. And with the ’90s and early-2000s aesthetic still in style, the boots feel right at home alongside relaxed-baggy pants, boxy jackets, and layered knits. I’ll wear mine with relaxed denim for an easy everyday fit, but they also play well with cargos and heavier winter layers. They’re definitely heftier than a sleek dress boot and take a few wears to break in, but the laces let you adjust the fit to your liking.

Merrell’s offering is perfect for avid hikers because it’s designed for days when the weather can turn and the terrain gets rocky. The waterproof membrane seals out water while allowing moisture to escape, and the combination of pig suede leather and breathable mesh gives the upper durability and ventilation. I also like the bellows tongue, which helps keep dirt and trail debris from sneaking inside. Underfoot, an air cushion in the heel absorbs shock and adds stability over uneven ground. It’s not the most stylish boot, but you can get away with wearing it to a casual lunch in the park.

When I want a lace-up boot to be the star of the show, I put on this pair from Steve Madden. At 5.5 inches tall, the shaft hits around the ankle, giving the boot a chunky look without coming too far up the leg. The mix of black leather and suede gives the upper a nice contrast in texture. It also has a sturdy lug sole and chunky 1.5-inch heel that give it a rugged-meets-fashion-forward look, which adds a little edge to even the most basic jeans-and-tee combination.

I love how they go with dark-wash or black denim, where the monochromatic color palette lets the boot’s texture and hardware do the talking. The standout detail is the blue-toned metal eyelets where the laces thread through, which breaks up the all-black build.

Our grooming editor, Sean Zucker, put Amberjack’s Chukka through its paces and was impressed by how comfortable it felt right out of the box. The full-grain leather upper and soft sheepskin lining give it a refined finish. The best feature is the heat-activated insole, which gradually molds to your feet with wear over time. The brand paired it with a lightweight outsole that feels more like a cushioned sneaker than a traditional boot. Zucker wore it on rainy commutes, into the office, and around the city on weekend errands, without dealing with sore arches or slipping on wet sidewalks during his testing. I’d put this firmly in the smart-casual category: It works especially well with chinos and tailored trousers, but looks just as natural with dark denim.

If your job keeps you on construction sites or any other settings that require you to be on your feet for hours at a time, Keen’s waterproof boot is built with that kind of workday in mind. Home builder Matt Risinger recommends the style from the brand because “the break-in period is nonexistent”. The carbon toe cap helps protect against impact and compression, and it weighs 15% less than as traditional steel toe. The waterproof membrane seals out while allowing sweat to escape, and the oil- and slip-resistant rubber outsole adds traction on the job. I also like this option’s overall aesthetic, which resembles a high-top sneaker versus bulky work boot. Underfoot, brand’s proprietary lightweight midsole delivers energy return, giving responsive cushioning for those long shifts when you don’t have the luxury of sitting down.

I’ve always liked the look of a classic moc toe boot, especially when I want something rugged that works with the rest of my wardrobe. Quince’s moc toe boot gives me that heritage workwear vibe without the Red Wing Boot price tag. I’ve worn mine with a sweater and cream-colored trousers for dinner. This contrast between the sturdy boot and lighter pants keeps the outfit from feeling too heavy. The top-grain cowhide leather delivers that traditional, broken-in look over time, and the Goodyear welt construction provides long-term durability, so I won’t have to buy another pair next year.

Underfoot, the non-marking rubber wedge outsole provides traction, and the removable OrthoLite insole adds cushioning and arch support for days when I’m walking around downtown New York City. It has the kind of sturdy feel I want from a moc toe, but the styling is versatile enough for casual office days or semi-dressy occasions.
