#2749. Des Linden’s Simple (and Somewhat Shocking) 100-Mile Fueling Plan

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Boston Marathon champion Des Linden will toe the line at her first 100-miler on October 31, 2026, at the Javelina Jundred near Fountain Hills, Arizona. To prepare for the distance and hot desert conditions, she worked with a team of sports scientists, including Olympic-marathoner-turned-ultrarunner-champion Magda Boulet, to dial in her metabolic needs and a fueling plan. She will primarily rely on liquid calories, with the addition of some more out-of-the-box products like “cooling” menthol gels, while understanding that mid-race adjustments will be key.

For Boston Marathon champion-turned-competitive ultrarunner Des Linden, one question keeps driving her forward: “Can I do this?”

After years of racing some of the world’s most prestigious road marathons, the two-time Olympian is shifting her focus to off-road competitions. On October 31, she’ll attempt her most ambitious athletic endeavor yet when she toes the start line of the Javelina Jundred, a 100-mile trail race through Arizona’s Sonoran Desert that also serves as a Golden Ticket race into the 2027 Western States 100.

“I feel like part of me is just missing being afraid of something; the unknown,” Linden tells Trail Runner. “What is this going to pull out of me? What is this going to ask of me? That’s all really fascinating.”

In addition to the distance—nearly 40 miles more than Linden has ever covered in one go–Javelina presents a slew of unfamiliar challenges. One of the biggest is fueling and hydration over the course of 13-plus hours in desert conditions. Linden got a small taste, literally, at the Black Canyon 50K in February. It was her first ultramarathon and she acknowledged after the race that all of those years of fueling on the roads was only so helpful.

“I went into Black Canyon 50k with a loose fueling plan—to have a Gu at each aid station and sip on an electrolyte beverage between stations,” Linden said. “I wasn’t diligent about it during the race and certainly didn’t account for the time required for trail miles versus road miles. After the race, I realized it was a very easy place to make improvements, and getting it dialed before MDS would be important.”

It’s a relatable learning curve that Linden has taken seriously in the eight months since. Together with a team of sports scientists, including Olympic-marathoner-turned ultrarunning-champion Magda Boulet, Linden has tested her metabolic needs under hot ultramarathon conditions to develop a (somewhat surprising) fueling plan for October 31.

Linden retired from professional road running in 2025 after an illustrious career that included two Olympic Games, two runner-up finishes at both the Chicago and Boston Marathons, and an iconic win at the 2018 Boston Marathon—becoming the first American woman to do so in 33 years. Rather than hanging up her running shoes, Linden is targeting some of the world’s most prestigious trail races.

“I think a lot of this part of my career is getting away from comparing myself to my former self. So this is uncharted territory for me,” Linden says.

This past April, Linden, 43, competed in only her second ultra-trail event, finishing third at Marathon des Sables (MDS), a six-day stage race in the southern Moroccan Sahara often referred to as the “Toughest Footrace on Earth.” While Linden has spent her career refining how she fuels for peak performance and recovery, her MDS experience reinforced just how critical nutrition is to sustaining her endurance and rebounding after hours on her feet. She adds that ultrarunning has pushed her to reevaluate her approach to nutrition as she continues to take on longer and more demanding challenges.

“The distance itself makes nutrition so much more important,” Linden says. “You can’t cover that distance if you’re skimping or shortcutting getting the calories in throughout the day.”

The Javelina Jundred is an open and exposed course. The race is defined by heat: the average high is around 82 degrees Fahrenheit and the average low is roughly 58 degrees. Managing more time on her feet also creates more opportunities for things to go awry. Research has long suggested that heat stress can reduce appetite: A 2018 study in Nature found that exercise suppresses appetite by heating up a region of the brain responsible for regulating eating. Of course, this appetite suppression is counterproductive since you are expending lots of extra energy while running, and create a negative feedback loop that self-perpetuates.

Along with dehydration, racing in the heat creates a unique set of challenges. Linden has been working hard to prepare for them.

To prepare for the challenge, Linden enlisted experts from Gu Energy Labs, based in Berkeley, California, including Roxanne Vogel, Ph.D., nutrition scientist, exercise physiologist, and director of research and education, and Magda Boulet, Gu Energy Labs president and one of the world’s most accomplished ultrarunners. Boulet’s own evolution from Olympic marathoner to Western States 100, Leadville 100, and Marathon des Sables champion has made her a valuable resource for Linden’s own ultramarathon journey.

“I imagine most ultrarunners will look and be like ‘you’re not ready at all.’ But they don’t necessarily know that behind the scenes I have the ultrarunner,” Linden says.

Ahead of MDS this spring, Linden underwent laboratory testing at Gu’s Performance Lab with the goal of understanding her energy expenditure, hydration needs, and fueling tolerance during ultramarathon efforts. As part of the assessment, Vogel set out to measure Linden’s sweat rate and sodium losses under heat stress, her caloric demands at race pace, and how her body uses carbohydrate versus fat in simulated desert conditions.

The data helped Vogel develop an individualized metabolic and hydration profile for Linden. Vogel discovered that Linden isn’t a very salty sweater, and she also learned that Linden needs approximately 10 grams of carbohydrate per kilogram of body weight per day. (For a 120-pound person, that’s roughly 550 grams of carbs.)

Going into MDS, the team developed a precise nutrition strategy for Linden, which included Gu Roctane Drink Mix along with gels and chews for her on-course fueling. After each stage, Linden leaned on nut butter and Roctane Protein Recovery Drink Mix to supply the necessary carbohydrates, electrolytes, and 20 grams of whey protein. Her dinner included a savory dehydrated potato mash with added protein and sodium. Linden also integrated Gu Roctane BCAA capsules before and after the stages to support muscle repair and reduce soreness.

Vogel says those core principles will be part of Linden’s approach at Javelina, but the demands of a single day 100-mile race in the Arizona desert will require some adjustments. For Javelina, liquid calories will be the backbone of her nutrition strategy. The target is to consume approximately 16 ounces (500 milliliters) of Roctane Drink Mix per hour. Per 16-ounce serving, it offers 250 calories, 60 grams of carbs, and 320 milligrams of sodium. Linden also plans to integrate some products that remain under development, Vogel says, including Roctane MC-318, a patented menthol-based energy gel designed to help athletes feel perceptually cooler. The product is not yet commercially available, but has already been tested by athletes at races such as Badwater 135, Western States 100, and Marathon des Sables.

The Javelina course consists of five loops along rolling desert singletrack. It’s a format that allows for both Linden and her crew to accurately anticipate her arrival at aid stations, where she’ll be able to replenish supplies and, crucially, will allow for crew to frequently assess how she’s managing the nutrition and hydration plan.

The terrain will help determine when Linden takes in different forms of fuel. Uphill sections, when she’s moving more slowly, provide an opportunity to take gels or chews. On downhills, when she wants to let her legs unwind and rip down as fast as possible, she’ll focus more on liquids. Her approach also reflects a shift from road marathon racing, where fueling opportunities are often more structured and brief.

“In the marathon, [every] 5K you grab the bottle, you want eight to 10 ounces and you want to get rid of the bottle as quickly as possible. We have the luxury of carrying it and sipping on it, and also strategizing,” Linden says.

Temperature will be one of the most significant variables. Research has found that during prolonged exercise in the heat, gastrointestinal symptoms are greater.

“People think it has to be really hot for them to need to make changes to their nutrition or their electrolyte plan,” Vogel says. “The cutoff is somewhere around 75 degrees Fahrenheit. Above that, you’re certainly going to be at an increased level of needs in terms of your hydration and electrolytes, plus also potentially your carbohydrate needs. Even in somewhat moderately warm temperatures, athletes can be needing more than what they would normally take in.”

As the temperature rises throughout the day over the course of the race, Linden anticipates leaning more heavily on liquids. The drink mix can provide fluid and carbohydrates while helping her meet her electrolyte needs, and gels with water will fill any gaps.

“She’s never done a [single day] race this long. The longer you’re out there, the more likely your gut is going to have some issues,” Vogel says. “When you’re exercising, blood flow is going to your working muscles. Less of it is going to your digestive track. So that’s one thing that’s putting stress on the system that makes it harder to digest and absorb [nutrition]. Hence the whole reason for things like gels. They’re easy to digest.”

During the hottest part of the day, Vogel says the team may decrease Linden’s carbohydrate intake to account for changes in digestive tolerance. But as temperatures fall into the night, they’ll look to increase her carbs. Vogel says caffeine will require similar consideration.

“During very hot weather events, caffeine can actually be triggering to the gut in a bad way,” Vogel says. “Rather than following a fixed schedule regardless of conditions, we might actually pull back on caffeine during the heat of the day and start to strategically add it back in as it cools off later in the day.”

Another factor to consider: flavor fatigue, when sensory receptors on your tongue that detect flavor become less responsive with repeated consumption.

“Flavor fatigue is something we know and understand. In hot environments, you don’t generally want to have things that are what we call indulgent—chocolates and caramels and vanillas that are going to stay heavy on the palate. We want more things like citrus and light fruity or even sour, tart flavors that are going to clear the palate or stimulate some thirst,” Vogel says.

Ultimately, the goal is to make the strategy simple to execute, Vogel says. Linden’s success will depend not only on having a precise nutrition plan, but on adjusting on the fly when fatigue and discomfort begin to accumulate.

“I feel like it’s a race within itself. Did I try to execute as well as possible? Did I follow the plan? Did I make good decisions?” Linden says. “I want to finish, and I think there’s so many things that can go in so many different directions in that amount of time and that distance. So if I fueled enough to get myself through 100 miles, that’s a victory.”

#3580. How Long Should Your Runs Be During Marathon Race Week?

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Taper nerves are real, but one last workout can do more harm than good. Here’s the right range to stick to for the week.

After months of early alarms, long runs, and workouts, logging fewer and fewer miles from the start of your taper through race week can feel uncomfortable. Short runs may leave you wondering whether you should add just a few more miles, pick up the pace, or squeeze in one last confidence-building workout before you hit the starting line.

But the final week is not about chasing fitness. At this point, training has little effect on your progress; the remaining runs primarily serve as maintenance. You want to feel fresh and ready to race—not like you just put in a tough workout. That restraint will pay off on race day, when rested legs let you tap into the fitness you’ve already built.

So what should you cap your runs at during race week? Here’s what to know to protect your fitness and show off your performance gains when you get to the starting line.

Runner’s World previously reported that, beginning Monday of marathon week, runners should keep runs to about four miles or less. Yes, just four miles.

That comes with a few exceptions. Many Runner’s World marathon training plans recommend one six- to seven-mile run about four days before race day, often involving some intervals to keep you sharp.

The point of keeping runs so short? The months before a marathon are when you build endurance. The final days before your race are when your body gets the chance to fully absorb that work.

Research backs this up, demonstrating an association between progressively reduced training in the final weeks before a marathon and better race performances.

For those thinking about pushing it, know that a tough workout during race week is unlikely to make you fitter for your race, and on top of that, it can actually hurt the performance you’ve spent months preparing to achieve.

This is why the four- to seven-mile cap on race-week runs may prove especially useful for those who feel unsettled by the light taper schedule, especially in the final days before their race. It’s a boundary that gives you enough time on your feet to stay connected to your normal running routine, while helping keep your restless pre-race brain from turning an easy run into an unnecessary training session.

Nearly all race-week running should be easy—about 90 seconds to two minutes per mile slower than goal marathon pace, Runner’s World previously reported. The few stretches that aren’t easy come in the form of familiar race-pace pickups, strides, or short intervals in an effort to keep you loose, not wear you out.

We’ve all heard of the adage, “Nothing new on race day,” but in this case it applies to race week as well. Josh Wessler, an ACSM-certified personal trainer and RRCA-certified run coach, previously cautioned runners against ditching their training plan entirely during race week, meaning you should continue practicing consistency without introducing new stressors. Keep things short, easy, and predictable.

Taper nerves can make the urge to push harder feel especially convincing; Wessler says it’s common for runners to question their fitness and readiness during race week. But a hard effort is unlikely to make you fitter now.

Race week is about “preserving energy and sharpening, not building fitness,” Maya Ellison, CPT, RRCA-certified run coach and owner of P.A.C.E.D. Coaching Services, previously told Runner’s World.

Runners should also avoid trying new shoes, paces, gels, or fuel alternatives in race week, Namrita Brooke, PhD, RDN, endurance coach and sports dietitian, previously told Runner’s World. Those variables should have been tested weeks earlier, with time to make adjustments.

The bottom line: Let your final runs before 26.2 be boring. Wear the gear you know works. Run at an effort you recognize. You’ve already built your marathon fitness; keeping race-week runs capped at about an hour helps ensure you get to that hard-earned fitness when it counts.

Matt Rudisill is an Associate Service Editor who has been with Runner's World since 2025. A Nittany Lion through-and-through, Matt graduated from Penn State in 2022 with a degree in journalism and worked in communications for the university's athletic department for three years as the main contact and photographer for its nationally-ranked cross country and track & field teams. Matt was also heavily involved in communications efforts for Penn State football, men's basketball, and women's gymnastics. In his role with Runner's World, Matt has interviewed Olympians, world champions, and countless experts in the field to create service content that helps runners of all ages and experience levels train smarter and race faster. When he’s not out jogging, Matt can be found tweeting bad takes about the Phillies or watching movies.

#1420. Inside Dodger Stadium’s Fascinating History—Including 5 Facts You Probably Didn’t Know

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The Brooklyn Dodgers once played in a small ballpark in the borough’s Flatbush neighborhood. So how did the team end up in the Dodger Stadium of today—a midcentury-modern structure with sweeping views of Los Angeles?

“The city of Los Angeles was the ultimate Plan B for the Brooklyn Dodgers,” Mark Langill, the team’s historian, says in a new installment of AD’s Inside History video series. However, when team president Walter O’Malley saw the freeway access leading to the canyon of Chavez Ravine, just north of downtown LA, the sports exec thought it’d be “the perfect place” for a stadium that would be in a league of its own.

Dodger Stadium, which opened in 1962 and seats a whopping 56,000 fans, has now hosted games in 12 World Series, including in 2024 and 2025 when the Los Angeles Dodgers won the championship. Though the team hoped for a historic three-peat this year—a third consecutive World Series win would have marked the first time a team achieved this since the New York Yankees came out victorious in 2000—they were eliminated by the Milwaukee Brewers in the playoffs.

Ahead of this year’s World Series, AD visits the third-oldest Major League Baseball stadium—behind Fenway Park (home to the Boston Red Sox) and Wrigley Field (home of the Chicago Cubs)—to learn more about its history. Below are a few highlights.

Dodger Stadium is known for its colorful seating, and architect Emil Praeger had a specific reason for arranging the seats in a gradient from yellow at field level to orange, turquoise, and sky blue at the top. “The choice of the colors was selected to mimic the sunset,” says Janet Marie Smith, executive vice president of planning and development for the LA Dodgers.

Every entrance of Dodger Stadium “is marked by something different,” says Smith, adding that landmarks like oversized Dodgers bobbleheads can be especially helpful when getting back to your car after a game. And of course, the stadium wouldn’t be complete without paying tribute to Jackie Robinson, who broke Major League Baseball’s color line in 1947. Alongside a bronze statue of the baseball great are some of his profound quotes chosen in consultation with his widow, Rachel Robinson, and his daughter, Sharon. They are displayed out front because his “power and impact on America wasn’t just on the playing field,” says Smith.

Players heading straight to Dodger Stadium from LAX may think they ended up exactly where they started. A hallway that serves as the entrance to the clubhouse is inspired by the tile mosaics at the airport that have appeared in movies like 1967’s Point Blank and 1997’s Jackie Brown. “We wanted to give honor to Janet Bennett, who designed the mosaics at LAX,” says Smith.

Because Dodger Stadium was in need of advertisement space, hexagonal signs bordering the field were created to “mimic the scoreboard,” says Smith. The six-sided polygon is found in various places at the venue—though some didn’t take shape until 2013, when additions to the venue were crafted from old sketches laid out in 1959. “We’re really pleased to have finally finished things that were authored half a century ago,” says Smith.

The outside of Dodger Stadium is just as important as the inside. In 1963, O’Malley invested in landscaping that would grow into lush botanical gardens just outside of its gates. “We’re so proud of having the only botanist on staff of any Major League team,” says Smith, noting that the green grounds have become one of the stadium’s “defining” features. Outside are more than 100 concrete planters shaped like oversized martini glasses, or champagne bowls, that the Dodger landscaping crew “lovingly restored” over the years.

#3326. How Kristoffer Polaha Gained and Lost 25 Pounds for ‘American Hostage’

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

Kristoffer “Kris” Polaha took on a serious challenge when he agreed to add around 25 pounds of fat to his usually fit frame for his role in American Hostage, only to drop it soon after in order to continue with other filming commitments. And while the chance to say “yes” to every indulgent meal gave the star a sense of freedom, he tells M&F that the side effects had him ready to return to his normal state.

Of course, Polaha’s mission to pile on the pounds for American Hostage wasn’t a personal cry for help. The actor had permission to stack up his plates in order to play Dick Hall in the true story of the mortgage broker who was abducted for 63 hours by a disgruntled client in 1977. The crisis soon became a media event as his captor, Tony Kiritsis, courted the press and called into his local radio station, all while holding Hall at gun point.

“Dick Hall was a guy who’d reached a point in his life where he’d given up,” explains Polaha. “He stopped caring about what he looked like, and he stopped caring about being in shape and fit.” To prep, the actor had around 12 weeks to add the weight. “I just kind of ate whatever I wanted, so it really consisted of a diet of burgers and all of the foods that you want to eat,” he says. “I ended up getting up to about, 211 pounds. That was the heaviest I’ve ever been, so that was a big, big deal.”

Polaha explains that he generally likes to fast in the mornings but starting in October of 2025, breakfast was back in. “I’d have a big breakfast!” he recalls, noting that even as the American Hostage production got underway, the script made it easy to stay heavy. “The role of Dick Hall required me to be shackled to a chair,” said the star of those long, sedentary filming days. “And so, I would get to work, and I would just sit in that chair for hours on end. It was scarily easy to pack on the pounds.”

At the height of his indulgences, Polaha says that he would end his long shooting days by frequenting an Earl’s diner in Winnipeg, sinking a drop of the black stuff with his evening meal. “I was drinking Guinness with my dinner,” he shares. “A hamburger and a Guinness at, like, 11.30 p.m. at night.”

Ice cream would also become a regular supper. But while Polaha made the most of his calories, he tells M&F that there were also some side effects to ponder. The star says that there was often a depressing reflection looking back in the mirror, as the combination of his makeup and excess weight provided an unwelcome glimpse of what his future could look like, if he didn’t get back on the fitness wagon. Then there was the general uncomfortableness of his fatter physique.

“I was less flexible,” he says. “Every time I bent down, my stomach got in my way, and I just wasn’t used to it. And it happened quickly. So, suddenly, I was walking around in a different skin. I’ve always been a really lean guy, and so it was a big shift for me. At home, my wife was complaining that I was snoring. So, think all this was mostly a wake-up call.”

With American Hostage now available to binge watch, Polaha is well past his binge eating stage. While critics have praised Polaha’s commitment and portrayal of Hall, the star then had to set about shedding those excess  pounds, ready  for his next projects.

In February, Polaha began to point the needle on the scales back in the opposite direction, working diligently on his diet. “I think it really raises the question of, ‘how do you want to move through the world,” says the actor of his motivation. “Do you want to be agile, flexible, strong, lean?”

Polaha found that losing weight is more challenging than gaining it, however. “I think it’s a lot easier to put on than it is to come off, but you do have to give yourself patience,” says the actor, who went back to his routine of fasting in the mornings and restarting his daily exercise regimen. Polaha is also a fan of hot yoga for both the mental aspect, and the physical process of flushing out toxins. And he likes to get his steps in by running or jogging.

The actor notes that nighttime snacking urges still come to him, but he makes smart swaps, replacing ice cream with something that he can chew for longer, while still cutting a few calories to satisfy his sweet fix, sharing that he likes to take his time with a handful of Good & Plenty’s. But for those thinking that staying lean means abstaining from your favorite foods, Polaha dispels that myth. “I don’t have a crazy health regimen,” he explains. “I still eat what I want, it’s just a question of how much.”

Now back at this goal weight, Kris Polaha will need to stay fit as he has several new projects coming out, including the Buried in Barstow movies on Lifetime, and a new Hallmark Christmas movie called Mistletoe and Mimosas.

American Hostage is now streaming on Prime Video.

#1514. ‘I’m 79 and Lift Heavy 5 Days a Week. Here’s What It Has Taught Me About Healthy Aging.’

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

“I don’t see getting older as a reason to stop pushing myself. If anything, every new accomplishment feels more meaningful because I’m doing it at this stage of my life.”

I’ve always made it a priority to be healthy, active, and strong. But for a long time, exercise used to be more of a casual activity for me. I never could’ve predicted that I’d become my strongest, most active self in my 70s.

Growing up, my mom taught me early on that exercise and eating well were important parts of taking care of myself. When I was a kid, I played tennis and racquetball and loved to run. Then, as an adult, I became a regular at the gym and loved trying different workouts, from Jazzercise and step aerobics to at-home strength-training videos. I enjoyed the way exercise made me feel, and continued all of these workouts off and on throughout the decades.

Once I hit my 70s, though, I started thinking more about how to age well—and the role that exercise could play in that. I did some research and learned that in order to stay strong, capable, and independent as I got older, I needed to build strength and muscle. So, I decided it was time to shake up my routine and get into consistent strength training.

A Burn Boot Camp studio had just opened near me, offering high-intensity group strength and conditioning workouts. I liked the idea of combining heavy lifting with the motivation of a group class, so I decided to give it a try. The first class was hard because I was lifting heavier weights than I was used to, but I loved the energy of group fitness and having a coach. For the first time ever, I got real guidance on my form, learned modifications when an exercise didn’t feel quite right, and challenged myself in a safe way. I started going to the class five days a week to build a new routine that I genuinely looked forward to.

After making these changes to my workout routine and diet, I started feeling good inside and out. I was growing more visible muscle in my arms and legs and felt stronger and more stable in my everyday activities. I also had more energy during the day and was more confident in what my body could do. Seeing (and feeling) these changes in my 70s showed me that I could still get stronger, challenge myself in new ways, and make meaningful progress at this stage of life.

I still go to the Burn Boot Camp classes, typically doing three strength-centered classes and one to two cardio-centered classes each week. During workouts, we hit five to seven workout stations and complete each exercise for a certain number of reps and sets, or time.

In strength classes, I use dumbbells at each station to work different upper- and lower-body muscle groups throughout the week. During upper-body workouts, some of the moves I do include overhead presses, pull-ups, pushups, triceps extensions, chest flys, and dumbbell rows. On lower-body days, I do deadlifts, Romanian deadlifts, lunges, and different squat variations. Then, cardio days are more focused on bodyweight conditioning, with exercises like jumping jacks, high knees, burpees, hurdles, and explosive running drills. Some workouts also mix in strength-based movements, such as medicine ball throws or squats with an overhead press.

Throughout all of it, I prioritize proper form to help prevent injury and build strength safely. I also progressively increase the weight I use every few weeks so I can continue growing muscle. It’s incredible to pump out reps with weights that used to feel too heavy: Recently, I went from 10- to 12-lb weights for overhead presses, increased my chest fly from 20 to 25 lbs, and hit a new PR with a 70-lb Romanian deadlift.

On top of that, I recently started doing reformer Pilates once or twice a week on the days I’m not lifting to help improve my flexibility and mobility. The lower-intensity workout is a nice complement to traditional strength training, and I’ve already noticed that my balance, stability, and posture have improved. Additionally, I aim for 5,000 steps a day and do hip and shoulder stretches as well as mobility exercises most days for joint health. These small, consistent habits help me stay mobile, support my strength training, and make it easier to keep living life independently.

Ever since I started eating more protein, I’ve loved drinking shakes—and this one tastes like a milkshake.

Patagonia is one of my favorite brands because its products are high quality, and I love this sports bra.

A few months ago, I started taking 10 grams of creatine every day because research shows it may benefit muscle and brain health.

As I’ve gotten older, I’ve realized that a casual workout here or there isn’t enough to help me age as well as I want to. My ultimate goal is to build long-term strength, muscle, and mobility so I can move and travel independently for years to come. That’s what inspired me to start lifting weights consistently, and that’s why I keep challenging my body through progressive overload, gradually increasing the weight when I’m ready.

Seven years after changing up my workout routine, I’ve become so much stronger—everyday activities like carrying groceries, getting in and out of the car, and keeping up with a busy day feel easier. That progress keeps me motivated. At 79, I’m still showing up, increasing my weights, and proving to myself that getting older doesn’t mean you can’t be the strongest version of yourself.

One of the parts I love most about group fitness is the community. Over the years, I’ve made friends with people of all ages and fitness levels, and we’ve become a real source of encouragement for one another. We celebrate milestones, push each other through challenging workouts, and help keep the whole group accountable.

Some of these friendships have grown beyond the gym, too, which has made the experience even more meaningful. For me, fitness isn’t just about getting stronger physically—it’s also about surrounding myself with people who inspire me to keep showing up, challenging myself, and enjoying the process.

As I approach 80, I’m proud of how much I can still challenge myself, and I’m curious to see what else I can accomplish. I don’t want to put an arbitrary ceiling on what my body can do simply because of my age. Instead, I look for new ways to test myself, whether that’s picking up a heavier set of dumbbells, trying reformer Pilates for the first time, or hitting a new PR in the gym. Each challenge gives me something to work toward and reminds me there’s always room to grow.

Of course, I recognize that my body has changed over the years, and there are certain parts of working out that I approach differently now. For example, if my coach programs running during a cardio class, I’ll walk instead because the impact doesn’t feel great on my knees. I’ve learned that modifying an exercise isn’t a failure or setback; it’s a way to keep moving safely and consistently. I listen to my body and adjust when I need to, because I don’t see getting older as a reason to stop pushing myself. If anything, every new accomplishment feels more meaningful because I’m doing it at this stage of my life.

I’m not focused on what I could do at 30 or 50—I’m focused on what I can do now, how much stronger I can become, and what I might be capable of achieving next.

Andi Breitowich is a freelance writer who covers health, fitness, relationships, beauty, and smart living. She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. #2659. Parents Of An Only Child Deserve To Feel Stressed Too

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Parental. Stress. Is. Valid. For. Everyone.

“Do you have any more kids or just the one?” It’s a question I’ve been asked countless times by fellow parents at various birthday parties or school pickups while we stand around watching our kiddos play. And my answer is always the same: “No, it’s just my son. He’s an only child.” The question has always seemed innocent enough and without any detectable judgment. But there’s just something about that phrase “just the one” that’s always rubbed me the wrong way. As if one, by itself, isn’t enough or less than what it should be.

As an only child myself, I never thought of it as anything to be ashamed of. But now that I’m a mom, I'm much more aware of the stigma surrounding the “one-and-done” mentality. No one is ever directly rude or insensitive, but subtle remarks like “just the one” or assurances that “there’s still time” to have another suggest my life — and my child’s life — are lacking in some way. It’s as if to say our family isn’t whole just the way we are.

It can feel a little isolating at times. In fact, there have been moments when I’ve hesitated to open up to other moms about my own parenting struggles or concerns because I only have one kid, whereas many of them have multiple. I’ve found myself wondering, How can my problems compare when I’m dealing with one kiddo and they’re juggling two or three or even four? It can make me feel like I don’t have a right to complain or vent about my child’s latest tantrum because they are dealing with those things on a larger scale than I am.

It’s worth noting that no parent has ever said any of this to me directly. But it’s an underlying worry that always creeps into the back of my mind. In many ways, society (and social media, a viral extension of society) has conditioned me to think that way. If you don’t have any kids, you’re made to think your life is incomplete. If you have one kid, you’re encouraged to give them a sibling or they’ll be lonely. It feels like it’s never enough. But I’m done allowing myself to feel that way. It ends now.

These days, I keep reminding myself that I have just as much of a right to vent as parents of multiple kids. It's not a competition, and parental stress is valid regardless of how many kids you have. Full stop.

That’s not to say that I don’t appreciate the added struggles and responsibilities that come with having more than one child. Taking family trips, juggling sports schedules and school drop-offs, not to mention dealing with a revolving door of colds and other ailments, are challenging enough with one kid, let alone multiples. But you never know what someone might be going through that could make caring for one kid much harder than it looks.

One glaring example that comes to mind is the whole concept of having a “village” to lean on in times of need. Many parents with multiple kids might have grandparents or extended family nearby who can help at a moment’s notice, while some parents with one child might not have any loved ones nearby. Support (or lack thereof) can make a huge difference when it comes to caring for little ones. And, of course, you have no idea what each parent is going through emotionally, physically, or even financially, which can all contribute to a rise in stress levels, regardless of how many children you have.

But honestly, even if you do have a ton of family close by who are ready and willing to help, that shouldn’t automatically mean you need to have more kids. Parents of only children shouldn’t need to explain themselves to anyone or be made to feel like they are doing something wrong. Every family, both big and small, deserves to take up space in the world.

Being a parent can be the most rewarding thing in the world. But it’s also a full-time job with zero breaks and very little appreciation given in return. Every mom and dad is a superhero in their own way and deserves the right to complain or feel all the feelings. I don’t want to take any credit away from parents with multiple children. I simply want to feel like my own struggles and grievances deserve a seat at the table too.

Kelly Schremph is an entertainment and lifestyle writer with bylines at Us Weekly, Refinery29, Bustle, Romper, Scary Mommy, and more. She lives in California with her husband, their son, Chris, and their yellow lab, Luna, who still acts very much like a puppy. When Kelly isn’t watching TV (or writing about it), she’s usually chasing after one of them.

#2549. Skrmetti, Mahmoud, and B.P.J v. Bostock and Kingdom

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

The Court seems concerned enough about transitioning children but not adult (prisoners).

This year, the Bureau of Prison determined that "sex-trait-modification surgeries, hormone interventions in general, and social accommodations could no longer be justified as treatment for gender dysphoria." The lower court enjoined this policy, and the Solicitor General sought an emergency stay. Today the Supreme Court denied a stay in Trump v. Kingdom by a 7-2 vote. Justices Thomas and Alito noted they would have granted the application, though there was no written opinion.

First, just yesterday Justice Kagan noted that the Trump appointees rule against President Trump as proof that they are not hacks. And she surely knew this case would come down as further proof of this point. Kagan's point seems to be that ruling against the President that appoints you makes you a fair justice. That premise is only true if in fact that law does not support the appointing President's policy. If a Justice simply rules against the President who appoints him for the sake of optics, then the Justice is no longer a judge.

Justice Alito made this point well in his new book, summarized in the Wall Street Journal.

Insecure originalists may worry that they are subconsciously engaging in "judicial activism" in the service of their own policy preferences. As a result, they may welcome occasions when their originalist analysis leads to results they dislike. They experience a brief catharsis, a release of their anxiety about succumbing to temptation.

Twenty-first-century originalists should shed this insecure mindset.

I wonder if Justice Alito thinks that Justice Scalia's constant praise of his vote in Texas v. Johnson was evidence of this "catharsis." Justice Kavanaugh used to praise the Nixon appointees who ruled against Nixon in the Watergate Tapes case. That premise reinforces the wrong image. I've come to believe that United States v. Nixon was a travesty, and the Justices who joined that were caught up in the Watergate swamp fever. The decision has not held up well over the past five decades.

Let's stop praising how often the Trump appointees rule against Trump. The more useful metric, in my view, is when the Court's two most conservative members (Thomas and Alito) are alone in dissent. I updated my wall of receipts in July 2026 as a recent accounting.

Second, how do we reconcile Mahmoud, B.P.J., and Skrmetti on the one hand and Bostock and Kingdom on the other? For sure, all of these cases presented different legal issues. Mahmoud was a Free Exercise challenge, Skrmetti presented an Equal Protection issue, B.P.J. was about Title IX, Bostock was an interpretation of Title VII, and Kingdom was an APA challenge on the interim orders docket. Yet all of these cases were really about transgender rights. Back in April I wrote that the transgender tide was turning. Was I wrong? Why did all of the recent cases turn out one way but Kingdom turns out the other?

Well, the Court is certainly concerned about transitioning children. They upheld bans on gender transition surgeries in Skrmetti. They required opt-outs from transgender-related indoctrination in Maryland public schools. (Had the story books been only about gay marriage, I don't know that the case would have come out the same way.) The Justices allowed the exclusion of biologically male athletes from female sports. I could add to that list Mirabelli v. Bonta, which prohibited schools from secretly transitioning children.

It's possible that Justice Gorsuch simply saw the light after Bostock, but maybe there is another explanation: he cares more about children than he cares about transgender rights. So his equities tilted to the right. But in a case where adults seek this treatment, Gorsuch cut the other way. Moreover, when those consenting adults are prisoners, Justice Gorsuch's equities are far clearer. For a libertarian, how could the carceral state deny this treatment to incarcerated persons who can't go elsewhere to get it?

Third, I'm struggling with Justice Kavanaugh's position here. He had a free vote, as the split was 7-2. He could have at least written a separate opinion explaining why relief was not appropriate here, but when the case returns on the merits, the outcome may be different. He does that sort of thing all the time in far less controversial cases. But here, we only have the sound of silence. I think he is signaling the government loses on the merits here. Maybe he suspects Trump will be out of office by the time this case returns to the Court, and the policy will be abandoned.

Fourth, I'm sure Justice Barrett figured out some procedural reason why the federal government must remove healthy reproductive organs from prisoners. That is, unless she agrees with the Respondent's arguments that BOP's position is "pretextual and [was] reverse-engineered to implement" President Trump's order. So we're back to pretext, and trying to divine the President's real motivations. Trump v. Cook established that Trump does not get the presumption of regularity. Barrett dissented in that case, through she may not disagree with the broader premise.

Fifth, the application was filed on September 28, briefing concluded on October 9, and the order was issued on October 21. What took so long if this was a straight-up denial without any writings? I suspect that Justices Alito and Thomas were hopeful they could scrounge more votes but could not. Maybe they even circulated a dissent but withdrew it. Something behind the scenes happened here. If only we knew what happened? Thankfully reporters at CNN and the New York Times are no longer able to otbain leaks. The best they can do is ask KBJ's friends for comments and interview the Chief's neighbors in Maine.

NEXT: Body Armor Ban Struck Down by N.Y. Federal Court

Josh Blackman is a constitutional law professor at the South Texas College of Law Houston and the President of the Harlan Institute. Follow him @JoshMBlackman.

#1577. <b><strong>You Can Run Your First Marathon After 50—Just Like These Women</strong></b>

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

These are the strategies that got them to 26.2.

For most of her life, Patti Whiteye believed she wasn’t a runner. She thought she was too slow, not athletic enough, or that running just simply wasn’t for her. Then, in 2023, when she was 55, her husband suffered two heart attacks, prompting her to take her health and fitness more seriously. She started walking 10,000 steps a day, and four months later, a casual conversation with her husband about an upcoming marathon turned into an impromptu decision to run it themselves.

With just six months to prepare for the 26.2-mile race, Whiteye had a lot of work ahead of her. On her first day of training, she couldn’t run more than 100 steps. Then she discovered the run/walk method, alternating short running intervals with timed walking breaks, and something clicked. For the first time, she realized that instead of struggling to run continuously, she could build endurance at her own pace. “The first few weeks were really hard, but once I learned to trust that each workout was building toward marathon day, I stopped worrying about the rest and leaned into the process,” she says.

Little by little, mile after mile, the woman who spent decades convinced she wasn’t a runner began to see herself becoming a marathoner. Then, in October 2025 at the Marine Corps Marathon—at 56 years old—she did just that.

Crossing the finish line “changed my life in a way that’s hard to put into words,” says Whiteye. “It was slow, and I did a lot of walking, but throughout the entire experience, I was just so grateful to be able to move my body in this way.” Since then, that feeling of accomplishment has kept her going. Whiteye completed her second marathon last April and is now training for another later this fall.

At 54, Vanessa Lindley is returning to the New York City Marathon after walking her first in 2024 for charity, with no formal training and one simple goal: to cross the finish line. “After I finished, I was on this high and knew I needed to race again, so I entered the lottery [for the 2026 New York City Marathon] and got a spot,” she says.

This time, Lindley is taking a more intentional approach, following a structured training plan she found online that includes three days of shorter run/walk workouts and one day dedicated to a longer session. By gradually incorporating more running into her routine, she hopes to improve her race pace while still embracing the experience.

Still, for these two women, the marathon journey is about more than reaching the finish line; it’s also about discovering what’s possible when they challenge their own expectations.

Mildred Baker, at 80 years old, is preparing to run her very first marathon, using the same run/walk method that helped Whiteye and Lindley find their stride. Baker’s running journey began in 2023, when she joined New York Road Runner Striders, a walking group for older adults, and found a community that encouraged her to push beyond what she thought was possible. Before long, walking turned into run/walk intervals, which led to 5Ks and 10Ks, and eventually, the idea of running the 2026 New York City Marathon.

Baker knew she needed a plan, so she found an online schedule tailored to first-time marathoners her age and began training in May, gradually building her mileage with the run/walk method. Now, with countless miles under her belt and the starting line in sight, she’s confident she can go the distance. “I ran 18 miles the other day and felt great, so I know I can do this,” she says.

That same sense of community is also what drew Karen Guttridge to running. At 50, she had never run a day in her life. But after being diagnosed with low bone density shortly after her 50th birthday, she was encouraged by her doctor to take up running to help improve it. Like many beginners, Guttridge wasn’t an immediate fan, but finding a running buddy who committed to a “couch to 5K” program with her changed the game. “The whole community around running is what kept me going,” she says. “I just kept saying yes to every opportunity and decided that wherever running was meant to take me, I would go.”

Now, 15 years later, at 65, Guttridge is a certified personal trainer and run coach. She has run three marathons and is gearing up for her fourth. “My mantra is ‘let’s see what happens when I try,’” she says. “I thought I was far too late to start running in my 50s, but it was actually the perfect time to try, because there would have been so much of life I missed out on if I hadn’t started.”

Inspired to take on a marathon of your own? With the right preparation and a plan that works for your body, you can build the endurance and confidence to go the distance at any age, with any level of running experience. Here’s how to get started.

If you’re a seasoned marathoner, a 12- to 16-week training cycle may be enough to get you to the starting line. But if you’re tackling your first marathon, it’s better to give yourself at least 18 to 24 weeks for formal training, says Katie Cyrana-Hillery, PT, DPT, CSCS, a physical therapist, RRCA-certified run coach, and strength and conditioning specialist at Evolve Physical Therapy and Performance. “This longer timeline allows your bones, muscles, tendons, and joints to adapt to the increased workload, along with your cardiovascular system,” she says.

But, before you pull up a program, it’s best to begin running more regularly and do what coaches call “building your base.” That ideally consists of three to six months of running a few days per week to improve your cardiorespiratory system and get your joints and bones more familiar with the demands of running.

Patience really is key when it comes to marathon training. “You are asking your body to do something it may never have done before, and adaptation takes time,” says Kathleen Weber, MD, a sports medicine physician at Midwest Orthopaedics at Rush in Chicago. “Women over 50 can absolutely run their first marathon; the key is just to give your body enough time to adapt to the demands of training rather than trying to rush the process.”

As the women featured above can attest, you don’t have to run every mile to become a marathoner. The run/walk method is a practical way to build endurance, especially if you’re new to distance running. Cyrana-Hillery suggests starting with manageable running intervals of 30 seconds to a minute, separated by 30-second-to-two-minute walking breaks.

“The objective can either be to gradually increase your running intervals until you’re able to run continuously, or to train to complete the marathon using a run/walk strategy,” she says. “Both are valid approaches to covering 26.2 miles.” The secret is finding a rhythm you can sustain over the long haul.

As for how to keep all those intervals straight? You can utilize an app on your phone, like the Jeff Galloway Run Walk Run App (Galloway is the creator of the run/walk method) or invest in a running watch, where you can create workouts with different intervals. Both ways will give you a buzz to remind you when it’s time to switch between running and walking.

Strength training is an essential part of marathon preparation, particularly for women over 50. “Running is a repetitive aerobic activity, while strength training helps build the muscular capacity to absorb impact and produce force,” says Dr. Weber. Stronger muscles also support running mechanics, improve stability, and combat fatigue as your weekly mileage increases, she adds.

To set yourself up for success, Dr. Weber recommends two to three strength-training sessions per week, with a special emphasis on building strength in your glutes, quads, hamstrings, calves, and core. If you’re new to resistance training, just be sure to start with lighter weights and focus on good form.

More running doesn’t always mean better results. Rest days give your body time to repair muscle tissue and adapt to the demands of training, ultimately helping you build both strength and endurance. “You absolutely do not need to run every day as a first-time marathoner,” says Cyrana-Hillery. “I recommend starting with three days of running per week to allow enough time for recovery and strength training.”

Just as important as scheduling rest days is recognizing when your body needs extra recovery. Disrupted sleep, lingering soreness, an elevated resting heart rate, or declining running performance can all signal that you’re pushing too hard, says Cyrana-Hillery. Prioritizing gentle mobility exercises, dynamic stretching, adequate sleep, and proper fueling can also help maintain range of motion, support muscle repair, improve running efficiency, and reduce injury risk, adds Dr. Weber. Think of it this way: Your body gets stronger not just from the work you do, but from how well you recover afterward.

“It’s normal for your body to feel challenged as you increase your training load, but persistent or worsening pain is different from normal training fatigue and shouldn’t be ignored,” says Dr. Weber.

If something doesn’t feel right, don’t be afraid to shorten a run, slow your pace, take an extra rest day, or switch to a run/walk approach. If pain affects how you move, check in with a health professional rather than trying to push through. As Dr. Weber puts it, “A training plan is a guide, not a contract. Your job is to adapt the plan to your body, not force your body to conform to the plan.”

Training for a marathon can be hard, intimidating, and nerve-wracking, but it should also be fun, exciting, and rewarding. So instead of only focusing on the end goal, celebrate the small wins along the way, whether that’s running a little farther than you did last week, feeling stronger on your runs, or sticking to your training schedule when things feel good.

“Training for your first marathon is a big accomplishment in itself, so don’t lose sight of the joy and excitement of the experience,” says Dr. Weber. “The ultimate goal is to arrive at the starting line healthy, enjoy the journey, and discover what you’re capable of.”

She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. #3383. 7 Knee-Strengthening Exercises That Prevent Pesky Knee Pain

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Don’t let a twinge slow down your pedal stroke—or keep you out of the saddle.

Because cycling isn’t a high-impact sport, you’d be forgiven for thinking your knees are safe. But Tara Parsons, a USAC-certified cycling coach and Rapha Women’s Ambassador says she hears riders complaining about knee pain all the time. In fact, 23 percent of riders experience knee pain, according to a study of 116 professional cyclists—and you can bet that number is higher among amateurs.

The knee acts as a hinge between the hip and the ankle, so just think about how many times you flex and extend your knees during a ride. “If you’re missing 5 percent of your range or in a position that’s slightly less than ideal, a small issue can turn into a big problem for someone who's averaging 50,000 reps a weekend,” says Dane DeLozier, the biomechanics lab director at and co-founder of Revo Physiotherapy and Sports Performance in Boulder, CO.

“This, in combination with a lifestyle that involves sitting at desks all day, can cause the weakening of muscles of the trunk, such as the core, glutes, and hips,” adds Parsons.

From there, it’s a domino effect: “When those muscles become weak or underused, they can become inactive or underactive. And when that happens, the muscles that you use a lot on the bike, such as the quads, end up overcompensating. This overcompensation causes poor knee tracking and poor alignment throughout the pedal stroke, which can lead to knee pain,” says Parsons. And that’s not even considering the stress you’re putting on your knees if your bike isn’t fit correctly or if you’re positioning in the saddle is out of whack.

Pay attention to pain in the patellar tendon, the tendon right on the front of the knee, and the IT band, which goes from the hip down to the knee, says DeLozier—those are common areas for pain, irritation, and inflammation that can hamper knee movement. “If that irritation persists, it’s probably best to go see a PT or your physician to figure out what the root cause is,” he adds.

Whether you’re currently dealing with pain or not, it’s important to build up strength around the joint. Strength training off the bike is crucial to developing those muscles. Plus, “your weekly strengthening routine should involve not just strengthening and activation, but also foam rolling or other type of soft tissue manipulation and mobilization” to keep your muscles in peak condition, says Parsons.

Not sure where to start? These knee-strengthening exercises will help you pedal on pain-free.

How to do this knee-strengthening exercise: Perform 2 to 3 sets of each exercise two times per week. You will need a resistance band loop, a Bosu trainer, and a medium weight. An exercise mat is optional.

The exercises in this workout are demonstrated below by Noam Tamir, certified strength and conditioning specialist and founder of TS Fitness in New York City, so you can learn the proper form. Follow along in the video above with Brian Levine, USAT Level 1 coach and endurance athlete.

Place a resistance band loop around legs just above the knee. Lie on your left side with knees bent, and ankles, knees, and hips stacked. Rest your head on left hand to avoid straining your neck and rest right hand on mat in front of you. Keeping your heels together, rotate right knee up toward ceiling as much as possible. Return to starting position. Complete 15 reps, then repeat on right side.

Lie faceup on the mat with a resistance band loop around your legs just above the knees and knees bent, heels close to butt, arms at sides. Contract glutes, and press into heels to lift hips up toward ceiling so your body forms a straight line from shoulders to knees as you simultaneously press knees out to keep them in line with hips and maintain tension on the band. Pause. Lower and repeat for 10 to 15 reps.

Start standing on a Bosu trainer with hands on hips for balance. Take a big step back with left foot and lower into a lunge with right leg forming a 90-degree angle. Press through right heel to return left leg to start. Complete 10 reps, then repeat on other leg.

Place a band around ankles and stand with feet hip-width apart so band is taut. Lower into a micro squat, then step out to the left. Bring right foot in so feet are hip-width apart again; continue walking, taking 15 steps to the left before reversing to take 15 steps to the right, keeping the band taut the entire time.

Start standing and holding a kettlebell or medium weight in right hand. Shift weight onto left leg and micro bend left knee. Hinge at the hips as you lower weight to floor and extend right leg back behind you for balance. Continue lowering the weight until you are about parallel to the floor while keeping back straight. Return to the starting position. Repeat for 10 to 15 reps then switch sides.

Start on all fours with wrists under shoulders, knees under hips, toes tucked, and back flat. While keeping knee bent, lift right heel up as if to “stamp” your footprint on the ceiling. Return to starting position. Complete 15 reps then repeat on left side.

Start on all fours with wrists under shoulders, knees under hips, toes tucked, and back flat. Extend right arm and left leg straight out until they’re parallel to the floor. Maintain a flat back, level hips, and focus on pulling your belly button toward your spine. Return to all fours, then raise left arm and right leg. Continue alternating for 90 seconds.

#3661. What’s a Good 5K Time?

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Mark Zuckerberg didn’t seem too happy with a recent 20:35. But how does that stack up with the typical 5K time?

In 2025, Meta boss Mark Zuckerberg ran the Stanford Medicine My Heart Counts 5K for the third year in a row, coming in at his second fastest time, 20:35. That translates to a 6:38 per mile. Pretty impressive for the person who created Facebook, right?So, why did he include the song, “Pretty Slowly” by Benson Boone, in his postrace social post? Sure, it wasn’t a personal best, but not all races will be, and the data shows he’s well above average when it comes to his race finish times.

According to a Running USA report based on race data collected from 2013 to 2023, the typical 5K finish time for men is 32 minutes and, for women, it’s 39 minutes. That puts Zuck a whole 10 minutes ahead of the mean.

Based on the various tracking platforms, these averages can go up a bit or down a bit (and you can find more data-backed information in this breakdown of average 5K finish times, along with ways to create and hit your own goals).

“Pretty slowly?” We beg to differ. The beauty of the 5K is that just about anyone can do them, from beginner runners to veterans. Whatever your running experience, committed training is essential for any runner who wants to reach a goal.

Here, we have two workouts that will help you reach any 5K goal, whether you strive to master the distance for the first time or want to aim for a sub-20. You can also find a training plan for any goal with our new How to Master the 5K program.

These two workouts, expert-approved by Austin-based run coach Jess Movold, both reference three training zones. Generally speaking, the yellow zone is an easy effort, the orange zone is a comfortably hard effort, and the red zone is flat-out hard. Knowing—and training—each zone is crucial to effective 5K preparation. Inspired by the following workouts? We have more fun, effective training runs to help you improve your paces.

Why it works: This 37-minute workout should be your first step. Do it once a week for at least three weeks before adding a tempo run for endurance. Don’t focus on pace, just pay attention to your effort during each interval. After the workout, check your fitness tracker or watch. Over three weeks, your pace should go up as your effort level goes down. In other words, running faster should feel easier.

Why it works: The interval workout above builds speed, while this four- to five-mile workout improves stamina and efficiency. Run at your “red line,” just below the point at which you feel like the workout is so hard that you have to stop. Do this workout once a week, at least two days away from an interval run. Take note of your mile time each week to gauge your progress.
